\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{i}}-\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{0}}.

If
\infty^{j}\neq\infty
and
i\neq j
, then by (
3.33
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\infty^{0,\varepsilon}}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon+\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{i}}-\int_{\varepsilon+\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{0}}

\displaystyle=\log(\infty^{j}-\infty^{i})-\log(\infty^{0}+\varepsilon-\infty^{i})+\log(\varepsilon)-\log(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})

\displaystyle=\log\Big(\frac{\infty^{j}-\infty^{i}}{\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}}\Big)-\log(\infty^{0}+\varepsilon-\infty^{i})+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big)}{n_{0}+1}+\log\big(z_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big).

Omitting the term
\log\big(z_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big)
which diverges and letting
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
, we arrive at

p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=\log\bigg(\frac{\infty^{j}-\infty^{i}}{(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})(\infty^{0}-\infty^{i})}\bigg)+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0})\big)}{n_{0}+1}.

If
\infty^{j}=\infty
, then by applying the above case to a point
R
belonging to a small neighborhood of
\infty
, we find

p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{R}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=\log\bigg(\frac{z_{R}-\infty^{i}}{(z_{R}-\infty^{0})(\infty^{0}-\infty^{i})}\bigg)+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0})\big)}{n_{0}+1}.

Moreover

\displaystyle\int_{R}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/{z_{R}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau(1-\tau\infty^{i})}-\frac{1}{\tau(1-\tau\infty^{0})}\bigg)d\tau=\log\Big(\frac{z_{R}-\infty^{0}}{z_{R}-\infty^{i}}\Big).

Therefore

p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=-\log\big(\infty^{0}-\infty^{i})+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0})\big)}{n_{0}+1}.

We now treat the case
i=j
. Using twice (
3.33
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\infty^{0,\varepsilon}}^{\infty^{i,\epsilon}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon+\infty^{0}}^{\epsilon+\infty^{i}}\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{i}}-\int_{\varepsilon+\infty^{0}}^{\epsilon+\infty^{i}}\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{0}}

\displaystyle=\log(\epsilon)-\log(\infty^{0}+\varepsilon-\infty^{i})+\log(\varepsilon)-\log(\epsilon+\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\big(\mu_{i}(\infty^{i,\epsilon})\big)}{n_{i}+1}+\log\big(z_{i}(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})\big)-\log(\infty^{0}+\varepsilon-\infty^{i})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big)}{n_{0}+1}+\log\big(z_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon}))-\log(\epsilon+\infty^{i}-\infty^{0}).

Accordingly, we arrive at

p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=\frac{\log\big(\mu_{i}(\infty^{i})\big)}{n_{i}+1}+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0})\big)}{n_{0}+1}-2\log(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})-\log(-1).

Second case:
\infty^{i}=\infty
. In this case, we have
\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=-\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{0}}
.

If
\infty^{j}\in\mathbb{C}
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\infty^{0,\varepsilon}}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\varepsilon+\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{0}}=\log(\varepsilon)-\log(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big)}{n_{0}+1}+\log\big(z_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big)-\log(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})

and thus

p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=\frac{\log(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0})\big)}{n_{0}+1}-\log(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}).

If
\infty^{j}=\infty=\infty^{i}
, then we have

\displaystyle\int_{\infty^{0,\varepsilon}}^{\infty^{i,\epsilon}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\varepsilon+\infty^{0}}^{1/\epsilon}\frac{dz_{P}}{z_{P}-\infty^{0}}=\log(\epsilon)-\log(1-\epsilon\infty^{0})+\log(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{\log\big(\mu_{i}(\infty^{i,\epsilon})\big)}{n_{i}+1}+\log\big(z_{i}(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})\big)-\log(1-\epsilon\infty^{0})+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big)}{n_{0}+1}+\log\big(z_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big).

Therefore

p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)=\frac{\log\big(\mu_{i}(\infty^{i})\big)}{n_{i}+1}+\frac{\log\big(\mu_{0}(\infty^{0})\big)}{n_{0}+1}.

ii)
Let
i=0,\dots,m
and
\infty^{i,\varepsilon}
be a point belonging to a small neighborhood of
\infty^{i}
such that
z_{i}(\infty^{i,\varepsilon}):=\lambda(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})^{-\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}}=\varepsilon
. Here, as usual,
z_{i}(P)
denotes the standard local parameter near the point
\infty^{i}
. We first claim the following asymptotic behavior:

\begin{split}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)&=\varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}^{\prime}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\\
&\quad+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\omega_{l}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\partial_{z_{l}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)+o(\varepsilon^{2}),\end{split}

(3.34)

where
\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})
and
\omega_{k}^{\prime}(\infty^{i})
are the evaluations defined by (
2.7
).

In particular, the following holds:

\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)\Big)=\log(\varepsilon)+\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)+O(\varepsilon)\right).

(3.35)

Indeed, for all
k=1,\dots,g
, we observe that

\displaystyle\chi_{k}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\infty^{i}}^{\infty^{i,\varepsilon}}\omega_{k}(P)=\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\Big(\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})+\omega_{k}^{\prime}(\infty^{i})z_{i}(P)+\dots\Big)dz_{i}(P)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\omega_{k}^{\prime}(\infty^{i})+o(\varepsilon^{2}),

and we can write

\displaystyle f(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)=\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(\chi_{1}(\varepsilon),\ \dots\ ,\chi_{g}(\varepsilon)\Big).

Differentiating
f
with respect to
\varepsilon
at
\varepsilon=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(0)=\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big);

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(0)=\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}^{\prime}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)+\sum_{k,l=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\omega_{l}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\partial_{z_{l}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big).

On the other hand, since
\Theta_{\Delta}
is odd, it follows that
f(0)=\Theta_{\Delta}(0)=0
. We thus get (
3.34
) expanding
f
in the Taylor series near
\varepsilon=0
.

We now move on to prove the stated formulas for the functions
s_{ij}
.

First case:
j\neq i
.
Take a point
\infty^{0,\varepsilon}
belonging to a small neighborhood of
\infty^{0}

with
z_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon}):=\lambda(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})^{-\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}}=\varepsilon
.

In view of (
3.28
) and (
3.35
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\infty^{0,\varepsilon}}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)}\right)-\log\left(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)}\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)}\right)-\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\log(\varepsilon)+\log\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)+O(\varepsilon)\bigg).

Therefore by subtracting the divergent term
\log(\varepsilon)=\log\big(z_{0}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})\big)

and letting
\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0
, we deduce that

\displaystyle p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=\log\bigg(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\log\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\bigg)-\log(-1).

Second case:
j=i
.
Take
\infty^{0,\varepsilon}
as in the first case and choose
\infty^{i,\epsilon}
in neighborhood of
\infty^{i}
such that
z_{i}(\infty^{i,\epsilon}):=\lambda(\infty^{i,\epsilon})^{-\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}}=\epsilon
.

Again by virtue of (
3.28
) and (
3.35
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\infty^{0,\varepsilon}}^{\infty^{i,\epsilon}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i,\epsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)\Big)-\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i,\epsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)\Big)+\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\log(\epsilon)+\log\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)+O(\epsilon)\bigg)-\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0,\varepsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i,\epsilon})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)\Big)+\log(\varepsilon)+\log\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)+O(\varepsilon)\bigg).

Thus, by arguing as above we can see that

\displaystyle s_{ii}:=p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)

\displaystyle=\log\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\bigg)+\log\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-2\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)\Big)-\log(-1).

The proof of the theorem is now complete.

\square

Remark 3.4

Assume that the surface
C_{g}
is of genus
g\geq 1
and consider the holomorphic differentials
\omega_{k}
and the multivalued differentials
\phi_{u^{k}}
(
3.29
). Similarly to (
3.32
), we can also express the flat functions
s^{i}(\omega_{k})
and
s^{i}\big(\phi_{u^{k}})
in terms of the Riemann Theta function and the Abel map. More precisely, for any
k=1,\dots,g
, we have

\displaystyle s^{i}(\omega_{k})=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\omega_{k}=\mathcal{A}_{k}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}_{k}(\infty^{0});

\displaystyle s^{i}(\phi_{u^{k}})=-\oint_{a_{k}}\log\bigg(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)}\bigg)d\lambda(Q).

In addition, if we make use of (
3.28
) as well as the quasi-periodicity properties of the Riemann theta function, then we observe that:

s^{i}(\omega_{k})=s^{i}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})=\rho^{k}(\phi_{s^{i}})\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad s^{i}(\phi_{u^{k}})=u^{k}(\phi_{s^{i}}),\quad\quad\forall\ i,k.

Let us define the
N\times N
-matrix
H_{AB}:=t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})
for the
N
operations
\{t^{A}\}
in (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) and the corresponding differentials
\{\phi_{t^{A}}\}
given by (
3.23
). The next result studies the symmetry property of this matrix.

Proposition 3.5

Let
P_{0}
be a marked point on
C_{g}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=0
. Assume that all the cycles
\{a_{k},b_{k}\}
start at the point
P_{0}
. Then the following symmetry property holds:

t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})=t^{B}(\phi_{t^{A}})+\log(-1)\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(1-\delta_{ij})\delta_{s^{i},t^{A}}\delta_{s^{j},t^{B}},

(3.36)

where
t^{A}
and
t^{B}
are from the list of flat functions (
3.21
)-(
3.22
).

Proof:
According to Theorem
3.3
, we know that

s^{j}(\phi_{s^{i}})=s_{ij}=s_{ji}+\log(-1)=s^{i}(\phi_{s^{j}})+\log(-1),\quad\forall\ i\neq j.

When
t^{A}
or
t^{B}
is not of type
s^{i}
, then it suffices to justify (
3.36
) when the contours defining the functions
t^{A}
and
t^{B}
have an intersection point. Thus we need to discuss the following three cases

\rho^{k}\big(\phi_{u^{k}}\big)=u^{k}\big(\phi_{\rho^{k}}\big),\quad\quad v^{i}\big(\phi_{s^{i}}\big)=s^{i}\big(\phi_{v^{i}}\big)\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad t^{i,\alpha}\big(\phi_{s^{i}}\big)=s^{i}\big(\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\big).

Equality (
3.30
) tells us that

\rho^{j}\big(\phi_{u^{k}}\big)=u^{k}\big(\phi_{\rho^{j}}\big)-\lambda(P_{0})\delta_{jk},\quad j,k=1,\dots,g,

with
P_{0}
being the intersection point of the cycles
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
. Thus the first equality holds true under the added assumption on the cycles
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
.

Let us prove the second equality. Denote by
\gamma_{i}
a curve on the surface
C_{g}
from
\infty^{0}
to
\infty^{i}
not passing through any of the ramification points
P_{j}
or any of the
\infty^{j}
except of extremities.

Let

i)
U
be a small connected open subset of
C_{g}
containing the point
\infty^{i}
such that

\forall\ P,Q\in U,\quad W(P,Q)=\Big(\frac{1}{(z_{i}(P)-z_{i}(Q))^{2}}+\sum_{j,l\geq 0}c_{j,l}z_{i}^{j}(P)z_{i}^{l}(Q)\Big)dz_{i}(P)dz_{i}(Q),

where
z_{i}(P)
is the standard local parameter near
\infty^{i}
defined by
\lambda(P)=z_{i}^{-n_{i}-1}(P)
;

ii)
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
be a cycle inside
U
such that

\underset{Q=\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{Q\in\mathfrak{c}_{i}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q);

iii)
P_{0,i}
be a point in
U\cap\gamma_{i}
chosen in such a way that there is no intersection point between the cycle
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
and the part of the curve
\gamma_{i}
from
\infty^{0}
to
P_{0,i}
.

With these assumptions, it suffices to prove that

p.v.\int_{P_{0,i}}^{\infty^{i}}\bigg(\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)\bigg)=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\lambda(Q)\int_{P_{0,i}}^{\infty^{i}}W(P,Q)\bigg).

We have, using the notation introduced in (
2.7
),

\displaystyle\forall\ P\in U,\quad\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{i}!}W^{(n_{i})}(P,\infty^{i})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{n_{i}+1}{z_{i}(P)^{n_{i}+2}}+\sum_{j\geq 0}c_{j,n_{i}}z_{i}^{j}(P)\Big)dz_{i}(P)

\displaystyle=-d\lambda(P)+\Big(\sum_{j\geq 0}c_{j,n_{i}}z_{i}^{j}(P)\Big)dz_{i}(P)

and then

\displaystyle\int_{P_{0,i}}^{\infty^{i,\varepsilon}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\lambda(P_{0,i})-\lambda(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})+\int_{z_{i}(P_{0,i})}^{\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{j\geq 0}c_{j,n_{i}}z_{i}^{j}(P)\Big)dz_{i}(P)

\displaystyle=\lambda(P_{0,i})-\lambda(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})+\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{c_{j,n_{i}}}{j+1}\big(\varepsilon^{j+1}-z_{i}^{j+1}(P_{0,i})\big),

where
\varepsilon=z_{i}(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})=\Big(\lambda(\infty^{i,\varepsilon})\Big)^{-\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}}
.

Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
and omitting the divergent term, we arrive at

p.v.\int_{P_{0,i}}^{\infty^{i}}\bigg(\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)\bigg)=\lambda(P_{0,i})-\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{c_{j,n_{i}}}{j+1}z_{i}^{j+1}(P_{0,i}).

On the other hand

\displaystyle\int_{P_{0,i}}^{\infty^{i}}W(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{z_{i}(P_{0,i})}^{0}\bigg(\frac{1}{(z_{i}(P)-z_{i}(Q))^{2}}+\sum_{j,l\geq 0}c_{j,l}z_{i}^{j}(P)z_{i}^{l}(Q)\bigg)dz_{i}(P)\right)dz_{i}(Q)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{1}{z_{i}(Q)}+\frac{1}{z_{i}(P_{0,i})-z_{i}(Q)}-\sum_{j,l\geq 0}\frac{c_{j,l}}{j+1}z_{i}^{j+1}(P_{0,i})z_{i}^{l}(Q)\bigg)dz_{i}(Q).

Therefore

\displaystyle\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\lambda(Q)\int_{P_{0,i}}^{\infty^{i}}W(P,Q)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\underset{0}{\rm res}\left(z_{i}^{-n_{i}-1}(Q)\bigg(\frac{1}{z_{i}(Q)}+\frac{1}{z_{i}(P_{0,i})-z_{i}(Q)}-\sum_{j,l\geq 0}\frac{c_{j,l}}{j+1}z_{i}^{j+1}(P_{0,i})z_{i}^{l}(Q)\bigg)dz_{i}(Q)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z_{i}^{n_{i}+1}(P_{0,i})}-\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{c_{j,n_{i}}}{j+1}z_{i}^{j+1}(P_{0,i})

\displaystyle=\lambda(P_{0,i})-\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{c_{j,n_{i}}}{j+1}z_{i}^{j+1}(P_{0,i}).

Thus we have established that
v^{i}\big(\phi_{s^{i}}\big)=s^{i}\big(\phi_{v^{i}}\big)
. The symmetry
t^{{i,\alpha}}\big(\phi_{s^{i}}\big)=s^{i}\big(\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\big)
can be shown similarly.

\square

Theorem 3.4

Let
\big\{\big(C_{g},\lambda\big),\{a_{k},b_{k}\}\big\}
be a point of (a fixed covering of) the simple Hurwitz space

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
and
P_{0}
be a marked point on
C_{g}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=0
. Assume that all the cycles
\{a_{k},b_{k}\}
start at the point
P_{0}
.

Then the
N
flat functions of the set
\mathcal{S}(\omega)
defined by (
3.21
)-(
3.22
) give a system of flat coordinates of the Dubrovin metric
\eta(\omega)
.

Moreover, in these coordinates, the nonzero entries of the constant matrix of the contravariant metric
\eta^{*}(\omega)
are as follows:

\begin{split}&\eta^{*}(\omega)(dt^{i,\alpha}(\omega),dt^{j,\beta}(\omega))=\delta_{ij}\delta_{\alpha+\beta,n_{j}+1};\\
&\eta^{*}(\omega)(dv^{i}(\omega),ds^{j}(\omega))=\delta_{ij};\\
&\eta^{*}(\omega)(d\rho^{i}(\omega),du^{j}(\omega))=\delta_{ij}.\end{split}

(3.37)

Proof:
We already know that the functions of the set
\mathcal{S}(\omega)
are all flat with respect to the metric
\eta(\omega)
.
Let us calculate the matrix of
\eta^{*}(\omega)
in these coordinates. The proof relies on the following tools:

- the characteristic properties of the differentials (
3.23
) that were described in Proposition
3.4
;

- the following particular versions of formulas (
3.18
)-(
3.19
) for
\eta^{*}(\omega)=\left(\mathbf{ds^{2}_{0,1}}(\omega)\right)^{*}
:

\eta^{*}(\omega)\left(dt^{A}(\omega),dt^{B}(\omega)\right)=e.t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\underset{P_{k}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\phi_{t^{B}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)},

(3.38)

where
e
is the vector field
e:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
.

Note that the symmetry property (
3.36
) implies that
e.t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})=e.t^{B}(\phi_{t^{A}})
.

From their analytic properties, the differentials
\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}
,
\phi_{v^{i}}
,
\phi_{s^{i}}
and
\phi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k}
are all Abelian differentials on the surface
C_{g}
and they are all holomorphic near any simple ramification point
P_{k}
. Therefore, by analysing the poles of the differential
\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\phi_{t^{B}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}
and applying the residue theorem, it follows that the right hand side of (
3.38
) is straightforwardly computed provided that
t^{A}
and
t^{B}
are among the four types of operations
\big\{t^{i,\alpha},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k}\big\}
.

For example if we consider the case
t^{A}=t^{i,\alpha}
and
t^{B}=t^{i,\beta}
, with
i=0,\dots,m
and
\alpha,\beta=1,\dots,n_{i}
, then the residue theorem and the behavior

\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\Big(\sqrt{n_{i}+1}z_{i}(P)^{-\alpha-1}+\text{reg}\Big)dz_{i}(P),\quad\quad\lambda(P)=z_{i}(P)^{-n_{i}-1},

imply that

\displaystyle\eta^{*}(\omega)\left(dt^{A}(\omega),dt^{B}(\omega)\right)=e.t^{i,\alpha}(\phi_{t^{i,\beta}})=-\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}(P)\phi_{t^{i,\beta}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\underset{0}{{\rm res}}\bigg(z_{i}(P)^{n_{i}+2}\Big(\sqrt{n_{i}+1}z_{i}(P)^{-\alpha-1}+\text{reg}\Big)\Big(\sqrt{n_{i}+1}z_{i}(P)^{-\beta-1}+\text{reg}\Big)\bigg)dz_{i}(P)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha+\beta,n_{i}+1}.

However, the use of (
3.38
) in the case where
\phi_{t^{A}}=\phi_{u^{k}}
is the multivalued differential having jumps along the cycle
b_{k}
needs some details.

For
k=1,\dots,g
, let us consider the differential

\Phi_{k}(P):=\frac{\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{u^{k}}(P),

(3.39)

where
\phi
is one of the differentials listed in (
3.23
). Then
\Phi_{k}
is a multivalued differential and by (
3.29
), its jumps are as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\Phi_{k}(P+a_{j})-\Phi_{k}(P)=0,&\hbox{if}\quad j=1,\dots,g;\\
\\
\Phi_{k}(P+b_{k})-\Phi_{k}(P)=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\phi(P),&\hbox{if}\quad\phi\neq\phi_{u^{k}};\\
\\
\Phi_{k}(P+b_{k})-\Phi_{k}(P)=-4{\rm{i}}\pi\phi_{u^{k}}(P)-4\pi^{2}d\lambda(P),&\hbox{if}\quad\phi=\phi_{u^{k}};\\
\\
\Phi_{k}(P+b_{j})-\Phi_{k}(P)=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\phi_{u^{k}}(P),&\hbox{if}\quad\phi=\phi_{u^{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad j\neq k;\\
\\
\Phi_{k}(P+b_{j})-\Phi_{k}(P)=0,&\hbox{if}\quad\text{$\phi$ is Abelian and $j\neq k$}.\end{array}

In addition, since the multivalued differentials
\phi_{u^{1}},\dots,\phi_{u^{g}}
are all single valued and holomorphic inside the fundamental polygon
F_{g}
associated with a compact surface
C_{g}
, it follows that the differential
\Phi_{k}
is single valued and meromorphic inside
F_{g}
and has simple poles at the ramifications points
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}
.

Furthermore, by using the distinctive analytic properties of the differentials (
3.23
) (see Proposition
3.4
) and keeping in mind the presence of the factor
d\lambda(P)
, we deduce that
\Phi_{k}
has no other poles inside the fundamental polygon
F_{g}
.

Thus, by applying the residue theorem and recalling that
\partial F_{g}=\sum_{j=1}^{g}\big(a_{j}+b_{j}+a_{j}^{-1}+b_{j}^{-1}\big)
and taking into account the mentioned jumps of
\Phi_{k}
, we obtain

\displaystyle e.u^{k}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{N}\underset{P_{s}}{{\rm res}}\Phi_{k}(P)=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{\partial{F_{g}}}\Phi_{k}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{a_{j}}\Phi_{k}(P)+\oint_{a_{j}^{-1}}\Phi_{k}(Q)\bigg)+\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{b_{j}}\Phi_{k}(P)+\oint_{b_{j}^{-1}}\Phi_{k}(Q)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{a_{j}}\Phi_{k}(P)-\oint_{a_{j}}\Phi_{k}(P+b_{j})\bigg)+\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{b_{j}}\Phi_{k}(P)-\oint_{b_{j}}\underbrace{\Phi_{k}(P+a_{j}^{-1})}_{=\Phi_{k}(P)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=1,j\neq k}^{g}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{u^{j}}}\oint_{a_{j}}\phi_{u^{k}}\bigg)+\big(1-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{u^{k}}}\big)\oint_{a_{k}}\phi+\delta_{\phi,\phi_{u^{k}}}\oint_{a_{k}}\Big(2\phi_{u^{k}}(P)-2{\rm{i}}\pi d\lambda(P)\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(1-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{u^{k}}}\big)\delta_{\phi,\phi_{\rho^{k}}}=\delta_{\phi,\phi_{\rho^{k}}},

where
\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{B}}}
denotes the Kronecker delta function.

\square

Remark 3.5

Observe that if
g\geq 1
, then by using explicit formulas (
3.32
) for the functions
s_{ij}
, we see that the action of the vector field
e:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
on
s_{ij}
is completely determined by knowing its action on the following functions:

\mathbb{B}_{kl}=2{\rm{i}}\pi\rho^{l}(\omega_{k}),\quad\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})=\frac{t^{i,1}(\omega_{k})}{\sqrt{n_{i}+1}},\quad\mathcal{A}_{k}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}_{k}(\infty^{0})=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\omega_{k}=s^{i}(\omega_{k}).

This provides an alternative way to show that
e.s_{ij}=0
.

Using (
3.37
), we remark that all the rows of the constant matrix of the Darboux-Egoroff metric
\eta^{*}(\omega)
contain exactly one nonzero
entry which is 1.This permits us to introduce the following natural duality relation between flat coordinates of
\eta(\omega)
:

Definition 3.1

For each
t^{A}\in\mathcal{S}(\omega):=\Big\{t^{i,\alpha}(\omega),v^{i}(\omega),s^{i}(\omega),\rho^{k}(\omega),u^{k}(\omega)\Big\}
, let us denote by
t^{A^{\prime}}
the unique flat function belonging to the set
\mathcal{S}(\omega)
such that

\eta^{*}(\omega)\big(dt^{A}(\omega),dt^{A^{\prime}}(\omega)\big)=1.

(3.40)

The flat coordinates
t^{A}
and
t^{A^{\prime}}
are called dual to each other with respect to the metric
\eta(\omega)
.

Let us mention the following remarks:

•

From (
3.37
) and (
3.38
), it follows that the introduced
\eta(\omega)
-duality does not depend on the choice of the differential
\omega
that we work with.

•

For any
A
, we have
t^{(A^{\prime})^{\prime}}(\omega)=t^{A}(\omega)
.

•

Due to (
3.37
), the five families of operations
t^{i,\alpha},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k}
and
u^{k}
are connected by:

\begin{split}&t^{({i,\alpha})^{\prime}}(\omega)=t^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(\omega),\quad\quad\quad\quad v^{{i}^{\prime}}(\omega)=s^{i}(\omega),\quad\quad\ \quad\rho^{k^{\prime}}(\omega)=u^{k}(\omega);\\
&\phi_{t^{({i,\alpha})^{\prime}}}(P)=\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha}}(P),\quad\quad\quad\phi_{v^{i^{\prime}}}(P)=\phi_{s^{i}}(P),\quad\quad\phi_{\rho^{k^{\prime}}}(P)=\phi_{u^{k}}(P).\end{split}

(3.41)

Proposition 3.6

The derivatives of the canonical coordinates
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq N}
with respect to
\eta(\omega)
-flat coordinates
t^{A}(\omega)
belonging to the system (
3.21
) are as follows

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\lambda_{j}}{\partial t^{A}(\omega)}=\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})},

(3.42)

where
\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}
is the differential (
3.14
) associated with the
\eta(\omega)
-flat function
t^{A^{\prime}}(\omega)
.

Proof:
In
[
14
]
, Dubrovin has proven (
3.42
) by using a thermodynamical type identity. We suggest a different proof based on Rauch’s formulas, (
3.37
) as well as some basic facts from linear algebra.

Let us denote by
(\Lambda_{AB})
the constant matrix of
\eta(\omega)
in flat coordinates
\{t^{A}(\omega)\}
and
(\eta_{jj})
the diagonal matrix of
\eta(\omega)
in the coordinates
\{\lambda_{j}\}
.

On one hand, Rauch’s formula (
3.15
) yields that

dt^{A}(\omega)=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}t^{A}(\omega)d\lambda_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\omega(P_{j})\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})d\lambda_{j}.

On the other hand, using twice the fact that
\eta(\omega)
induces a linear isomorphism from the tangent space onto the cotangent space, namely regarding
(\Lambda_{AB})
and
(\eta_{jj})
as the change of basis matrices, we can write

\displaystyle dt^{A}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{B}\Lambda^{AB}\eta(\omega)\Big(\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)},\cdot\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{B}\Lambda^{AB}\eta(\omega)\Big(\sum_{j}\big(\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}\lambda_{j}\big)\partial_{\lambda_{j}},\cdot\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\sum_{B}\Lambda^{AB}\big(\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}\lambda_{j}\big)\eta(\omega)(\partial_{\lambda_{j}},\cdot)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\bigg(\eta_{jj}\sum_{B}\Lambda^{AB}\big(\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}\lambda_{j}\big)\bigg)d\lambda_{j}.

Thus we must have

\sum_{B}\Lambda^{AB}\big(\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}\lambda_{j}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{jj}\omega(P_{j})\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})=\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}.

Since
\Lambda^{AB}=\eta^{*}(\omega)\big(dt^{A}(\omega),dt^{B}(\omega)\big)=\delta_{t^{B},t^{A^{\prime}}}
, we deduce that

\sum_{B}\Lambda^{AB}\big(\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}\lambda_{j}\big)=\partial_{t^{A^{\prime}}(\omega)}\lambda_{j}.

This establishes the desired formula (
3.42
).

\square

Corollary 3.1

The following assertions hold true:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)}=\sum_{j}\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}\partial_{\lambda_{j}};\end{split}

(3.43)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\eta(\omega)\left(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}(P_{j})=\eta^{*}(\omega)\left(dt^{A^{\prime}}(\omega),t^{B^{\prime}}(\omega)\right).\end{split}

(3.44)

In particular, the flat metric
\eta(\omega)
can be written as

\eta(\omega)=\textstyle\sum_{A}dt^{A}(\omega)\otimes dt^{A^{\prime}}(\omega).

(3.45)

Proof:
The relation (
3.43
) is a direct consequence of (
3.42
) combined with the chain rule formula. The first equality in (
3.44
) follows from (
3.43
) and (
3.20
) and the second one from (
3.17
).
Lastly, in view of (
3.44
) and (
3.37
), we obtain the stated expression for the metric
\eta(\omega)
.

\square

Remark 3.6

In the case where
\omega=\phi
is a primary differential, Dubrovin’s method to calculate the constant matrix of the flat metric
\eta(\phi)
in flat coordinates uses his bilinear pairing (see formula (5.60) in
[
14
]
). In
[
41
]
, p. 562-564, Shramchenko employed the same method and gave a detailed computation of constant matrices of the Daroubx-Egoroff metrics in the real double setting of Frobenius manifolds.

We close this subsection by mentioning the following duality-exchange relation:

Proposition 3.7

Let
\omega
be a given differential satisfying (
3.1
) and (
3.3
). Let
t^{A}(\omega),t^{B}(\omega)
be two
\eta(\omega)
-flat coordinates from the set (
3.21
)-(
3.22
) and
\phi_{t^{A}}
,
\phi_{t^{B}}
be the corresponding differentials belonging to the list (
3.23
).

Then the following holds:

\frac{\partial\phi_{t^{B}}(P)}{\partial{t^{A}(\omega)}}=\frac{\partial\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P)}{\partial{t^{B^{\prime}}(\omega)}}.

Proof:
We have

\displaystyle\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)}\phi_{t^{B}}(P)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\phi_{t^{B}}(P)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}\phi_{t^{B}}(P_{j})W(P,P_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\frac{\phi_{t^{B}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P)=\partial_{t^{B^{\prime}}(\omega)}\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P),

where the first and fourth equalities follow from (
3.43
), while the second and third ones are a direct consequence of Rauch’s formulas
(
3.2
).

\square

3.4
Flat coordinates of intersection forms

According to Dubrovin
[
14
]
, given a semi-simple Frobenius manifold
(\mathcal{M},\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})
, there is a second flat metric
\mathfrak{g}
, called the intersection form, defined on an open subset of
\mathcal{M}
. The metric
\mathfrak{g}
and the initial metric
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
give rise to the so-called flat pencil.

In the case of a semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifolds, the (covariant metric induced by the) intersection form is defined by:

\mathbf{g}(\omega):=\mathbf{ds^{2}_{1,0}}(\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\omega(P_{j})^{2}}{2\lambda_{j}}(d\lambda_{j})^{2},

(3.46)

where, as above,
\omega
denotes a differential satisfying (
3.1
) and (
3.3
).

In this subsection, we focus on flat coordinates of the intersection form (
3.46
) considered as a flat metric on a connected open subset
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{m})
of the double Hurwitz space. Recently, Romano used a Dubrovin type bilinear pairing and gave a set of flat coordinates of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega)

[
34
]
. However, he did not calculate the corresponding constant matrix.

In our case, by employing the language of the symmetric bidifferential
W(P,Q)
, we show that the approach used in Section 3.3 can be adapted to study of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega)
.

Theorem 3.5

Let
\big\{\big(C_{g},\lambda\big),\{a_{k},b_{k}\}\big\}
be a point of the double Hurwitz space

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{m})
and Let
P_{0}
be a marked point on
C_{g}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=1
. Assume that all the cycles
\{a_{k},b_{k}\}
start at the point
P_{0}
and the differential
\omega
has zero
a
-periods.

Then the following
N=2g+m+n
functions provide a system of flat coordinates of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega)
:

\begin{array}[]{llll}s^{i}(\omega):=\displaystyle p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\omega(P),&\quad i=1,\dots,m;\\
\\
y^{j}(\omega):=\displaystyle p.v.\int_{\mathbf{z}^{0}}^{\mathbf{z}^{j}}\omega(P),&\quad j=1,\dots,n;\\
\\
\rho^{k}(\omega):=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b_{k}}\omega(P),&\quad k=1,\dots,g;\\
\\
q^{k}(\omega):=\displaystyle\oint_{a_{k}}\log\big(\lambda(P)\big)\omega(P),&\quad k=1,\dots,g.\end{array}

(3.47)

In addition, in these coordinates, the nonzero entries of the contravariant metric
\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)
are

\begin{split}&\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)\big(ds^{i}(\omega),ds^{j}(\omega)\big)=\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1};\\
&\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)\big(dy^{i}(\omega),dy^{j}(\omega)\big)=-\frac{1}{m_{0}+1}-\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{m_{i}+1};\\
&\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)\big(d\rho^{i}(\omega),dq^{j}(\omega)\big)=\delta_{ij}.\end{split}

(3.48)

Here the positive integers
n_{i}+1
and
m_{j}+1
denote respectively the multiplicities of the prescribed ramifications points
\infty^{i}
and
\mathbf{z}^{j}
, with
i=0,\dots,m
and
j=0,\dots,n
.

Before we start to prove this theorem, let us mention some remarks and preliminary results.

\bullet
Similarly to the function
s^{i}(\omega)
, the principal value in the formula of
y^{j}(\omega)
is defined by subtraction of the divergent part of the integral as a function of the local parameters
\mathfrak{z}_{0}(P):=\lambda(P)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}+1}}
and
\mathfrak{z}_{i}(P):=\lambda(P)^{\frac{1}{m_{i}+1}}
near the points
\mathbf{z}^{0}
and
\mathbf{z}^{i}
, respectively.

\bullet
Let us denote by
\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{i}}(P)
the normalized Abelian differential of the third kind having simple poles at the points
\mathbf{z}^{0}
and
\mathbf{z}^{i}
, i.e.

\underset{\mathbf{z}^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{i}}=1,\quad\underset{\mathbf{z}^{0}}{{\rm res}}\ \Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{i}}=-1,\quad\oint_{a_{k}}\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{i}}=0,\quad\forall\ k=1,\dots,g.

We emphasize that formulas analogous to (
3.27
) and (
3.28
) and a direct adaptation of Theorem
3.3
allow us to calculate the functions

y_{ij}:=p.v.\int_{\mathbf{z}^{0}}^{\mathbf{z}^{j}}\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{i}}(P),\quad\quad\forall\ i,j=1,\dots,n.

More precisely, we obtain the following statements:

1)

Assume that
g=0
and
\mathbf{z}^{0}\in\mathbb{C}
. For
i=0,\dots,n
, consider the meromorphic function
\nu_{i}
defined by

\nu_{i}(P)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(z_{P}-\mathbf{z}^{i})^{-m_{i}-1}\lambda(P),&\quad\hbox{if}\quad\mathbf{z}^{i}\in\mathbb{C},\\
\\
z_{P}^{m_{i}+1}\lambda(P),&\quad\hbox{if}\quad\mathbf{z}^{i}=\infty.\end{array}\right.

(3.49)

i)

If
i\neq j
and
\mathbf{z}^{i},\mathbf{z}^{j}\in\mathbb{C}
, then

y_{ij}=\log\left(\frac{\mathbf{z}^{j}-\mathbf{z}^{i}}{\big(\mathbf{z}^{j}-\mathbf{z}^{0}\big)\big(\mathbf{z}^{0}-\mathbf{z}^{i}\big)}\right)-\frac{\log\big(\nu_{0}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\big)}{m_{0}+1}.

When
\mathbf{z}^{r}=\infty
, with
r=i
or
j
, we deduce the desired formula from the previous one by letting
\mathbf{z}^{r}\to\infty
.

ii)

If
i=j
we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}y_{ii}=\displaystyle-\frac{\log\big(\nu_{i}(\mathbf{z}^{i})\big)}{m_{i}+1}-\frac{\log\big(\nu_{0}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\big)}{m_{0}+1}-2\log\big(\mathbf{z}^{i}-\mathbf{z}^{0}\big)-\log(-1),&\hbox{if}\quad\mathbf{z}^{i}\in\mathbb{C};\\
\\
y_{ii}=\displaystyle-\frac{\log\big(\nu_{i}(\mathbf{z}^{i})\big)}{m_{i}+1}-\frac{\log\big(\nu_{0}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\big)}{m_{0}+1},&\hbox{if}\quad\mathbf{z}^{i}=\infty.\end{array}

2)

Assume that
g\geq 1
.

i)

When
i\neq j
, we have

y_{ij}=\log\left(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{i})\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\big)\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{i})-\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\big)}\right)+\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\right)-\log(-1).

ii)

If
i=j
we have

y_{ii}=\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\mathbf{z}^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\right)+\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\right)-2\log\left(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{i})-\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{z}^{0})\big)\right)-\log(-1).

\bullet
Consider the differentials connected with the four families of flat functions
s^{i},y^{i},\rho^{k},q^{k}

(as above, the link here is in the sense of (
3.13
) -(
3.14
)):

\begin{split}&\psi_{s^{i}}(P)=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}W(P,Q)=\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P);\\
&\psi_{y^{i}}(P)=\int_{\mathbf{z}^{0}}^{\mathbf{z}^{i}}W(P,Q)=\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{i}}(P);\\
&\psi_{\rho^{k}}(P)=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b_{k}}W(P,Q)=\omega_{k}(P);\\
&\psi_{q^{k}}(P)=\oint_{a_{k}}\log\big(\lambda(Q)\big)W(P,Q).\end{split}

(3.50)

The analytic properties of the Abelian differentials
\psi_{s^{i}},\psi_{y^{i}},\psi_{\rho^{k}}
have previously been specified. Let us deal with the differential
\psi_{q^{k}}
. Since the differential
W(P,\cdot)
has vanishing
a
-periods (
2.13
), the definition of
\psi_{q^{k}}
does not depend on the choice of the branch of the complex logarithm and its
a
-periods are all zero. Now, we claim that
\psi_{q^{k}}(P)
is a multivalued differential with jumps along the cycle
b_{k}
and

\psi_{q^{k}}(P+b_{k})-\psi_{q^{k}}(P)=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\frac{d\lambda(P)}{\lambda(P)}.

(3.51)

Indeed, using (
2.11
) and integrating by parts, we can write

\psi_{q^{k}}(P)=-\oint_{a_{k}}d_{P}\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)\Big)\frac{d\lambda(Q)}{\lambda(Q)}=dF_{q^{k}}(P),

where
F_{q^{k}}
is given by

F_{q^{k}}(P):=\int^{P}\psi_{q^{k}}=-\oint_{a_{k}}\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)\Big)\frac{d\lambda(Q)}{\lambda(Q)}.

In view of relation (
2.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle F_{q^{k}}(P+a_{j})-F_{q^{k}}(P)

\displaystyle=\oint_{a_{j}}\psi_{q^{k}}=0;

\displaystyle F_{q^{k}}(P+b_{j})-F_{q^{k}}(P)

\displaystyle=\oint_{b_{j}}\psi_{q^{k}}=-\oint_{a_{k}}\log\bigg(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P+b_{j})-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)}\bigg)\frac{d\lambda(Q)}{\lambda(Q)}

\displaystyle=-\oint_{a_{k}}\bigg(-{\rm{i}}\pi\mathop{\langle\mathbb{B}e_{j},e_{j}\rangle}-2{\rm{i}}\pi\mathop{\langle\beta,e_{j}\rangle}-2{\rm{i}}\pi\int_{Q}^{P}\omega_{j}\bigg)\frac{d\lambda(Q)}{\lambda(Q)}

\displaystyle=2{\rm{i}}\pi\oint_{a_{k}}\Big(\int_{Q}^{P}\omega_{j}\Big)\frac{d\lambda(Q)}{\lambda(Q)}

\displaystyle=2{\rm{i}}\pi\oint_{a_{k}}\bigg(d_{Q}\Big(\log\big(\lambda(Q)\big)\int_{Q}^{P}\omega_{j}\Big)+\log\big(\lambda(Q)\big)\omega_{j}(Q)\bigg)

\displaystyle=2{\rm{i}}\pi\bigg(\log\big(\lambda(Q)\big)\int_{Q}^{P}\omega_{j}\bigg)\bigg|_{Q=Q_{0}}^{Q=Q_{0}+a_{k}}+2{\rm{i}}\pi\oint_{a_{k}}\log\big(\lambda(Q)\big)\omega_{j}(Q)

\displaystyle=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\delta_{jk}\log\big(\lambda(Q_{0})\big)+2{\rm{i}}\pi q^{k}(\omega_{j}),

where
Q_{0}
is a starting point of the cycle
a_{k}
. Here, we are going to study general properties of the differential
\psi_{q^{k}}
and so (contrary to the assumption of Theorem
3.5
) we don’t suppose that the cycles
\{a_{k},b_{k}\}
start at a particular point
P_{0}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=1
.

From the above calculation, we see that
\psi_{q^{k}}(P+b_{j})-\psi_{q^{k}}(P)=0
for all
j\neq k
. Moreover, regarding
P
as a starting point of the cycle
b_{k}
in the expression

F_{q^{k}}(P+b_{j})-F_{q^{k}}(P)
and choosing
Q_{0}=P
to be the intersection points of
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
, we arrive at (
3.51
).

\bullet
Let
u^{A}(\omega),u^{B}(\omega)\in\Big\{s^{i}(\omega),y^{j}(\omega),\rho^{k}(\omega),q^{k}(\omega)\Big\}
. The above computation shows that

q^{k}(\psi_{\rho^{j}})=\oint_{a_{k}}\log\big(\lambda(Q)\big)\omega_{j}(Q)=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b_{j}}\psi_{q^{k}}=\rho^{j}(\psi_{q^{k}}),\quad\quad\forall\ j,k=1,\dots,g;

provided that the cycles
\{a_{k},b_{k}\}
start at a marked point
P_{0}
so that
\lambda(P_{0})=1
.

Analogously to the symmetry property (
3.36
), using the latter assumption as well as the explicit formulas for the functions
s_{ij}
and
y_{ij}
, we see that the interchanging of the order of two operations
u^{A},u^{B}
among
\big\{s^{i},y^{j},\rho^{k},q^{k}\big\}
only results in the addition of a constant. More concretely, the following holds:

u^{A}(\psi_{u^{B}})=u^{B}(\psi_{u^{A}})+\log(-1)\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(1-\delta_{ij})\delta_{s^{i},u^{A}}\delta_{s^{j},u^{B}}+\log(-1)\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(1-\delta_{ij})\delta_{y^{i},u^{A}}\delta_{y^{j},u^{B}}.

(3.52)

Now we are in position to prove Theorem
3.5
.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
:
By applying (
3.16
) with
\beta_{1}=1
,
\beta_{2}=0
and taking into account the properties of the symmetric bidifferential
W(P,Q)
, we deduce that the functions
s^{i}(\omega)
,
y^{j}(\omega)
,
\rho^{k}(\omega)
and
q^{k}(\omega)
in (
3.47
) are all flat with respect to the intersection form
\mathbf{g}(\omega)
.

Now, in order to calculate the entries of the constant matrix of the metric
\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)
, we will adapt the scheme of the proof of Theorem
3.4
.
More precisely, the crucial ingredient is a formula analogous to (
3.38
):

\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)\left(du^{A}(\omega),du^{B}(\omega)\right)=E.u^{A}(\psi_{u^{B}})=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\underset{P_{k}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{u^{A}}(P)\psi_{u^{B}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)},

(3.53)

where
E
is the vector field
E:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
and
\psi_{u^{A}}(P)
,
\psi_{u^{B}}(P)
are among the differentials in (
3.50
). Note that (
3.53
) is an immediate consequence of (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) where we take
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(1,0)
.

Due to the residue theorem and (
3.53
) we have

\displaystyle E.s^{i}(\psi_{s^{j}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\underset{P_{k}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=-\underset{\infty^{0}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}-\delta_{ij}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}.

Similarly we get

\displaystyle E.s^{i}(\psi_{y^{j}})=E.s^{i}(\psi_{\rho^{k}})=E.y^{i}(\psi_{\rho^{k}})=E.\rho^{k}(\psi_{\rho^{j}})=0;

\displaystyle E.y^{i}(\psi_{y^{j}})=-\frac{1}{m_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{m_{i}+1}.

It remains to make precise the action of vector field
E
on the functions
q^{k}(\psi)
with
\psi\in\big\{\psi_{s^{i}},\psi_{y^{j}},\psi_{\rho^{k}},\psi_{q^{k}}\big\}
. To this end, let us consider the differential

\Psi_{k}(P):=\frac{\lambda(P)\psi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\psi_{q^{k}}(P),\quad k=1,\dots,g,

where
\psi
is one of the differentials belonging to (
3.50
).

Then
\Psi_{k}
is a multivalued differential and its jumps are as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\Psi_{k}(P+a_{j})-\Psi_{k}(P)=0,&\hbox{if}\quad j=1,\dots,g;\\
\\
\Psi_{k}(P+b_{k})-\Psi_{k}(P)=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\psi(P),&\hbox{if}\quad\psi\neq\psi_{q^{k}};\\
\\
\Psi_{k}(P+b_{k})-\Psi_{k}(P)=-4{\rm{i}}\pi\psi_{q^{k}}(P)-4\pi^{2}\frac{d\lambda(P)}{\lambda(P)},&\hbox{if}\quad\psi=\psi_{q^{k}};\\
\\
\Psi_{k}(P+b_{j})-\Psi_{k}(P)=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\psi_{q^{k}}(P),&\hbox{if}\quad\psi=\psi_{q^{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad j\neq k;\\
\\
\Psi_{k}(P+b_{j})-\Psi_{k}(P)=0,&\hbox{if}\quad\text{$\psi$ is Abelian and $j\neq k$}.\end{array}

To summarize, the jumps along the cycles
b_{j}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}(P+b_{j})-\Psi_{k}(P)

\displaystyle=\big(1-\delta_{\psi,\psi_{q^{k}}}\big)\big(-2{\rm{i}}\pi\psi(P)\big)+\delta_{\psi,\psi_{q^{k}}}\Big(-4{\rm{i}}\pi\psi_{q^{k}}(P)-4\pi^{2}\frac{d\lambda(P)}{\lambda(P)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+(1-\delta_{jk})\delta_{\psi,\psi_{q^{j}}}\big(-2{\rm{i}}\pi\psi_{q^{k}}(P)\big).

On the other hand, observing that the multivalued differentials
\psi_{q^{1}},\dots,\psi_{q^{g}}
are all single valued and holomorphic inside the fundamental polygon
F_{g}
associated with
C_{g}
, we deduce that the differential
\Psi_{k}
becomes meromorphic inside
F_{g}
and the ramification points
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}
are its unique simple poles belonging to
F_{g}
.

Therefore, from (
3.53
) we have

\displaystyle E.q^{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{N}\underset{P_{s}}{{\rm res}}\Psi_{k}(P)=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{\partial{F_{g}}}\Psi_{k}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{a_{j}}\Psi_{k}(P)-\oint_{a_{j}}\Psi_{k}(P+b_{j})\bigg)+\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{b_{j}}\Psi_{k}(P)-\oint_{b_{j}}\Psi_{k}(P+a_{j}^{-1})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=1,j\neq k}^{g}\delta_{\psi,\psi_{q^{j}}}\oint_{a_{j}}\psi_{q^{k}}\bigg)+\big(1-\delta_{\psi,\psi_{q^{k}}}\big)\oint_{a_{k}}\psi+\delta_{\psi,\psi_{q^{k}}}\oint_{a_{k}}\bigg(2\psi_{q^{k}(P)}-2{\rm{i}}\pi\frac{d\lambda(P)}{\lambda(P)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\big(1-\delta_{\psi,\psi_{q^{k}}}\big)\delta_{\psi,\psi_{\rho^{k}}}=\delta_{\psi,\psi_{\rho^{k}}},

with
\delta_{\psi,\psi_{\rho^{k}}}
being the Kronecker delta. This finishes the proof of the theorem.

\square

Corollary 3.2

The nonzero entries of constant matrix of the intersection form
\mathbf{g}(\omega)
are given by

\begin{split}&\mathbf{g}(\omega)\big(\partial_{s^{i}(\omega)},\partial_{s^{j}(\omega)}\big)=(n_{i}+1)\delta_{ij}-\frac{(n_{i}+1)(n_{j}+1)}{L};\\
&\mathbf{g}(\omega)\big(\partial_{y^{i}(\omega)},\partial_{y^{j}(\omega)}\big)=-(m_{i}+1)\delta_{ij}+\frac{(m_{i}+1)(m_{j}+1)}{L};\\
&\mathbf{g}(\omega)\big(\partial_{\rho^{i}(\omega)},\partial_{q^{j}(\omega)}\big)=\delta_{ij}.\end{split}

(3.54)

Here
L
denotes the common number of sheets of the coverings
[(C_{g},\lambda)]\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{m})
.

Proof:
Let
\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{A}_{ij})
be a
m\times m
square matrix of the form

\mathbf{A}_{ij}=\alpha_{0}+\delta_{ij}\alpha_{i},

where
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}
are all nonzero complex numbers. Then one can check that the matrix
\mathbf{A}
is invertible and the entries of its inverse are:

\mathbf{A}^{ij}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\delta_{ij}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\sigma},\quad\quad\text{with}\quad\sigma:=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{1}{\alpha_{k}}.

When
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{m}
are the positive real numbers:

\alpha_{i}=\frac{1}{n_{i}+1},\quad\forall\ i=0,1,\dots,m,

we have

\sigma=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{1}{\alpha_{k}}=L.

This and (
3.48
) imply (
3.54
).

\square

Corollary 3.3

Let
u^{A}(\omega)\in\Big\{s^{i}(\omega),y^{j}(\omega),\rho^{k}(\omega),q^{k}(\omega)\Big\}
. Then the partial derivative
\partial_{u^{A}(\omega)}
has the following form

\partial_{u^{A}(\omega)}=\sum_{j}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\omega(P_{j})}\sum_{\alpha}\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\psi_{u^{\alpha}}(P_{j})\bigg)\partial_{\lambda_{j}},

(3.55)

where
\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}=\mathbf{g}(\omega)\big(\partial_{u^{A}(\omega)},\partial_{u^{\alpha}(\omega)}\big)
are the entries (
3.54
) of the constant matrix of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega)
and
\psi_{u^{A}}
is among the differentials (
3.50
) corresponding to the function
u^{A}
.

Proof:
By arguing as in the proof of (
3.42
), we can check that the partial derivatives of
\lambda_{j}
with respect to flat coordinates
\{u^{A}(\omega)\}_{A}
are given by

\partial_{u^{A}(\omega)}\lambda_{j}=\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\omega(P_{j})}\sum_{\alpha}\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\psi_{u^{\alpha}}(P_{j}).

This shows that (
3.55
) holds true.

\square

At the end of this subsection, we shall describe the matrix of the intersection form
\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)
in flat coordinates (
3.21
)-(
3.22
) of the Darboux-Egoroff metric
\eta(\omega)
defined by (
3.20
). Then we shall view
\mathbf{g}(\omega)
as a metric on an open subset of the particular double Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{0})
. The latter is an open subset of the simple Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by the conditions
\lambda_{j}\neq 0
, for all
j=1,\dots,N
.

From
[
14
]
(see also next section), it is known that the vector field
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
plays the role of the Euler vector field of the semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold structures induced by Dubrovin’s primary differentials (
3.23
). Thus the obtained matrix of
\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)
describes, through formula (
3.56
) below, the quasi-homogeneity properties of flat coordinates of the
N
Darboux-Egoroff metrics
\big\{\eta(\phi_{t^{A}})\big\}_{A}
of Dubrovin.

Theorem 3.6

Let
\omega(P)=\int_{\gamma}h(\lambda(Q))W(P,Q)
be a differential on a surface
C_{g}
satisfying (
3.1
) and (
3.3
),
\eta(\omega)
be the corresponding Darboux-Egoroff metric (
3.20
) and
E
be the vector field
E=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
.

Then, in the flat coordinates
t^{A}(\omega)\in\mathcal{S}(\omega)
described in (
3.21
)-(
3.22
), the entries of the matrix of the intersection form
\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)

are:

\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)\left(dt^{A}(\omega),dt^{B}(\omega)\right)=E.t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})=(d_{A}+d_{B})t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})+r_{A,B},

(3.56)

where
r_{A,B}
is the constant

r_{A,B}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\delta_{t^{A},s^{i}}\delta_{t^{B},s^{j}}\Big(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\Big)=r_{B,A}

(3.57)

and
d_{A}
(resp.
d_{B}
) is the nonnegative constant appearing in the integral representation (
3.24
) of the differential
\phi_{t^{A}}
(resp.
\phi_{t^{B}}
).

Proof:
According to Theorem
3.2
and formulas (
3.18
)-(
3.19
), the following two equalities are valid:

\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega)\left(dt^{A}(\omega),dt^{B}(\omega)\right)=E.t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\underset{P_{k}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\phi_{t^{B}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}.

This shows the first equality in (
3.56
).

Let
\phi
be one of the differentials belonging to the set (
3.23
). If
\phi\neq\phi_{u^{k}}
(i.e.
\phi
has no jumps), then the point
\infty^{i}
is a pole of the meromorphic function
\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}
if and only if
\phi
is of type
\phi=\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}
or
\phi=\phi_{v^{i}}
.
Moreover, according to behaviors (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) of the Abelian differentials
\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}
and
\phi_{v^{i}}
near the point
\infty^{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\Big(\frac{z_{i}(P)^{-\alpha}}{\sqrt{n_{i}+1}}+\text{reg}\Big)-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}\Big(z_{i}(P)^{-n_{i}-1}+\text{reg}\Big)

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{i}+1}}\lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}+\text{reg}\Big)-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}\Big(\lambda(P)+\text{reg}\Big).

Moreover, when
\phi=\phi_{u^{k}}
,
k=1,\dots,g
, then the restriction of the function
\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}
to the fundamental polygon
F_{g}
associated with the surface
C_{g}
is holomorphic.

Therefore, the following useful identity holds true

\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{0}(P)\bigg)=-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}(\phi_{0})-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{i}(\phi_{0})

(3.58)

holds true whenever the differential
\phi_{0}
is holomorphic near
\infty^{i}
and
\phi
is among the differentials listed in (
3.23
).

Now, in order to prove the second equality in (
3.56
), we distinguish five cases depending on the five types of operations
t^{i,\alpha},\ v^{i},\ s^{i},\ \rho^{k},\ u^{k}
.

First case:
t^{A}=u^{k}
.
The equality
E.u^{k}(\phi_{t^{B}})=(1+d_{B})u^{k}(\phi_{t^{B}})
is a particular case of formula (
8.12
) established in Appendix 2.

Second case:
t^{A}=\rho^{k}
.
Assume that
\phi\in\big\{\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}},\phi_{v^{i}},\phi_{s^{i}},\phi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k}\big\}
, i.e.
\phi\neq\phi_{u^{k}}
for all
k=1,\dots,g
. Using the residue theorem and applying (
3.58
) to
\phi_{0}=\phi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k}
(holomorphic differential), we deduce that

\displaystyle E.\rho^{k}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\omega_{k}(P)\bigg)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\omega_{k}(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}\big(\omega_{k}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{i}\big(\omega_{k}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}\rho^{k}\big(\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}\rho^{k}\big(\phi_{v^{i}}\big).

When
\phi=\phi_{u^{k}}
, then the symmetry property (
3.36
) and the first case imply that

E.\rho^{k}(\phi_{u^{k}})=E.u^{k}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})=u^{k}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})=\rho^{k}(\phi_{u^{k}}).

Thus the desired formula (
3.56
) is valid in this case.

Third case:
t^{A}=v^{j}
.
By (
3.36
), we know that
v^{j}(\phi_{u^{k}})=u^{k}(\phi_{v^{j}})
and
v^{j}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})=\rho^{k}(\phi_{v^{j}})
for all
k=1,\dots,g
.
Hence the action of the vector field
E
on the functions
v^{j}(\phi_{u^{k}})
and
v^{j}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})
is studied in the first and second cases. Thus, it suffices to consider the cases
E.v^{j}(\phi)
, where
\phi\in\big\{\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}},\phi_{v^{i}},\phi_{s^{i}}\big\}
.

Given that the differential
\phi_{0}(P):=\phi_{v^{j}}(P)+d\lambda(P)
is holomorphic near
\infty^{j}
(by (
3.26
), we see that the left hand side of (
3.58
) applied to this
\phi_{0}
can be rewritten as

\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{v^{j}}(P)\bigg)+v^{j}(\phi)

while its right hand side becomes

\displaystyle-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{j,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}\Big(t^{j,\alpha}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)+t^{j,\alpha}\big(d\lambda(P)\big)\Big)-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{j}}}\Big(v^{j}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)+v^{j}\big(d\lambda(P)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{j,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}t^{j,\alpha}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{j}}}v^{j}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big).

This amounts to the following equality:

\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{v^{j}}(P)\bigg)=-v^{j}(\phi)-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{j,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}t^{j,\alpha}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)-\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{j}}}v^{j}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big).

This and the key identity (
3.58
) lead to:

\displaystyle E.v^{j}(\phi)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\underset{P_{k}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{v^{j}}(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{v^{j}}(P)\bigg)-\sum_{i=0,i\neq j}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{v^{j}}(P)\bigg)-\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{m}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{v^{j}}(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle=v^{j}(\phi)+\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{j,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}t^{j,\alpha}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)+\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{j}}}v^{j}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)+\sum_{i=0,i\neq j}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)+\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{m}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{i}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)

\displaystyle=v^{j}(\phi)+\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}v^{j}\big(\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{j}\big(\phi_{v^{i}}\big),

with the last equality being a direct consequence of the following particular cases of (
3.36
):

t^{i,\alpha}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)=v^{j}\big(\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\big),\quad\quad v^{i}\big(\phi_{v^{j}}\big)=v^{j}\big(\phi_{v^{i}}\big).

Forth case:
t^{A}=t^{j,\beta}
.
Assume that
\phi\in\big\{\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}},\phi_{s^{i}}\big\}
. Then by similar arguments we obtain

\displaystyle E.t^{j,\beta}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{t^{j,\beta}}(P)\bigg)-\sum_{i=0,i\neq j}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{t^{j,\beta}}(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{n_{j}+1}t^{j,\beta}(\phi)+\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{j,\beta}\big(\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\big).

Fifth case:
t^{A}=s^{j}
.
By symmetry, it suffices to treat the case
\phi=\phi_{s^{i}}=\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}
. The action of the vector field
E
on
s^{j}(\phi_{s^{i}})=s_{ij}
was already described in (
3.48
).

\square

4
Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold structures

4.1
Frobenius manifold structures on simple Hurwitz spaces

The goal of this subsection is to build some Frobenius manifold structures on simple Hurwitz spaces
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
induced by quasi-homogeneous differentials (in the sense of Definition
4.2
below) and construct the corresponding prepotentials via a new approach. As we will see below, Dubrovin’s primary differentials in (
3.23
) are all quasi-homogeneous differentials. Thus our construction includes semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold of Dubrovin as particular cases.

As pointed out in the introduction, the prepotentials of the obtained Frobenius manifold structures provide quasi-homogeneous solutions to the generalized WDVV equations (
1.6
).

4.1.1
Frobenius algebra structures

Let
\eta(\omega):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\big(\omega(P_{j})\big)^{2}(d\lambda_{j})^{2}
be the Darboux-Egoroff metric on the manifold

\mathcal{M}_{\omega}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by

\omega(P_{j})\neq 0,\quad\forall\ j=1,\dots,N.

Here, as above, the differential
\omega
is of the form
\omega(P)=\int_{\gamma}h(\lambda(Q))W(P,Q)
such that the contour
\gamma
and the function
h
satisfy respectively the conditions
C1)
and
C2)
in (
3.1
).

On each tangent space
T_{p}\mathcal{M}_{\omega}
, we define a commutative and associative multiplication by

\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\circ\partial_{\lambda_{j}}=\delta_{ij}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}.

(4.1)

Particularly, we easily see that the vector field
e=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
is the unit vector field of the multiplication (
4.1
).

For
x=\sum_{j}x_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
,
y=\sum_{j}y_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
and
z=\sum_{j}z_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\in T_{p}\mathcal{M}_{\omega}
, consider the
(0,3)
-tensor

\mathbf{c}_{\omega}(x,y,z):=\eta(\omega)(x\circ y,z)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}x_{j}y_{j}z_{j}\omega(P_{j})^{2}.

(4.2)

Since the tensor
\mathbf{c}_{\omega}(x,y,z)
is symmetric, the quadruplet
\big(T_{p}\mathcal{M}_{\omega},\circ,e,\eta(\omega)\big)
is a commutative and associative Frobenius algebra with unity (according to Definition
2.1
).

Note that formula (
3.43
) allows us to describe the Frobenius algebra structure of
T_{p}\mathcal{M}_{\omega}
by means of the new system of flat coordinates
\mathcal{S}(\omega)=\big\{t^{i,\alpha}(\omega),v^{i}(\omega),s^{i}(\omega),\rho^{k}(\omega),u^{k}(\omega)\big\}
of the metric
\eta(\omega)
. The upcoming obtained expressions (
4.3
)-(
4.5
) involve the
\eta(\omega)
-duality relations between flat coordinates that were introduced in (
3.40
) and (
3.41
):

a)

The multiplication “
\circ
” in (
4.1
) takes the following form in flat coordinates:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)}\circ\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}&=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})^{2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\\
&=\sum_{C}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{C}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}\bigg)\partial_{t^{C}(\omega)}.\end{split}

(4.3)

b)

The unit vector field
e=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
is represented by:

e=\sum_{A}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})\omega(P_{j})\bigg)\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)}.

(4.4)

c)

The symmetric 3-tensor
\mathbf{c}_{\omega}
defined by (
4.2
) has the form:

\mathbf{c}_{\omega}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{C}(\omega)}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{C^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}.

(4.5)

This can be rewritten as a residue:

\mathbf{c}_{\omega}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{C}(\omega)}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\underset{P_{j}}{\text{res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}(P)\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}(P)\phi_{t^{C^{\prime}}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)\omega(P)}.

Note that expressions (
4.3
) and (
4.5
) imply that

\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)}\circ\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)}=\textstyle\sum_{C}\mathbf{c}_{\omega}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{C^{\prime}}(\omega)}\big)\partial_{t^{C}(\omega)}

and thus the structure constants of the algebra multiplication “
\circ
” are

\mathbf{c}_{\omega}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{C^{\prime}}(\omega)}\big)=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\eta^{C\alpha}\mathbf{c}_{\omega}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega)},\partial_{t^{\alpha}(\omega)}\big),

with
\eta^{AB}=\delta_{B,A^{\prime}}
being the constant matrix (
3.37
).

4.1.2
Primary differentials

Definition 4.1

Let
\omega(P)=\int_{\gamma}h(\lambda(Q))W(Q,P)
be a fixed differential as in (
3.1
) and (
3.3
) and
\eta(\omega)
be the corresponding Darboux-Egoroff metric (
3.20
).

We shall call the differential
\omega
primary with respect to unit vector field
e=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
(
e
-primary) if

\nabla^{\eta(\omega)}.e=0,

where
\nabla^{\eta(\omega)}
is the Levi-Civita connection of the metric
\eta(\omega)
.

In other words,
e
-primary differentials are exactly those that ensure the flatness of the unit vector field
e
. In this case, the components
e^{A}(t)
of
e
in flat coordinates
\{t^{A}(\omega)\}
of the metric
\eta(\omega)
are constant functions.

Proposition 4.1

For all
j=1,\dots,N
, we have

\nabla^{\eta(\omega)}_{\partial_{\lambda_{j}}}.e=\frac{e.\omega(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}.

(4.6)

In particular, the following statements are equivalent:

1.

The differential
\omega
is primary with respect to
e
;

2.

For all
j=1,\dots,N
,

e.\omega(P_{j})=0;

(4.7)

3.

For all
j=1,\dots,N
,

\int_{\gamma}h^{\prime}(\lambda(Q))W(Q,P_{j})=0.

(4.8)

Proof:
Bearing in mind the Christoffel coefficients of the diagonal metric
\eta(\omega)
given by (
3.6
) and the form
e=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
of the unit vector field
e
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{\eta(\omega)}_{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}e

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\eta(\omega)}_{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Big)=\sum_{j}\sum_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}(\eta)\partial_{\lambda_{k}}=\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}(\eta)\partial_{\lambda_{k}}+\sum_{k}\Gamma_{ii}^{k}(\eta)\partial_{\lambda_{k}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{j\neq i}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}(\eta)\Big)\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\sum_{j\neq i}\Gamma_{ij}^{j}(\eta)\partial_{\lambda_{j}}+\sum_{k\neq i}\Gamma_{ii}^{k}(\eta)\partial_{\lambda_{k}}+\Gamma_{ii}^{i}(\eta)\partial_{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega(P_{i})}\Big(\sum_{j\neq i}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\omega(P_{i})\Big)\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e.\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}.

This proves (
4.6
).

The equivalence between the three assertions follows immediately from (
4.6
) and the action (
3.8
) of the vector field
e
on the functions
\omega(P_{i})
.

\square

As a corollary, the characterization (
4.8
) and the properties of the symmetric bidifferential yield the following.

Corollary 4.1

Consider the
N
differentials
\phi_{t^{A}}\in\Big\{\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}},\phi_{v^{i}},\phi_{s^{i}},\phi_{\rho^{k}},\phi_{u^{k}}\Big\}
associated with the
\eta(\omega)
-flat coordinates and given by (
3.23
), with
N=2g+2m+\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}n_{i}
.

Then all the
N
differentials
\phi_{t^{A}}
are primary with respect to the unit vector field
e=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
.

These primary differentials coincide (up to multiplicative constants) with the Dubrovin primary differentials
[
14
,
41
]
.

Proposition 4.2

Assume that
\omega_{0}:=\phi_{t^{A_{0}}}
is a fixed primary differential belonging to the family (
3.23
).

Then the unit vector field
e=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
of the Frobenius algebra associated with
\omega_{0}
reduces to

e=\partial_{t^{A_{0}^{\prime}}(\omega_{0})}.

(4.9)

Proof:
In view of (
4.4
), (
3.17
) with
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0,1)
and (
3.40
), we obtain

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=\sum_{B}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\phi_{t^{B}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{A_{0}}}(P_{j})\bigg)\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{B}\bigg(\eta^{*}(\omega_{0})\big(dt^{A_{0}}(\omega_{0}),dt^{B}(\omega_{0})\big)\bigg)\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{B}\delta_{B,A_{0}^{\prime}}\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}=\partial_{t^{A_{0}^{\prime}}(\omega_{0})}.

Alternatively, the result can be also checked by applying (
3.43
) to the partial derivative
\partial_{t^{A_{0}^{\prime}}(\omega_{0})}
and
\omega_{0}=\phi_{t^{A_{0}}}
.

\square

4.1.3
Euler vector field and quasi-homogeneous differentials

Definition 4.2

Let
\omega_{0}
be a differential as in (
3.1
) and (
3.3
). We shall call
\omega_{0}
quasi-homogenous of degree
d_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
if there is a nonzero constant
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\omega_{0}(P)=c_{0}\int_{\ell_{0}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{d_{0}}W(P,Q).

(4.10)

More generally, a differential is called quasi-homogeneous of degree
d_{0}
if it can be written as linear combination of differentials of the form
\int_{\ell_{0}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{d_{0}}W(P,Q)
.

From their integral representation (
3.24
) with respect to the kernel
W(P,Q)
, primary differentials given in (
3.23
) are quasi-homogeneous and their degrees satisfy (
4.12
). Note that duality relation (
4.12
) will be important for our construction of prepotentials associated with a quasi-homogeneous differential (see next subsection).

Proposition 4.3

The
N
primary differentials listed in (
3.23
) are all quasi-homogeneous with degrees

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&{\rm{deg}}\left(\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\right)=\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1},&\hskip 28.45274pti=0,\dots,m,&\hskip 28.45274pt\alpha=1,\dots,n_{i};\\
&{\rm{deg}}\left(\phi_{v^{i}}\right)=deg\left(\phi_{u^{k}}\right)=1,&\hskip 28.45274pti=1,\dots,m,&\hskip 28.45274ptk=1,\dots,g;\\
&{\rm{deg}}\left(\phi_{s^{i}}\right)=deg\left(\phi_{\rho^{k}}\right)=0,&\hskip 28.45274pti=1,\dots,m,&\hskip 28.45274ptk=1,\dots,g.\end{array}

(4.11)

Furthermore, their degrees satisfy the following duality relation:

{\rm{deg}}\big(\phi_{t^{A}}\big)+{\rm{deg}}\big(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\big)=1,\quad\text{for all A}.

(4.12)

Here
t^{A^{\prime}}
is the
\eta
-dual flat coordinate of
t^{A}
in the sense of (
3.40
) and (
3.41
).

Proof:
Using (
3.41
) and (
4.11
), we see that relation (
4.12
) holds.

\square

We are going to consider the following infinite family of quasi-homogenous differentials, none of which is primary with respect the unit vector field
e
. Each quasi-homogenous differential can give a non-normalized (i.e. with non-flat unity) Frobenius manifold structure (see Definition
2.2
).

\bullet
For
i=0,\dots,m
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
,
\beta\geq 1
,

\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}(P):=\underset{\infty^{i}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(Q)^{1+\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}W(P,Q)

is an Abelian differential of the second kind with unique pole of order
n_{i}+\beta+2
at
\infty^{i}
. The differential
\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}
has the following behavior

\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\frac{n_{i}+\beta+1}{z_{i}(P)^{n_{i}+\beta+2}}dz_{i}(P)+\ \text{holomorphic},\quad\quad\lambda(P)=z_{i}(P)^{-n_{i}-1}.

Let us point out that a formula analogous to (
3.38
) and the residue theorem show that the action of the unit vector field
e=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
on the function
t^{A}\big(\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}\big)
is determined by

\displaystyle e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{N}\underset{P_{r}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=-\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{i}+\beta+1}{n_{i}+1}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\lambda(P)^{\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}\phi_{t^{A}}(P).

Here
t^{A}
is one of the operations
\big\{t^{j,\alpha},v^{j},s^{j},\rho^{k},u^{k}\big\}
introduced in (
3.21
) and
\phi_{t^{A}}
is the corresponding differential.

In particular

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Big(e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}\big)\Big)=\frac{n_{i}+\beta+1}{2(n_{i}+1)}\phi_{t^{i,\beta}}(P_{j})\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j}),&\hbox{if}\quad j=1,\dots,N;\\
\\
\displaystyle e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}\big)=2v^{i}(\phi_{t^{A}}),&\hbox{if}\quad\beta=n_{i}+1;\\
\\
\displaystyle e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}}\big)=\frac{\beta(n_{i}+\beta+1)}{(n_{i}+1)^{3/2}}t^{i,\beta}(\phi_{t^{A}}),&\hbox{if}\quad\beta=1,\dots,n_{i}.\end{array}

\bullet
Assume that the covering
(C_{g},\lambda)
belongs to the particular double Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{0})
, with
\mathbf{n}=(n_{0}+1,\dots,n_{m}+1)
. As indicated before, this is an open subset of the simple Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by the conditions
\lambda_{j}\neq 0
, for all
j=1,\dots,N
.
Let
\mathbf{z}^{0},\dots,\mathbf{z}^{L-1}
be the zeros of the meromorphic function
\lambda
. Then the following (normalized) Abelian differential of the third kind:

\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}}(P)=\int_{\mathbf{z}^{0}}^{\mathbf{z}^{j}}W(P,Q),\quad\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\infty^{0}}(P)=\int_{\mathbf{z}^{0}}^{\infty^{0}}W(P,Q),\quad j=1,\dots,L-1,

are quasi-homogeneous differentials of degree 0.

In this case, for all
t^{A}\in\big\{t^{j,\alpha},v^{j},s^{j},\rho^{k},u^{k}\big\}
we have

\displaystyle e.t^{A}\big(\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{N}\underset{P_{r}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=-\underset{\mathbf{z}^{j}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}-\underset{\mathbf{z}^{0}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=\phi_{t^{A}}(\mathbf{z}^{0})-\phi_{t^{A}}(\mathbf{z}^{j});

\displaystyle e.t^{A}\big(\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\infty^{0}}\big)

\displaystyle=-\underset{\mathbf{z}^{0}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\infty^{0}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}=\phi_{t^{A}}(\mathbf{z}^{0}),

where
\phi_{t^{A}}(\mathbf{z}^{j})
is the evaluation (
2.7
) of the differential
\phi_{t^{A}}
at the zero
\mathbf{z}^{j}
.

\bullet
Let
k=1,\dots,g
and
\sigma,\nu
be two positive integers. Then the following differentials

\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P):=\oint_{a_{k}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\sigma}W(P,Q)\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P):=\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}W(P,Q)

are quasi-homogeneous of degrees
\sigma
and
\nu
, respectively.

The differentials
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
and
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}
are multivalued and their jumps are studied in (
8.8
) (see Appendix 2 below). Furthermore, if
t^{A}
denotes one of the operations
t^{i,\alpha},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k},u^{k}
listed in (
3.21
)-(
3.22
), then the action of the unit vector field
e=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
on the functions
t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)
and
t^{A}\big(\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big)
is also described in Appendix 2 (formula (
8.10
)).

Let us introduce a notation for the set of the quasi-homogeneous differentials mentioned so far.

\mathrm{QH}(E):=\Big\{\text{$e$-primary differentials (\ref {Primary-e})}\Big\}\bigcup\Big\{\phi_{t^{i,n_{i}+\beta+1}},\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}},\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\infty^{0}},\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}},\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\Big\}.

(4.13)

The above quasi-homogeneity terminology is justified by the following property.

Proposition 4.4

Let
(C_{g},\lambda)
be a point in the simple Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
.
Let
D_{E}
be the vector field defined by:

D_{E}:=\lambda(P)\partial_{\lambda(P)}+\textstyle\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}=\lambda(P)\partial_{\lambda(P)}+E.

If
\omega_{0}
is a quasi-homogeneous differential of degree
d_{0}\geq 0
(
4.10
), then

D_{E}.\omega_{0}(P)=d_{0}\omega_{0}(P).

(4.14)

Proof:
Given a branched covering
(C_{g},\lambda)
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
consider the family of branched coverings
\big\{(C_{g},\lambda_{\varepsilon})\big\}_{\varepsilon}
defined by

\lambda_{\varepsilon}(P)=e^{\varepsilon}\lambda(P),

where
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{C}
and
|\varepsilon|
is sufficiently small.
This is nothing but the particular case of (
3.10
) where
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(1,0)
.

Under this transformation, the differential
\omega_{0}
changes as follows:

\displaystyle\omega_{0}^{\varepsilon}(P)=c_{0}\int_{\ell_{0}}\big(\lambda_{\varepsilon}(Q)\big)^{d_{0}}W(P,Q)=e^{d_{0}\varepsilon}\omega_{0}(P).

Therefore

D_{E}\omega_{0}(P)=\frac{d}{d{\varepsilon}}\omega_{0}^{\varepsilon}(P)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}=d_{0}\omega_{0}(P).

\square

Remark 4.1

Formula (
4.14
) allows us to consider quasi-homogeneous differentials of degree
d_{0}\geq 0
as the differentials belonging to the vector space

Ker\big(D_{E}-d_{0}\big)
.

For example, we can view a linear combination of holomorphic differentials as a quasi-homogeneous differential of degree 0.

Proposition 4.5

Let
\omega_{0}
be a quasi-homogeneous differential of degree
d_{0}\geq 0
belonging to the set
\mathrm{QH}(E)
(
4.13
)
and
t^{A}(\omega_{0})
be one of the
\eta(\omega_{0})
-flat coordinates (
3.21
)-(
3.22
).

Then the vector field
E:=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
acts on the function
t^{A}(\omega_{0})
as follows:

E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})=(d_{A}+d_{0})t^{A}(\omega_{0})+r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}},

(4.15)

where
d_{A}
is the quasi-homogeneous degree of the primary differential
\phi_{t^{A}}
(see relations (
4.10
) and (
4.11
)) and
r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}}
is the constant defined by

r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle r_{B,A}=r_{A,B}&\hbox{if}\quad\text{$\omega_{0}=\phi_{t^{B}}$ is one of the primary differentials (\ref {Primary-e})};\\
\\
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{s^{i}}},&\hbox{if}\quad\omega_{0}=\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\infty^{0}};\\
\\
0,&\hbox{}\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(4.16)

Here
r_{A,B}
are given by (
3.57
).

Moreover, in flat coordinates (
3.21
)-(
3.22
) of the metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
(
3.20
), the vector field
E
admits the following representation

E=\sum_{A}\Big((d_{A}+d_{0})t^{A}(\omega_{0})+r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}}\Big)\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}.

(4.17)

Proof:
As in Section 3.4, in order to calculate the action of the vector field
E
on the functions
t^{A}(\omega_{0})
, we shall use the following identity:

E.t^{A}(\omega_{0}):=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})=\sum_{j}\underset{P_{j}}{\text{res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\phi_{t^{A}}(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}.

Here the second equality follows directly from (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) where we take
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(1,0)
.

If
\omega_{0}=\phi_{t^{B}}
is among the
e
-primary differentials (
3.23
), then (
4.15
) is nothing but the second equality in (
3.56
).

In the case where
\omega_{0}
is one of the multivalued differentials
\big\{\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}},\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big\}
, we give a detailed calculation that proves (
4.15
) in Appendix 2 (formulas (
8.12
) and (
8.13
)). With the help of the residue theorem and identity (
3.58
), we can straightforwardly obtain the remaining cases.

Finally, we have

E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\bigg(\sum_{A}\Big(\partial_{\lambda_{j}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})\Big)\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\bigg)=\sum_{A}\Big(E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})\Big)\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}.

Thus representation (
4.17
) of the vector field
E
is an immediate consequence of (
4.15
).

\square

Proposition 4.6

Let
\omega_{0}(P):=c_{0}\int_{\ell_{0}}\left(\lambda(Q)\right)^{d_{0}}W(P,Q)
be a fixed quasi-homogeneous differential of degree
d_{0}\geq 0
.

Then the vector field
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llllll}&[E,e]=-e;\\
\\
&[E,x\circ y]-[E,x]\circ y-x\circ[E,y]=x\circ y;\\
\\
&E.\omega_{0}(P_{j})=(d_{0}-1/2)\omega_{0}(P_{j}),&\hbox{}\quad\quad j=1,\dots,N;\\
\\
&\big(Lie_{E}.\eta(\omega_{0})\big)\big(\partial_{\lambda_{j}},\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\big)=(2d_{0}-3)\eta(\omega_{0})\big(\partial_{\lambda_{j}},\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\big),&\hbox{}\quad\quad j=1,\dots,N.\end{array}

Proof:
By simple calculation, we can check that the first two relations hold true. The third one is a particular case of (
3.8
) where we take
h(\lambda)=c_{0}\lambda^{d_{0}}
and
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(1,0)
.

Finally, the action of the Lie derivative along the vector field
E
on the diagonal metric
\eta(\omega_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\omega_{0}(P_{j})^{2}(d\lambda_{j})^{2}
is as follows

\displaystyle\big(Lie_{E}.\eta(\omega_{0})\big)\big(\partial_{\lambda_{j}},\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\big)

\displaystyle:=E.\Big(\eta(\omega_{0})\big(\partial_{\lambda_{j}},\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\big)\Big)-2\eta(\omega_{0})\big([E,\partial_{\lambda_{j}}],\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(E.\omega_{0}(P_{j})\big)\omega_{0}(P_{j})+\omega_{0}(P_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle=(2d_{0}-3)\frac{\omega_{0}(P_{j})^{2}}{2}=(2d_{0}-3)\eta(\omega_{0})\big(\partial_{\lambda_{j}},\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\big).

\square

Summarizing the above results, Propositions
4.5
and
4.6
tell us that the vector field
E=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
is covariantly linear and obeys the conditions (
2.1
) and thus it is an Euler vector field.

4.1.4
A new formula for the prepotential

A prepotential of a Frobenius manifold structure
(\mathcal{M},\circ,\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})
is a function
\mathrm{F}
of flat coordinates
\{t^{A}\}_{A}
of the corresponding flat metric
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
such that its third derivatives are given by the symmetric 3-tensor
\mathrm{c}
from the Definition
2.2
of a Frobenius manifold:

\displaystyle\partial_{t^{A}}\partial_{t^{B}}\partial_{t^{C}}\mathrm{F}=\mathrm{c}\left(\partial_{t^{A}},\partial_{t^{B}},\partial_{t^{C}}\right)=\mathop{\langle\partial_{t^{A}}\circ\partial_{t^{B}},\partial_{t^{C}}\rangle}.

Let us start with the following crucial observation concerning the symmetric 3-tensor (
4.5
):

Proposition 4.7

Let
\omega_{0}
be a differential satisfying (
3.1
) and (
3.3
) and
\eta(\omega_{0})
be the corresponding Darboux-Egoroff metric (
3.20
). Then the symmetric 3-tensor
\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}
(
4.5
) associated with
\omega_{0}
enjoys the following formula:

\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big)=\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)=\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right).

(4.18)

Proof:
From (
3.43
) we know that

\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}=\sum_{j}\frac{\phi_{t^{C^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega_{0}(P_{j})}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}.

This relation, Rauch’s formula (
3.15
) and expression (
4.5
) for the tensor
\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}
imply that the desired result
(
4.18
) holds true.

\square

In view of (
4.18
), given that one can permute the order of differentiation, we see that the 4-tensor

\partial_{t^{D}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big)

is totally symmetric in the four components. As a consequence, according to the above results and Definitions
2.2
and
2.3
, we reach the following conclusions.

Corollary 4.2

Assume that
\omega_{0}
is a quasi-homogeneous differential (
4.10
) of degree
d_{0}\geq 0
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
be the open subset of the simple Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by the conditions:

\omega_{0}(P_{j})\neq 0,\quad\forall\ j=1,\dots,N.

Then the open set
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
carries a Frobenius manifold structure given by the flat metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
(
3.20
), multiplication law (
4.1
) and Euler vector field (
4.17
).

If the differential
\omega_{0}
is additionally chosen among primary differentials (
3.23
) with respect to the unit vector field
e
