, then the above Frobenius manifold structure becomes normalized in the sense of Definition
2.3
.

The following theorem is the central result of this work. It establishes a new formula for prepotentials associated with the obtained Frobenius manifold structures that are induced by quasi-homogenous differentials and described in the above corollary.

Theorem 4.1

Let
\omega_{0}
be a given quasi-homogeneous differential (
4.10
) of degree
d_{0}\geq 0
satisfying (
3.3
). Let
\big(\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}},\eta(\omega_{0}),\circ,e,E\big)
be the Frobenius manifold structure from Corollary
4.2
. Let also

i)

E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})
be the affine function
E.t^{A}(\omega_{0}):=(d_{0}+d_{A})t^{A}(\omega_{0})+r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}}
, for all
A
(see (
4.15
));

ii)

t^{A^{\prime}}
denote the
\eta
-dual flat coordinate of
t^{A}
introduced in (
3.40
)-(
3.41
);

iii)

\big\{\phi_{t^{A}}\big\}_{A}
be the family of the
N
primary differentials (
3.23
) of Dubrovin;

iv)

r_{A,B}
be the constants given by (
3.57
).

Then the following function is a prepotential of the Frobenius manifold
\big(\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}},\eta(\omega_{0}),\circ,e,E\big)
:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}&=\frac{1}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{\left(E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})\right)\left(E.t^{B}(\omega_{0})\right)}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}})+t^{B^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}})\Big)\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\bigg(2\frac{E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}+t^{A}(\omega_{0})\bigg)r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0}).\end{split}

(4.19)

Moreover, the Hessian matrix of the function
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
does not depend on the chosen quasi-homogeneous differential
\omega_{0}
and its entries are given by

\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\right).

(4.20)

The proof of this theorem is a direct consequence of relation (
4.18
) combined with upcoming results
(
4.22
) and (
4.25
).

Proposition 4.8

Consider the
N
functions
\big\{\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}\big\}_{A}
, with

\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2\big(1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}\big)}\sum_{B}\bigg(\left(E.t^{B}(\omega_{0})\right)\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)-2r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0})\bigg).

(4.21)

Then the following statements hold:

1.

For any
B
, we have

\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)=\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{B,\omega_{0}}.

(4.22)

2.

The Euler vector field (
4.17
) acts on the function
\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}
as follows:

E.\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}=\left(1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}\right)\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}+\sum_{B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0}).

(4.23)

3.

The function
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
defined by (
4.19
) admits the following representation involving the functions
\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}
:

\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A}\big(E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})\big)\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}-\frac{1}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})t^{B}(\omega_{0}).

(4.24)

4.

The partial derivatives of
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
are:

\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}.

(4.25)

Proof:
Using the symmetry property of the tensor
\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}
following from expression
(
4.5
) together with the quasi-homogeneity property (
3.56
) of the functions
t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})
, i.e.
E.t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})=(d_{A}+d_{B})t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})+r_{A,B}
for the Euler vector field
E
, we deduce that

\displaystyle 2\big(1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}\big)\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=(d_{B}+d_{0})\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)-2r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}+\sum_{D}\left(E.t^{D}(\omega_{0})\right)\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{D^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{D^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=(d_{B}+d_{0})\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)-2r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}+\sum_{D}\left(E.t^{D}(\omega_{0})\right)\partial_{t^{D}(\omega_{0})}\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=(d_{B}+d_{0})\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)-2r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}+E.t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+E.t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\big(d_{B}+d_{0}+d_{B^{\prime}}+d_{A^{\prime}}\big)\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}\big)\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big),

where we have used the key duality formula
d_{B}+d_{B^{\prime}}=1
in the last line.

Now, by employing (
4.21
) and (
4.22
) and expression (
4.17
) for the Euler vector field, we directly arrive at (
4.23
). Furthermore, replacing the function
\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}
by its expression (
4.21
) in the left hand side of formula (
4.24
) and comparing the resulting with (
4.19
), we conclude that (
4.24
) holds.

Lastly, we are going to look at (
4.25
). Differentiating in (
4.24
) with respect to
t^{A}(\omega_{0})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{A}+d_{0}}{2(1+d_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{B}\big(E.t^{B}(\omega_{0})\big)\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{B,\omega_{0}}-\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{A}+d_{0}}{2(1+d_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{B}\big(E.t^{B}(\omega_{0})\big)\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}-\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{A}+d_{0}}{2(1+d_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}E.\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}-\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{A}+d_{A^{\prime}}+2d_{0}+1}{2(1+d_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}};

where we have used relation (
4.22
) in the second equality, (
4.17
) in the third equality, (
4.23
) in forth one and once again the duality relation
d_{A}+d_{A^{\prime}}=1
in the last equality. The proof of the proposition is now complete.

\square

Proof of Theorem
4.1
:
Differentiating three times the function
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
given by (
4.19
) and using successively (
4.25
), (
4.22
) and (
4.18
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\Big(t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+t^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big).

\square

Corollary 4.3

The prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
in (
4.19
) as well as its first, second and third partial derivatives are quasi-homogeneous functions, with respect to the Euler vector field
E
given by (
4.17
), in the sense of (
2.4
).

More precisely, for the quantities

\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}:=\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}},\quad\quad\mathbf{F}_{AB,\omega_{0}}:=\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}},\quad\quad\mathbf{F}_{ABC,\omega_{0}}:=\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}},

we have

\begin{split}&E.\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\textstyle 2(1+d_{0})\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{A,B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})t^{B}(\omega_{0});\\
&E.\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}=\textstyle\left(1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}\right)\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}+\sum_{B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0});\\
&E.\mathbf{F}_{AB,\omega_{0}}=\left(d_{A^{\prime}}+d_{B^{\prime}}\right)\mathbf{F}_{AB,\omega_{0}}+r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}};\\
&E.\mathbf{F}_{ABC,\omega_{0}}=\big(2-d_{0}-d_{A}-d_{B}-d_{C}\big)\mathbf{F}_{ABC,\omega_{0}}.\end{split}

(4.26)

Here
r_{A,B}
are the constants given by (
3.57
).

Proof:
In view of (
4.24
) and (
4.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle E.\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{A}\big(E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})\big)\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\sum_{A}\big(E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})\big)\mathbf{F}_{A,\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=2(1+d_{0})\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{A,B}r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})t^{B}(\omega_{0}).

The second relation in (
4.26
) is nothing but (
4.23
).
In addition, using (
4.20
) and (
3.56
), we see that the second partial derivatives
\mathbf{F}_{AB,\omega_{0}}
of
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}

are also quasi-homogeneous functions.

Now let us deal with the last equality in (
4.26
). By employing Theorem
4.1
, relation (
4.18
), the duality formula
d_{A}+d_{A^{\prime}}=1
as well as expression (
4.17
) for the Euler vector field, we can write

\displaystyle E.\mathbf{F}_{ABC,\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=E.\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big)=E.\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}\Big(E.t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)\Big)+[E,\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}].t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=(d_{A^{\prime}}+d_{B^{\prime}})\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)-(d_{0}+d_{C})\partial_{t^{C}(\omega_{0})}t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\big(2-d_{0}-d_{A}-d_{B}-d_{C}\big)\mathbf{F}_{ABC,\omega_{0}}.

\square

The aim of the upcoming result is to improve formula (
4.19
) by providing further precisions for some terms.

Proposition 4.9

The prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
in (
4.19
) can be written in the following form:

\begin{split}&\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{\big((d_{0}+d_{A})t^{A}(\omega_{0})+r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}}\big)\big((d_{0}+d_{B})t^{B}(\omega_{0})+r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{B}}}\big)}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}t^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}})\\
&\quad+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})-\frac{3}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\bigg)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}),\end{split}

(4.27)

where
v^{i}(\omega_{0})
is the function
v^{i}(\omega_{0}):=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}\ }\lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P)
.
Moreover, the first relation in (
4.26
) becomes

E.\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=2(1+d_{0})\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\bigg)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}).

(4.28)

Proof:
The proof of formula (
4.27
) is based on the symmetry property (
3.36
) of the functions
t^{A}(\phi_{t^{B}})
and the fact that the constants
r_{A,B}
, given by (
3.57
), are such that
r_{A,B}=0
whenever at least one of the operations
t^{A}
and
t^{B}
is not of type
s^{i}
. Here
t^{A}
,
t^{B}
and
s^{i}
are among the five types of operations given by (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) with
s^{i}:=p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}
.

Denote by
\widetilde{F}_{\omega_{0}}
(resp.
\widehat{F}_{\omega_{0}}
) the first (resp. second) sum in expression (
4.19
) for the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
. That is we can write
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\widetilde{F}_{\omega_{0}}-\widehat{F}_{\omega_{0}}
.

Let us first mention the following remarks:

1.

By (
3.41
) we have
\delta_{t^{A^{\prime}},s^{i}}=\delta_{t^{A},v^{i}}
(the Kronecker delta symbol).

2.

Let
d_{A}:={\rm{deg}}\big(\phi_{t^{A}}\big)
be the degree of the quasi-homogeneous (and primary) differential
\phi_{t^{A}}
defined by (
4.10
). According to (
4.11
), we have

\delta_{t^{A},v^{i}}d_{A^{\prime}}={\rm{deg}}\big(\phi_{{v^{i}}^{\prime}}\big)={\rm{deg}}\big(\phi_{s^{i}}\big)=0.

3.

v^{i}(\omega_{0})
is an eigenfunction of the Euler vector field
E
with
E.v^{i}(\omega_{0})=(d_{0}+1)v^{i}(\omega_{0})
.

Now, using these remarks as well as the symmetry relation (
3.36
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\omega_{0}}-\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{\left(E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})\right)\left(E.t^{B}(\omega_{0})\right)}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}t^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{\left(E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})\right)\left(E.t^{B}(\omega_{0})\right)}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}\bigg(\delta_{t^{A^{\prime}},s^{i}}\delta_{t^{B^{\prime}},s^{j}}(1-\delta_{ij})\log(-1)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\log(-1)}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big(1-\delta_{ij}\big)\frac{\left(E.v^{i}(\omega_{0})\right)\left(E.v^{j}(\omega_{0})\right)}{1+d_{0}+deg(\phi_{{v^{i}}^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big(1-\delta_{ij}\big)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}).

On the other hand, by employing once again the aforementioned remarks and the precise values of the constants
r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}
(
3.57
):

r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\delta_{t^{A^{\prime}},s^{i}}\delta_{t^{B^{\prime}},s^{j}}\Big(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\Big)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\delta_{t^{A},v^{i}}\delta_{t^{B},v^{j}}\Big(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\Big),

we arrive at

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\bigg(2\frac{E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}+t^{A}(\omega_{0})\bigg)r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}t^{B}(\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\bigg)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}).

Thus, we obtain (
4.27
). On the other hand, by using the first relation in (
4.26
) and the mentioned values of the constants
r_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}
, we conclude that the function
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
satisfies the quasi-homogeneity property (
4.28
).

\square

Remark 4.2

\bullet
According to Theorem
4.1
and relation (
4.28
), for each quasi-homogeneous differential
\omega_{0}
of degree
d_{0}\geq 0
, the function
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
(
4.19
) is a
(2+2d_{0})
-quasi-homogeneous solution to the generalized WDVV equations (
1.6
).

\bullet
By using (
4.27
) and omitting the quadratic terms, we deduce that

\mathcal{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{\big((d_{0}+d_{A})t^{A}(\omega_{0})+r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}}\big)\big((d_{0}+d_{B})t^{B}(\omega_{0})+r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{B}}}\big)}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}t^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}})

(4.29)

is also a prepotential of the described Frobenius manifold determined by the quasi-homogeneous differential
\omega_{0}
.

\bullet
In formulas (
4.19
) and (
4.27
), we have chosen to add quadratic terms in order to construct prepotentials that share the Hessian matrix (
4.20
) when they correspond to Frobenius manifold structures of the same family.

This property could be useful in various situation. We refer to Section 7 for some examples of applications.

\bullet
According to the symmetry relations (
3.36
) and the relation
\delta_{t^{A^{\prime}},s^{i}}=\delta_{t^{A},v^{i}}
, formula (
4.20
) can be rewritten as follows:

t^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}\right)=\partial_{t^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{t^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}-\frac{\log(-1)}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(1-\delta_{ij})\delta_{t^{A},v^{i}}\delta_{t^{B},v^{j}}.

(4.30)

\bullet
Assume that the simple Hurwitz space is of type
\mathcal{H}_{g,L}(n_{0})
, i.e. all the considered coverings
\lambda
have a unique pole
\infty^{0}

of order
n_{0}+1=L
(
L
is the number of sheets).

i)

The list of primary differentials (
3.23
) becomes
\big\{\phi_{t^{0,\alpha}},\phi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k},\phi_{u^{k}}\big\}
.

ii)

We only have three types of flat coordinates among those in (
3.21
)-(
3.22
) determined by the operations:

t^{0,\alpha},\quad\rho^{k},\quad u^{k},\quad\quad\alpha=1,\dots,n_{0},\ \ k=1,\dots,g.

iii)

The set
\mathrm{QH}(E)
(
4.13
) consisting of quasi-homogeneous differential reduces to

\Big\{\phi_{t^{0,\alpha}},\quad\phi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k},\quad\phi_{u^{k}},\quad\phi_{t^{0,n_{0}+\beta+1}},\quad\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}},\quad\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}},\quad\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\Big\}.

iv)

When
\omega_{0}
is among these quasi-homogeneous differentials, then, by (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), the flat functions
t^{A}(\omega_{0})
are eigenfunctions of the Euler vector field
E
with eigenvalues
d_{0}+d_{A}
.

As a consequence, formulas (
4.27
) and (
4.29
) coincide and reduce to the following

\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\mathcal{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{(d_{0}+d_{A})(d_{0}+d_{B})}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})t^{B}(\omega_{0})t^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}).

(4.31)

Note that the described situation in the last point is realized by the genus zero Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{0,n+1}(n)
of polynomial functions

\lambda(P)=z_{P}^{n+1}+a_{n}z_{P}^{n-1}+\dots+a_{1},

where
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
,
n\geq 3
, are complex numbers chosen in such a way the
n
zeros
\lambda^{\prime}(P)
are distinct.

\bullet
Assume that
\omega_{0}
is a linear combination of quasi-homogeneous differentials of the same degree
d_{0}\geq 0
such that the differential
\omega_{0}
fulfills the conditions in (
3.3
). According to Remark
4.1
,
\omega_{0}
is again a quasi-homogeneous differential of degree
d_{0}
. Let
t^{A}(\omega_{0})
be function from the set
\Big\{t^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0}),v^{i}(\omega_{0}),s^{i}(\omega_{0}),\rho^{k}(\omega_{0}),u^{k}(\omega_{0})\Big\}
defined by (
3.21
)-(
3.22
). Since the Euler vector field
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
acts on

t^{A}(\omega_{0})
in analogous way as in (
4.15
), it follows that the quasi-homogeneous differential
\omega_{0}
gives a Frobenius manifold structure.

As example, we can produce a quasi-homogeneous differential
\omega_{0}
of

- degree 0 by taking
\omega_{0}
as a linear combination of holomorphic differentials
\omega_{k}=\phi_{\rho^{k}}
and Abelian differentials of the third kind
\phi_{s^{i}}=\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}
;

-degree 1 by taking
\omega_{0}
in the vector space generating by the differentials
\phi_{v^{i}},\phi_{u^{k}}
and
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k}}(P):=\oint_{b_{k}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)
.

4.1.5
Prepotentials associated with holomorphic differentials: examples in high dimension

In this subsection we further investigate the quasi-homogeneous solutions (
4.19
)-(
4.27
) to the WDVV equations induced by normalized holomorphic differentials
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}
,
g\geq 1
.

Note that, by Corollary
4.1
and Proposition
4.11
, normalized holomorphic differentials are all primary with respect to the unit vector field
e=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
and quasi-homogeneous of degree 0.

Let us begin with the following result which gives the general explicit form of the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
(
4.19
)-(
4.27
) corresponding to the differential
\omega_{0}\in\big\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}\big\}
.

Theorem 4.2

Assume that
g\geq 1
and
\omega_{0}
is one of the normalized holomorphic differentials. Then the prepotential associated with
\omega_{0}
is given by:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{\alpha\beta}{(2n_{i}+2-\alpha)(n_{j}+1)}t^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0})t^{j,\beta}(\omega_{0})t^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(\phi_{t^{j,n_{j}+1-\beta}})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{2n_{i}+2-\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}\Big)t^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})t^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(\phi_{s^{j}})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{g}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{2n_{i}+2-\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}\Big)t^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0})u^{k}(\omega_{0})t^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})s^{i}(\phi_{s^{j}})+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{i}(\omega_{0})u^{k}(\omega_{0})s^{i}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{g}u^{k}(\omega_{0})u^{l}(\omega_{0})\rho^{k}(\phi_{\rho^{l}})\\
&\quad+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\bigg)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}).\end{split}

(4.32)

In addition, this prepotential satisfies

E_{\omega_{0}}.\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=2\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\Big(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\Big)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}),

where
E_{\omega_{0}}
is the following Euler vector field:

E_{\omega_{0}}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0})\partial_{t^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0})}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})\partial_{v^{i}(\omega_{0})}+\sum_{k=1}^{g}u^{k}(\omega_{0})\partial_{u^{k}(\omega_{0})}.

(4.33)

Proof:

Since the holomorphic differential
\omega_{0}
is of degree
d_{0}=0
, formulas (
3.56
) and (
4.15
) tell us that

E.t^{A}(\omega_{0})=d_{A}t^{A}(\omega_{0}),\quad\quad\text{for all $A$}.

Accordingly, formula (
4.27
) reads as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}-\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})+\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\bigg)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t^{A},t^{B}}\frac{d_{A}d_{B}}{2-d_{A}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})t^{B}(\omega_{0})t^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t^{A},t^{B}\in\{t^{i,\alpha},v^{i},u^{k}\}}\frac{d_{A}d_{B}}{2-d_{A}}t^{A}(\omega_{0})t^{B}(\omega_{0})t^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{t^{B^{\prime}}}),

where we have used the relation
d_{A^{\prime}}=1-d_{A}
in the first equality and in the second equality we used the fact that
d_{A}\neq 0
only when
t^{A}
is one of the three operations
t^{i,\alpha},v^{i},u^{k}
belonging to the list (
3.21
)-(
3.22
).

Thus we arrive at (
4.32
) by considering the degrees of the differentials
\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}
,
\phi_{v^{i}}
and
\phi_{u^{k}}
in (
4.11
) and duality relations (
3.41
).

On the other hand, expression (
4.33
) of the Euler vector field is nothing but the particular case of (
4.17
) where
d_{0}=0
and
r_{\omega_{0},\phi_{t^{A}}}=0
for all
A
. In addition, the stated quasi-homogeneity property of the prepotential (
4.32
) is an immediate consequence of (
4.28
).

\square

Remark 4.3

For the forth line of the prepotential (
4.32
), we mention the following:

\begin{split}&\rho^{k}(\phi_{\rho^{l}}):=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\oint_{b_{k}}\omega_{l}=\frac{\mathbb{B}_{kl}}{2{\rm{i}}\pi};\\
&s^{i}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})=s^{i}(\omega_{k}):=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\omega_{k};\\
&s^{i}(\phi_{s^{j}})=s_{ji}=\text{the functions given by (\ref {s-ij Formula})}.\end{split}

(4.34)

Now, by considering some specific cases of the combinatorial parameters
(g,L,n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
of the simple Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
, we shall discuss some consequences of formula (
4.32
).

Proposition 4.10

Assume that the Hurwitz space is
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,n+1}(n)
, i.e. the considered meromorphic functions have a unique pole
\infty^{0}
of order
n+1\geq 2
. Then the prepotential induced by a normalized holomorphic differential
\omega_{0}
is of the following form:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}&=\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha\beta}{(2n+2-\alpha)}t^{\alpha}(\omega_{0})t^{\beta}(\omega_{0})t^{n+1-\alpha}(\phi_{t^{n+1-\beta}})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{g}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{2n+2-\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{n+1}\Big)t^{\alpha}(\omega_{0})u^{k}(\omega_{0})t^{n+1-\alpha}(\phi_{\rho^{k}})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{k,l=1}^{g}u^{k}(\omega_{0})u^{l}(\omega_{0})\mathbb{B}_{kl}.\end{split}

(4.35)

Proof:
Formula (
4.35
) follows from (
4.32
) and (
4.34
).

\square

In the following corollary, we are going to rewrite (
4.35
) when
n=1
, i.e., the considered Hurwitz space is that of hyperelliptic curves

\Big\{(\mu,\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\quad\mu^{2}=\prod_{k=1}^{2g+1}(\lambda-\lambda_{i}),\quad\text{$\lambda_{i}\neq\lambda_{j}$ for $i\neq j$}\Big\}.

(4.36)

Corollary 4.4

Assume that the Hurwitz space is
\mathcal{H}_{g,2}(1)
. Then the prepotential in (
4.35
) reduces to:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}&=\frac{1}{12}\big(t(\omega_{0})\big)^{2}t(\phi_{t})+\frac{5}{12}t(\omega_{0})\sum_{k=1}^{g}u^{k}(\omega_{0})t(\omega_{k})+\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{k,l=1}^{g}u^{k}(\omega_{0})u^{l}(\omega_{0})\mathbb{B}_{kl}.\end{split}

(4.37)

Here
\phi_{t}(P)=\sqrt{2}W(P,\infty^{0})
is the Abelian differential of the second kind
and
t(\phi)
is the flat function
t(\phi):=\sqrt{2}\underset{\infty^{0}}{{\rm res}\ }\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\phi(P)=\sqrt{2}\phi(\infty^{0})
, with
\phi\in\big\{\phi_{t},\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}\big\}
.

As another corollary, we shall analyse formula (
4.35
) in the genus one case. For this, let us denote the chosen flat coordinates (
3.21
)-(
3.22
) associated with the unique normalized holomorphic differential
\omega_{0}
as follows:

\displaystyle t_{0}=\tau/(2{\rm{i}}\pi),\quad\text{with}\quad\Im(\tau)>0;

\displaystyle t_{\alpha}=t^{\alpha}(\omega_{0}):=\underset{\infty^{0}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n+1}}\omega_{0}(P),\quad\alpha=1,\dots,n;

\displaystyle t_{n+1}:=u(\omega_{0}):=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P).

With respect to this system of flat coordinates, the Dubrovin flat metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
(
3.20
) becomes anti-diagonal. Moreover, (
4.9
) yields that the unit vector
e=\sum_{j=1}^{n+2}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
is represented by
e=\partial_{t_{n+1}}
.

Corollary 4.5

With the above notation, the genus one case of the prepotential (
4.35
) is as follows:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}(t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{n+1})&=\frac{t_{0}}{2}(t_{n+1})^{2}+\frac{t_{n+1}}{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{2n+2-\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{n+1}\Big)t_{\alpha}t_{n+1-\alpha}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha\beta}{(2n+2-\alpha)}t_{\alpha}t_{\beta}t^{n+1-\alpha}(\phi_{t^{n+1-\beta}}).\end{split}

(4.38)

Note that the unknown functions
\big\{t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}}):\quad 1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq n\big\}
in (
4.38
) are
functions of
t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{n},t_{n+1}
such that

\partial_{t_{n+1}}t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}})=e.t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}})=\delta_{n+1,\alpha+\beta}.

Moreover, since the Euler vector field (
4.33
) is represented by
E=\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\frac{k}{n+1}\partial_{t_{k}}
, it follows that the function
t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}})
satisfies the following partial differential equation:

\sum_{k=1}^{n}k\frac{\partial}{\partial{t_{k}}}t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}})=(\alpha+\beta)t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}})-(n+1)\delta_{n+1,\alpha+\beta}.

On the other hand, according to (
3.36
), the functions
\big\{t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}})\big\}
satisfy:
t^{\alpha}(\phi_{t^{\beta}})=t^{\beta}(\phi_{t^{\alpha}})
.

Proposition 4.11

Assume that the simple Hurwitz space is of type
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,m+1}(0,\dots,0)
, that is the meromorphic functions have
m+1
simples poles
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}
, with
m\geq 1
.

Then the prepotential (
4.32
) associated with the holomorphic differential
\omega_{0}\in\big\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}\big\}
takes the following form:

\begin{split}&\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(v^{i}(\omega_{0})\big)^{2}\log\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{i}(\omega_{k})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big|\mathbb{B}\big)\bigg)\\
&\quad-\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(v^{i}(\omega_{0})\big)^{2}\bigg)\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{i}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{i}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)\Big)\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})\bigg)\log\left(\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{r}(\omega_{k})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big|\mathbb{B}\big)\right)\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})\log\left(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{i}(\omega_{1})-s^{j}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{i}(\omega_{g})-s^{j}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{i}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{i}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{j}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{j}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)}\right)\\
&\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{i}(\omega_{0})u^{k}(\omega_{0})s^{i}(\omega_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{g}u^{k}(\omega_{0})u^{l}(\omega_{0})\Big(\mathbb{B}_{kl}/(2{\rm{i}}\pi)\Big)\\
&\quad+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big(1+\delta_{ij}\big)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}).\end{split}

(4.39)

Here, as before,
\Theta_{\Delta}
is the Riemann theta function (
2.8
) corresponding to an odd and non-singular half integer characteristic
\Delta
.

Proof:
Due to (
4.32
), (
4.34
) and the fact that the poles of
\lambda
are all simple, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})s^{i}(\phi_{s^{j}})+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{i}(\omega_{0})u^{k}(\omega_{0})s^{i}(\omega_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{g}u^{k}(\omega_{0})u^{l}(\omega_{0})\Big(\mathbb{B}_{kl}/(2{\rm{i}}\pi)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0})-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big(1+\delta_{ij}\big)v^{i}(\omega_{0})v^{j}(\omega_{0}).

In order to establish the desired formula, it suffices to express the functions
s^{i}(\phi_{s^{j}})=s_{ji}
, obtained in (
3.32
), by means of flat coordinates
v^{i}(\omega_{k}),s^{i}(\omega_{k}),\mathbb{B}_{kl}
corresponding to the normalized holomorphic differentials
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}
.

Let us first mention that by notation (
2.7
) and the residue theorem, we can write

\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)\omega_{k}(P)=v^{i}(\omega_{k})

and

\omega_{k}(\infty^{0})=\underset{\infty^{0}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)\omega_{k}(P)=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)\omega_{k}(P)=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}v^{i}(\omega_{k}).

Therefore, by substituting this into formula (
3.32
), we get

\displaystyle s_{ii}

\displaystyle=\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{i})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\right)+\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\right)-2\log\left(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)\right)-\log(-1)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{i}(\omega_{k})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big|\mathbb{B}\big)\right)+\log\left(\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{r}(\omega_{k})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big|\mathbb{B}\big)\right)-2\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{i}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{i}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)\Big)

and when
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle s^{i}(\phi_{s^{j}})

\displaystyle=s_{ji}=\log\left(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})\big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{i})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(\infty^{j})-\mathcal{A}(\infty^{0})\big)}\right)+\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{g}\omega_{k}(\infty^{0})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big)\right)-\log(-1)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{i}(\omega_{1})-s^{j}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{i}(\omega_{g})-s^{j}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)}{\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{i}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{i}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)\Theta_{\Delta}\Big(s^{j}(\omega_{1}),\dots,s^{j}(\omega_{g})\big|\mathbb{B}\Big)}\right)+\log\left(\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{g}v^{r}(\omega_{k})\partial_{z_{k}}\Theta_{\Delta}\big(0\big|\mathbb{B}\big)\right).

This completes the proof of (
4.39
).

\square

Note that we have obtained the prepotential (
4.39
) by simple specialization of the general formula (
4.19
) (see also (
4.27
)) to the case of the Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,m+1}(0,\dots,0)
and the holomorphic primary differential
\omega_{0}
. Now we show how formula (
4.39
) leads directly to an explicit solution of the (non-generalized) WDVV equations if we specialize it to the genus one case. In other words, we obtain an explicit solution depending on
2m+2
variables without much calculation but simply by specializing the general formula to the case of the Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{1,m+1}(0,\dots,0)
and
a holomorphic differential
\omega_{0}
.

Consider the lattice
\mathbb{L}:=\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, with
\Im(\tau)>0
. Then the Riemann theta function
\Theta_{\Delta}(\cdot|\tau)
(
2.8
), corresponding to the odd and non-singular half integer characteristic
\Delta=[1/2,1/2]
, is closely related to the
\theta_{1}
-Jacobi function

\Theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(u|\tau):=i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}e^{{\rm{i}}\pi\tau(n+1/2)^{2}+2{\rm{i}}\pi(n+1/2)u}=-\theta_{1}(u|\theta),\quad\quad\Im(\tau)>0.

(4.40)

We refer to
[
8
]
(Section 13.20) and
[
2
,
6
,
43
]
for details on the Jacobi theta functions.

The dimension of the Frobenius manifold associated with the normalized holomorphic differential
\omega_{0}
is
N=2m+2
, with
m+1
being the common degree of the coverings belonging to the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1,m+1}(0,\dots,0)
.

Moreover, according to Theorem
3.4
, the following functions provide a system of flat coordinates of the flat metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
(
3.20
):

\begin{split}&t_{0}:=\tau/{2{\rm i}\pi}=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\oint_{b}\omega_{0};\\
&t_{k}:=s^{k}(\omega_{0})=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{k}}\omega_{0},\quad k=1,\dots,m;\\
&t_{2m+1-k}:=v^{k}(\omega_{0})=\underset{\infty^{k}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P),\quad k=1,\dots,m;\\
&t_{2m+1}:=u(\omega_{0})=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P).\end{split}

(4.41)

According to (
3.37
) and (
3.45
), the constant matrix of the metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
, with respect to flat coordinates (
4.41
), is anti-diagonal :

\eta_{\alpha\beta}=\delta_{2m+1,\alpha+\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\beta},\quad\alpha,\beta=0,\dots,2m+1.

Corollary 4.6

Assume that the Hurwitz space is
\mathcal{H}_{1,m+1}(0,\dots,0)
.
With the preceding notation, the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}(t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{2m+1})
induced by the normalized holomorphic
differential
\omega_{0}
is given by:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}&=\frac{(t_{2m+1})^{2}}{2}t_{0}+t_{2m+1}\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k}t_{2m+1-k}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\big(t_{2m+1-k}\big)^{2}\log(t_{2m+1-k})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{2m+1-k}\bigg)^{2}\log\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m}t_{2m+1-j}\bigg)-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\big(t_{2m+1-k}\big)^{2}\log\left(\frac{\theta_{1}\big(t_{k}\big|{2{\rm i}\pi}t_{0}\big)}{\theta_{1}^{\prime}\big(0\big|2{\rm i}\pi t_{0}\big)}\right)\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k=1,j\neq k}^{m}t_{2m+1-j}t_{2m+1-k}\log\bigg(\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime}\big(0\big|{2{\rm i}\pi}t_{0}\big)\theta_{1}\big(t_{j}-t_{k}\big|{2{\rm i}\pi}t_{0}\big)}{\theta_{1}\big(t_{j}\big|{2{\rm i}\pi}t_{0}\big)\theta_{1}\big(t_{k}\big|{2{\rm i}\pi}t_{0}\big)}\bigg)\\
&\quad+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{j,k=1,j\neq k}^{m}t_{2m+1-j}t_{2m+1-k}-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}(1+\delta_{jk})t_{2m+1-j}t_{2m+1-k}.\end{split}

(4.42)

In addition, the function
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
is quasi-homogeneous of degree
2
with respect to the Euler vector field
E_{\omega_{0}}=\sum_{k=0}^{m}t_{2n+1-k}\partial_{t_{2n+1-k}}
and

E_{\omega_{0}}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=2\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(t_{2n+1-k})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}t_{2n+1-k}\right)^{2}.

Proof:
Formula (
4.39
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(v^{i})^{2}\log\Big(v^{i}\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}^{\prime}\big(0\big|\tau\big)\Big)-\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{m}(v^{i})^{2}\bigg)\log\Big(\theta_{1}\big(s^{i}\big|\tau\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}v^{i}v^{j}\bigg)\log\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{m}v^{r}\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}^{\prime}\big(0\big|\tau\big)\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}v^{j}\log\bigg(\frac{\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\big(s^{i}-s^{j}\big|\tau\big)}{\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\big(s^{i}\big|\tau\big)\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\big(s^{j}\big|\tau\big)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+u\sum_{i=1}^{m}v^{i}s^{i}+\frac{(u)^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}v^{j}-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big(1+\delta_{ij}\big)v^{i}v^{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(v^{i})^{2}\log(v^{i})+\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{m}(v^{i})^{2}\bigg)\log\Big(\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}^{\prime}\big(0\big|2{\rm i}\pi t_{0}\big)\Big)-\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{m}(v^{i})^{2}\bigg)\log\Big(\theta_{1}\big(s^{i}\big|\tau\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}v^{i}v^{j}\bigg)\log\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{m}v^{r}\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}v^{j}\log\bigg(\frac{\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}^{\prime}\big(0\big|\tau\big)\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\big(s^{i}-s^{j}\big|\tau\big)}{\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\big(s^{i}\big|\tau\big)\theta_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\big(s^{j}\big|\tau\big)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+u\sum_{i=1}^{m}v^{i}s^{i}+\frac{(u)^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}v^{i}v^{j}-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big(1+\delta_{ij}\big)v^{i}v^{j}.

Thus we obtain (
4.42
) by using (
4.41
) and (
4.40
).

\square

Remark 4.4

When
m=1
, (
4.42
) becomes

\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{(t_{3})^{2}}{2}t_{0}+t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}+(t_{2})^{2}\log(t_{2})-(t_{2})^{2}\log\bigg(\frac{\theta_{1}(t_{1}|2\rm{i}\pi t_{0})}{\theta^{\prime}_{1}\big(0|2{\rm{i}}\pi t_{0}\big)}\bigg)-\frac{3}{2}(t_{2})^{2}.

(4.43)

Note that, up to the change of variables
(t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\longleftrightarrow(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})=(-t_{3},t_{2},t_{1},t_{0})
and up to the quadratic term
-\frac{3}{2}t_{2}^{2}
, our prepotential (
4.43
) coincides with the one appearing in
[
19
,
11
]
and obtained using a long computation based on Dubrovin’s bilinear pairing method. The mentioned change of variables does not affect the WDVV equations.

4.2
Almost Frobenius manifold structures on double Hurwitz spaces

In order to simplify formulas, summation over repeated
Greek indices
will be assumed throughout this subsection.

Let us start by the following definition of an almost Frobenius structure which is motivated by Definition 9 in
[
18
]
.

Definition 4.3

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a manifold of dimension N. An almost Frobenius manifold structure on
\mathcal{M}
of charge
D\neq 1
is the data of
\big(\mathcal{M},\ast,\mathbf{g},E\big)
such that each tangent space
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
is a Frobenius algebra varying smoothly or analytically over
\mathcal{M}
with the additional properties:

1.

the inner product
\mathbf{g}
is a flat metric on
\mathcal{M}
;

2.

in flat coordinates
\xi=(\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{N})
for the metric
\mathbf{g}
,

i)

the structure constants of the multiplication
\partial_{\xi^{i}}\ast\partial_{\xi^{j}}=\mathbf{c}_{\ast,ij}^{\alpha}(\xi)\partial_{\xi^{\alpha}}

can be locally represented in the form

\mathbf{c}_{\ast,ij}^{k}(\xi)=G^{k\alpha}\partial_{\xi^{\alpha}}\partial_{\xi^{i}}\partial_{\xi^{j}}F_{\ast}(\xi)

for some function
F_{\ast}
, with
G_{\alpha\beta}=\mathbf{g}\big(\partial_{\xi^{\alpha}},\partial_{\xi^{\beta}}\big)
and
\big(G^{\alpha\beta}\big)
being the inverse matrix of
\big(G_{\alpha\beta}\big)
.

ii)

the Euler vector field
E=\frac{(1-D)}{2}\sum_{j}\xi^{j}\partial_{\xi^{j}}
is the unity of the Frobenius algebra;

iii)

the function
F_{\ast}
satisfies the homogeneity property:

E.F_{\ast}=(1-D)F_{\ast}+2G_{\alpha\beta}\xi^{\alpha}\xi^{\beta}.

4.2.1
Frobenius algebras associated with intersection forms

Consider the intersection form
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\frac{\omega_{0}(P_{j})^{2}}{\lambda_{j}}(d\lambda_{j})^{2}
(
3.46
) induced by the differential
\omega_{0}
satisfying (
3.1
) and (
3.3
). From Appendix 1, we know that
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
is a flat metric defined on the open subset
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
of the double Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{m})
determined by the conditions

\omega_{0}(P_{j})\neq 0,\quad\forall\ j=1,\dots,N.

We have a commutative and associative algebra structure on the tangent spaces of the complex manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
defined by the multiplication
``\ast"
:

\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\ast\partial_{\lambda_{j}}=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{\lambda_{j}}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}.

(4.44)

Moreover, the multiplication (
4.44
) admits the vector field
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
as the unit vector field.

For
x=\sum_{j}x_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
,
y=\sum_{j}y_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
and
z=\sum_{j}z_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\in T_{p}\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
, consider the
(0,3)
-tensor

\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}(x,y,z):=\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})(x\ast y,z)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}x_{j}y_{j}z_{j}\frac{\omega_{0}(P_{j})^{2}}{\lambda_{j}^{2}}.

(4.45)

In particular, the multiplication
``\ast"
is compatible with the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
and thus, according to Definition
2.1
,
the data
\big(\ast,E,\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})\big)
gives rise to a Frobenius algebra structure on tangent spaces of
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
.

In the following result, we redescribe such Frobenius algebras structure on tangent spaces by using the already chosen system (
3.47
) of flat coordinates
\{u^{A}(\omega_{0})\}
of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
. The proof is an immediate consequence of formulas (
3.55
), (
4.44
) and (
4.45
).

Proposition 4.12

Let
\big(G_{AB}\big)
be the constant matrix (
3.54
) of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
(
3.46
) with respect to flat coordinates (
3.47
). The following assertions hold:

a)

In
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
-flat coordinates (
3.47
), the algebra multiplication “
\ast
” (
4.44
) is given by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}\ast\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}&=\sum_{j}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\omega_{0}(P_{j})^{2}}\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\phi_{u^{\alpha}}(P_{j})\textsf{G}_{B\beta}\phi_{u^{\beta}}(P_{j})\bigg)\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\\
&=\sum_{C}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j}\phi_{u^{C}}(P_{j})}{\omega_{0}(P_{j})}\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\phi_{u^{\alpha}}(P_{j})\textsf{G}_{B\beta}\phi_{u^{\beta}}(P_{j})\bigg)\bigg)\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}.\end{split}

(4.46)

b)

In flat coordinates
\{u^{A}(\omega_{0})\}_{A}
(
3.47
) of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
, the unit vector field
E
takes the following form:

E=\sum_{A}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\phi_{u^{A}}(P_{j})\omega_{0}(P_{j})\bigg)\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})};

(4.47)

c)

The symmetric 3-tensor
\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega}
(
4.45
):

\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\omega_{0}(P_{j})}\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\phi_{u^{\alpha}}(P_{j})\textsf{G}_{B\beta}\phi_{u^{\beta}}(P_{j})\textsf{G}_{C\sigma}\phi_{u^{\sigma}}(P_{j}).

(4.48)

d)

Structure constants of the multiplication “
\ast
” are such that:

\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}\ast\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}=\sum_{C}\Big(\textsf{G}^{C\theta}\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{\theta}(\omega_{0})}\big)\Big)\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}.

(4.49)

Definition 4.4

Let
\nabla^{\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})}
be the Levi-Civita connection of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
. The differential
\omega_{0}
is called primary with respect to unit vector field
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
if

\nabla^{\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})}E=0.

Proposition 4.13

Let
\omega_{0}(P):=\int_{\gamma}h(\lambda(Q))W(P,Q)
.

1.

The following statements are equivalent:

i)

The differential
\omega_{0}
is primary with respect to
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
;

ii)

For all
j=1,\dots,N
,

E.\omega_{0}(P_{j})=-\frac{\omega_{0}(P_{j})}{2};

iii)

For all
j=1,\dots,N
,

\int_{\gamma}\lambda(Q)h^{\prime}(\lambda(Q))W(Q,P_{j})=0.

(4.50)

2.

The
N
differentials
\psi_{u^{A}}
in (
3.50
) are all primary with respect to the vector field
E
.

3.

Assume that
\omega_{0}=\psi_{u^{A}}
is one of primary differentials (
3.50
). Then, with respect to flat coordinates of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
, the unit vector field
E
is represented by (sum over
\alpha
is assumed)

E=\textsf{G}^{A\alpha}\partial_{u^{\alpha}(\omega_{0})},

with
\textsf{G}^{A\alpha}:=\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega_{0})\big(du^{A}(\omega_{0}),du^{\alpha}(\omega_{0})\big)
being
the entries (
3.48
) of the intersection form
\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega_{0})
.

Proof:

1.
By using expressions (
3.6
) for Christoffel coefficients of the
intersection form
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0}):=\mathbf{ds^{2}_{1,0}}(\omega_{0})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})}_{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}E

\displaystyle=\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\sum_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}(\mathbf{g})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}=\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\sum_{j\neq i}\lambda_{j}\sum_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}(\mathbf{g})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}+\lambda_{i}\sum_{k}\Gamma_{ii}^{k}(\mathbf{g})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\Big(\sum_{j\neq i}\lambda_{j}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}(\mathbf{g})\Big)\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\sum_{j\neq i}\lambda_{j}\Gamma_{ij}^{j}(\mathbf{g})\partial_{\lambda_{j}}+\lambda_{i}\sum_{k\neq i}\Gamma_{ii}^{k}(\mathbf{g})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}+\lambda_{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{i}(\mathbf{g})\partial_{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\frac{1}{\omega(P_{i})}\Big(\sum_{j\neq i}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\omega(P_{i})\Big)\partial_{\lambda_{i}}+\lambda_{i}\Big(-\frac{1}{2\lambda_{i}}+\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}\Big)\partial_{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{E.\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}+\frac{1}{2}\Big)\partial_{\lambda_{i}}.

Therefore, this and the action (
3.8
) of the vector field
E
on the functions
\omega(P_{i})
show
the equivalence between the three assertions.

2.
Since the functions
h(\lambda)
defining the Abelian differentials
\psi_{s^{i}}=\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}
,
\psi_{y^{j}}=\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\mathbf{z}^{j}}
and
\psi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k}
are constants, they satisfy conditions (
4.50
). Furthermore, because of (
2.13
), these conditions are also fulfilled by the multivalued differentials
\psi_{q^{k}}
.

3.
Due to formula (
3.53
) we have

\textstyle E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}=\sum_{B}\Big(E.u^{B}(\omega_{0})\Big)\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}=\sum_{B}\Big(\mathbf{g}^{*}(\omega_{0})\big(du^{A}(\omega_{0}),du^{B}(\omega_{0})\big)\Big)\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}.

\square

Remark 4.5

The Abelian differential of the third kind
\omega_{0}(P)=\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{0}\infty^{0}}(P)=\int_{\mathbf{z}^{0}}^{\infty^{0}}W(P,Q)
is primary with respect to the vector field
E
, but it does not belong to the vector space generated by the differentials (
3.50
).

Moreover, in flat coordinates (
3.47
) of the metric
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
,
E
has the following form:

\displaystyle E=\sum_{A}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\underset{P_{k}}{\text{res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{u^{A}}(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\Big)\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}=\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\partial_{s^{i}(\omega_{0})}-\frac{1}{m_{0}+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{y^{i}(\omega_{0})}.

4.2.2
Prepotentials of almost Frobenius manifolds

Let
\omega_{0}
be a fixed quasi-homogeneous differential of degree
d_{0}\neq 0
with vanishing
a
-periods. Essentially,
\omega_{0}
will belong to the following family of Abelian differentials of the second kind:

\displaystyle\psi_{t^{i,\alpha}}(P):=\underset{\infty^{i}}{\rm res}\lambda(Q)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}W(P,Q),\quad\quad\quad\text{$i=1,\dots,m$\quad and\quad$\alpha\in\mathbb{N}$, $\alpha\geq 1$};

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}_{t^{j,\beta}}(P):=\underset{\mathbf{z}^{j}}{\rm res}\lambda(Q)^{-\frac{\beta}{m_{j}+1}}W(P,Q),\quad\quad\text{$j=1,\dots,n$\quad and\quad$\beta\in\mathbb{N}$, $\beta\geq 1$};

or the following family of multivalued differentials (studied in Appendix 2):

\displaystyle\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P):=\oint_{a_{k}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\sigma}W(P,Q),\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{$k=1,\dots,g$\quad and\quad$\sigma\in\mathbb{N}$, $\sigma\geq 1$}.

Note that the degrees of these differentials are:

deg\big(\psi_{t^{i,\alpha}}\big)=\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1},\quad\quad deg\big(\widetilde{\psi}_{t^{j,\beta}}\big)=-\frac{\beta}{m_{j}+1},\quad\quad deg\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)=\sigma.

Proposition 4.14

Let
\omega_{0}\in\big\{\psi_{t^{i,\alpha}},\widetilde{\psi}_{t^{j,\beta}},\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big\}
and
\big\{u^{A}(\omega_{0})\}_{A}
be the corresponding system of flat coordinates (
3.47
) and
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
be the unit vector field. Then

\begin{split}&E.u^{A}(\omega_{0})=d_{0}u^{A}(\omega_{0}),\quad\text{for all $A$};\\
&E=d_{0}\sum_{A}u^{A}(\omega_{0})\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}.\end{split}

(4.51)

Proof:
The second formula in (
4.51
) follows from the first one. In order to prove that
u^{A}(\omega_{0})
is an eigenfunction of the vector field
E
, we shall use formula (
3.53
) where we replace
\psi_{u^{B}}
by
\omega_{0}
.
Assume first that
\omega_{0}=\psi_{t^{i_{0},\alpha}}(P)
. Similarly to
(
3.25
), we have

\psi_{t^{i_{0},\alpha}}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i_{0}}}{=}\frac{\alpha}{z_{i_{0}}(P)^{\alpha+1}}dz_{i_{0}}(P)+\text{holomorphic},\quad\quad\lambda(P)=z_{i_{0}}(P)^{-n_{i_{0}}-1}.

When
\psi_{u^{A}}
is among the
E
-primary differentials
\psi_{s^{i}},\psi_{y^{j}},\psi_{\rho^{k}}
(see (
3.50
)), the function
\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{u^{A}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}
is holomorphic near the point
\infty^{i_{0}}
. Therefore by writing its local behavior near
\infty^{i_{0}}
and using notation (
2.7
) we deduce that

\displaystyle E.u^{A}(\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\underset{P_{k}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{u^{A}}(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}=-\underset{\infty^{i_{0}}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{u^{A}}(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{(n_{i_{0}}+1)(\alpha-1)!}\psi_{u^{A}}^{(\alpha-1)}(\infty^{i_{0}})=\frac{\alpha}{n_{i_{0}}+1}\underset{\infty^{i_{0}}}{{\rm res}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i_{0}}+1}}\psi_{u^{A}}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{n_{i_{0}}+1}u^{A}(\omega_{0})\quad\quad(\text{by changing the order of integration}).

When
\psi_{u^{A}}
is among the multivalued differentials
\psi_{q^{k}}(P):=\oint_{a_{k}}\log(\lambda(Q))W(P,Q)
, the restriction of function
\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{q^{k}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}
to a chosen fundamental polygon
F_{g}
is also holomorphic near the point
\infty^{i_{0}}
. Accordingly, in view of the jumps (
3.51
) of
\psi_{q^{k}}
and the residue theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle E.q^{k}(\omega_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\underset{P_{j}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{q^{k}}(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{\partial{F_{g}}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\psi_{q^{k}}(P)\bigg)-\underset{\infty^{i_{0}}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{q^{k}}(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{a_{k}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\Big(\psi_{q^{k}}(P)-\psi_{q^{k}}(P+b_{k})\Big)\bigg)+\frac{\alpha}{(n_{i_{0}}+1)(\alpha-1)!}\psi_{q^{k}}^{(\alpha-1)}(\infty^{i_{0}})

\displaystyle=\oint_{a_{k}}\omega_{0}(P)+\frac{\alpha}{n_{i_{0}}+1}\underset{\infty^{i_{0}}}{{\rm res}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i_{0}}+1}}\psi_{q^{k}}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{n_{i_{0}}+1}q^{k}(\omega_{0})\quad\quad(\text{by changing the order of integration}).

The case where
\omega_{0}=\widetilde{\psi}_{t^{j,\beta}}
can be done similarly. Let us now take
\omega_{0}=\phi_{u^{k_{0},\sigma}}
. Then the action of
E
on the functions

s^{i}(\omega_{0}),\rho^{k}(\omega_{0}),q^{j}(\omega_{0})
is included in formulas (
8.12
) and (
8.15
). Moreover, the arguments similar to those used in the preceeding case
E.q^{k}(\omega_{0})
allow us to arrive at the relation
E.y^{j}(\omega_{0})=\sigma y^{j}(\omega_{0})
.

\square

Theorem 4.3

Let
\omega_{0}\in\big\{\psi_{t^{i,\alpha}},\widetilde{\psi}_{t^{j,\beta}},\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big\}
be a quasi-homogeneous differential of degree
d_{0}\neq 0
.

1)

Consider the symmetric matrix
(H_{AB})
defined by

H_{AB}:=\frac{1}{2}\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\textsf{G}_{B\beta}\Big(u^{\alpha}\big(\psi_{u^{\beta}}\big)+u^{\beta}\big(\psi_{u^{\alpha}}\big)\Big).

(4.52)

Then the 3-tensor (
4.48
) can be expressed as:

\begin{split}\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}H_{AB}=\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big)=\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}H_{AC}.\end{split}

(4.53)

2)

Consider the function

\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{A,B}u^{A}(\omega_{0})u^{B}(\omega_{0})\Big(H_{AB}-\frac{3}{2d_{0}}\textsf{G}_{AB}\Big).

(4.54)

Then
\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}
satisfies the following properties:

i.

The third order partial derivatives of
\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}
are such that

\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}=\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big).

ii.

the function
\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}
is quasi-homogeneous w.r.t. the vector field
E
in (
4.51
) with

E.\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}=2d_{0}\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{A,B}\textsf{G}_{AB}u^{A}(\omega_{0})u^{B}(\omega_{0});

iii.

the matrix
(H_{AB})
in (
4.52
), which does not depend on
\omega_{0}
, is the Hessian matrix of
\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}
.

Proof:

1)
From (
3.55
) we know that:

\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}=\textstyle\sum_{j}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\omega_{0}(P_{j})}\textsf{G}_{C\sigma}\psi_{u^{\sigma}}(P_{j})\bigg)\partial_{\lambda_{j}}.

Using this, the Rauch formula and (
4.48
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}H_{AB}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\omega_{0}(P_{j})}\textsf{G}_{C\sigma}\psi_{u^{\sigma}}(P_{j})\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\textsf{G}_{B\beta}\psi_{u^{\alpha}}(P_{j})\psi_{u^{\beta}}(P_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big),

which gives (
4.53
).

2)
Using the first equality in (
3.53
), we deduce that the action of the vector field
E
on the functions
H_{AB}
is determined by

\displaystyle E.H_{AB}=\frac{1}{2}\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\textsf{G}_{B\beta}\Big(E.u^{\alpha}\big(\psi_{u^{\beta}}\big)+E.u^{\alpha}\big(\psi_{u^{\beta}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\textsf{G}_{A\alpha}\textsf{G}_{B\beta}G^{\alpha\beta}=G_{AB}.

This and (
4.51
) lead to:

\displaystyle\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})\Big(H_{AC}-\frac{3}{2d_{0}}\textsf{G}_{AC}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})\left(\sum_{D}u^{D}(\omega_{0})\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}H_{CD}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})\Big(H_{AC}-\frac{3}{2d_{0}}\textsf{G}_{AC}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})\Big(\sum_{D}u^{D}(\omega_{0})\partial_{u^{D}(\omega_{0})}H_{AC}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})\Big(H_{AC}-\frac{3}{2d_{0}}\textsf{G}_{AC}\Big)+\frac{1}{2d_{0}}\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})E.H_{AC}

\displaystyle=\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})H_{AC}-\frac{1}{d_{0}}\sum_{C}\textsf{G}_{AC}u^{C}(\omega_{0}).

In particular

E.\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}=d_{0}\sum_{A}u^{A}(\omega_{0})\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}=2d_{0}\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{A,B}\textsf{G}_{AB}u^{A}(\omega_{0})u^{B}(\omega_{0}).

Moreover, like before we have

\displaystyle\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}

\displaystyle=H_{AB}+\sum_{C}u^{C}(\omega_{0})\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}H_{AC}-\frac{1}{d_{0}}\textsf{G}_{AB}

\displaystyle=H_{AB}+\frac{1}{d_{0}}E.H_{AB}-\frac{1}{d_{0}}\textsf{G}_{AB}

\displaystyle=H_{AB}.

Finally, by relation (
4.53
) we conclude that

\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}=\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}H_{AB}=\mathbf{c}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{u^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{u^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big).

\square

Theorem
4.3
and Definition
4.3
enable us to conclude the following.

Corollary 4.7

Let
\omega_{0}\in\big\{\psi_{t^{i,\alpha}},\widetilde{\psi}_{t^{j,\beta}},\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big\}
be a quasi-homogeneous differential of degree
d_{0}\neq 0
.
Then the open set
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{m})
carries an almost Frobenius structure determined by the intersection form
\mathbf{g}(\omega_{0})
(
3.46
), the multiplication
``\ast"
(
4.46
), the unit=Euler vector field
E
(
4.51
) and the prepotential

\mathbf{F}_{\ast,\omega_{0}}
(
4.54
).
The charge is given by
D=1-2d_{0}\neq 1
.

5
Prepotentials of deformed Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold structures

In this section, our goal is to extend formula (
4.19
)-(
4.27
) to quasi-homogeneous solutions associated with deformations of Frobenius manifold structures on the simple Hurwitz spaces
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
, with
g\geq 1
.
As already indicated in the introduction, the deformed Frobenius structures on
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
depending
g(g+1)/2
complex parameters were studied by Shramchenko in
[
42
]
.

In order to achieve our goal, we follow the pattern of the Sections 3.3 and 4.1 and use the results obtained there.

5.1
Deformations of Frobenius manifold structures on simple Hurwitz spaces

Let
\left\{(C_{g},\lambda),\{a_{k},b_{k}\}\right\}
be a point in
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
and
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}
be the basis of normalized holomorphic differential on the surface
C_{g}
, with
\oint_{a_{l}}\omega_{k}=\delta_{kl}
. Here, the cycles
a_{k},b_{k}
are assumed to start at a chosen marked point
P_{0}\in C_{g}
so that
\lambda(P_{0})=0
.

We will use repeatedly the following preliminary result about the Riemann matrix of
b
-periods
\mathbb{B}=(\mathbb{B}_{kl})
.

Lemma 5.1

We have

e.\mathbb{B}_{kl}=0=E.\mathbb{B}_{kl},\quad\quad\forall\ k,l=1,\dots,g,

(5.1)

where
e=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
and
E=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
.

Proof:
Using notation (
3.21
), we have
\mathbb{B}_{kl}={2{\rm i}\pi}\rho^{k}(\phi_{\rho^{l}})
. Thus (
5.1
) follows from (
3.38
) and (
3.56
) and the fact that normalized holomorphic differentials are quasi-homogeneous differentials of degree 0 (as we know from (
4.11
)).

\square

\bullet

Darboux-Egoroff metrics in terms of the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferentials

Let
\mathfrak{p}=\big(\mathfrak{p}_{kl}\big)
and
\mathfrak{q}=\big(\mathfrak{q}_{kl}\big)
be two
g\times g
matrices such that

i)

the matrix
\mathfrak{q}
is nondegenerate and the matrix
\mathbf{q}:=\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathfrak{p}=\big(\mathbf{q}_{kl}\big)
is symmetric;

ii)

the matrix
I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}
is nondegenerate (here
\mathbb{B}
is the Riemann matrix and
I
is the identity matrix);

iii)

the matrix
\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}
is symmetric;

iv)

the parameters
\mathbf{q}_{kl}
are constants with respect to the branch points
\{\lambda_{j}\}
of
(C_{g},\lambda)
and
\lambda
.

Note that item
i)
tells us that the following
2g\times 2g
matrix is symplectic:

\mathcal{S}:=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\right)^{T}&0\\
\mathfrak{p}&\mathfrak{q}\end{pmatrix},

(5.2)

that is
\mathcal{S}^{T}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{J}
, with
\mathcal{J}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}
.

Moreover, when the matrix
\mathbf{q}
is nondegenerate (and symmetric),
\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}=\big(\mathbb{B}+\mathbf{q}^{-1}\big)^{-1}
is symmetric.

By following
[
42
]
, let us consider the following
\mathbf{q}
-deformation of the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
(
2.11
):

\begin{split}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)&:=W(P,Q)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(\mathfrak{q}+\mathfrak{p}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathfrak{p}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\omega_{\alpha}(P)\omega_{\beta}(Q)\\
&=W(P,Q)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\omega_{\alpha}(P)\omega_{\beta}(Q),\end{split}

(5.3)

where
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}
are the normalized holomorphic differentials.

In formula (
5.3
) as well as during this subsection and the next one, summation over the repeated Greek indices
\alpha,\beta=1,\dots,g
is assumed
.

We shall call
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential. Expression (
5.3
) for the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential differs slightly from the one used in
[
42
]
where the symmetric matrix
\mathbf{q}
can be degenerate. From the properties of the symmetric bidifferential
W(P,Q)
(
2.11
), the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
(
5.3
) is also symmetric and has a pole of second order on the diagonal with biresidue 1. Moreover, for all
k=1,\dots,g
we have

\displaystyle\oint_{Q\in a_{k}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{k\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}(P);

\displaystyle\oint_{Q\in b_{k}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle={2{\rm i}\pi}\omega_{k}(P)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\mathbb{B}_{\beta{k}}\omega_{\alpha}(P)

\displaystyle={2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\Big)_{\alpha{k}}\omega_{\alpha}(P).

Thus the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
fulfills the normalization condition

\oint_{Q\in a_{k}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)+\mathbf{q}_{k\alpha}\oint_{Q\in b_{\alpha}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)=0,\quad\quad\forall\ k=1,\dots,g,

(5.4)

with
\mathbf{q}_{\alpha\beta}
being the coefficient of the matrix
\mathbf{q}
.

From its expression (
5.3
), we see that the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
turns into the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
provided that
\mathbf{q}
is the zero matrix.

Before delving more into the properties of the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential, let us emphasize that the deformation (
5.3
) is motivated by the following viewpoint. Assume that the symplectic matrix (
5.2
) belongs to
Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
. Then the cycles
\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}:=\big(\widetilde{a}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{a}_{g}\big)^{T}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}:=\big(\widetilde{b}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{b}_{g}\big)^{T}
given by

\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{a}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\big(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\big)^{T}&0\\
\mathfrak{p}&\mathfrak{q}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{a}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\text{with}\quad\mathbf{a}:=\big(a_{1},\dots,a_{g}\big)^{T},\quad\mathbf{b}:=\big(b_{1},\dots,b_{g}\big)^{T}

define a new canonical homology basis on the surface
C_{g}
.
Thus the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential (
5.3
) becomes normalized by

\displaystyle\oint_{Q\in\widetilde{a}_{k}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{p}_{k\alpha}\oint_{Q\in b_{\alpha}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)+\mathfrak{q}_{k\beta}\oint_{Q\in a_{\beta}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}_{k\beta}\left(\mathbf{q}_{\beta\alpha}\oint_{Q\in b_{\alpha}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)+\oint_{Q\in a_{\beta}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)\right)=0,

where we used the equality
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{q}\mathbf{q}
and (
5.4
) in the second line.

As consequence, the characterization (
2.12
)-(
2.13
) allows us to view
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
(
5.3
) as the canonical symmetric bidifferential with respect to the new canonical homology basis
\{\widetilde{a}_{k},\widetilde{b}_{k}\}
on
C_{g}
and the corresponding basis of normalized holomorphic differentials

\widetilde{\omega}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\omega}_{g}
, with

\big(\widetilde{\omega}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\omega}_{g}\big)^{T}=\big(\mathbb{B}\mathfrak{p}^{T}+\mathfrak{q}^{T}\big)^{-1}\big(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{g}\big)^{T}.

The following result was proven in
[
42
]
when the
\mathfrak{p}=I
and
\mathbf{q}=\mathfrak{q}^{-1}
is symmetric and nondegenerate. We include a proof for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition 5.1

The
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
satisfies the following variational Rauch formulas:

\partial_{\lambda_{j}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,P_{j})W_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{j},Q),

(5.5)

where
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,P_{j})
is the evaluation (
2.5
) of the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
at
Q=P_{j}
.

In particular, the functions

\kappa_{ij}(\mathbf{q}):=\frac{1}{2}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{i},P_{j}):=\frac{1}{2}\frac{W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)}{dx_{i}(P)dx_{j}(Q)},\quad\quad i\neq j,

solve the Darboux-Egoroff equations:

\begin{split}&\partial_{\lambda_{k}}\kappa_{ij}(\mathbf{q})=\kappa_{ik}(\mathbf{q})\kappa_{kj}(\mathbf{q}),\quad\quad\text{$i,j,k$ are distinct};\\
&e.\kappa_{ij}(\mathbf{q}):=\sum_{j}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\kappa_{ij}(\mathbf{q})=0.\end{split}

(5.6)

Proof:
Since
\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathbb{B}_{kl}={{\rm i}\pi}\omega_{k}(P_{j})\omega_{l}(P_{j})
(by Rauch’s formula (
2.15
)), it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{\alpha\sigma}\mathbf{q}_{\sigma\beta}\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{\alpha\mu}\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{\nu\sigma}\mathbf{q}_{\sigma\beta}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Big(\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\Big)_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle={{\rm i}\pi}\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{\alpha\mu}\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{\nu\sigma}\mathbf{q}_{\sigma\beta}\mathbf{q}_{\mu\epsilon}\omega_{\epsilon}(P_{j})\omega_{\nu}(P_{j})

\displaystyle={{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\epsilon}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\nu\beta}\omega_{\epsilon}(P_{j})\omega_{\nu}(P_{j})

where we have taken the sum over all the repeated Greek indices and denoted by
\Lambda^{ij}
the coefficient of a nondegenerate matrix
(\Lambda_{ij})
.
This relation, Rauch’s formulas (
2.15
) and expression (
5.3
) for the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential imply that

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{j}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}W(P,P_{j})W_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{j},Q)-{{\rm i}\pi}W(P_{j},Q)\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\omega_{\alpha}(P)\omega_{\beta}(P_{j})

\displaystyle\quad-2{{\rm i}\pi}\bigg({{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\epsilon}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\nu\beta}\omega_{\epsilon}(P_{j})\omega_{\nu}(P_{j})\bigg)\omega_{\alpha}(P)\omega_{\beta}(Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,P_{j})W_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{j},Q).

The first equation in (
5.6
) is a direct consequence of Rauch’s variational formulas (
5.5
). On the other hand, (
5.3
) and (
5.1
) imply that

e.W_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{i},P_{j})=e.W(P_{i},P_{j})-2{{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\Big(e.\big(\omega_{\alpha}(P_{i})\omega_{\beta}(P_{j})\big)\Big).

Thus the second equation in (
5.6
) follows from
e.W(P_{i},P_{j})=0=e.\omega_{k}(P_{j})
, by (
3.7
) and (
3.8
).

\square

The above result leads to a family of Darboux-Egoroff metrics with respect to the introduced
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential
W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)
(
5.3
), similarly to (
3.20
).

Corollary 5.1

Let
\omega_{\mathbf{q}}
be the
\mathbf{q}
-differential on
C_{g}
defined by

\omega_{\mathbf{q}}(P)=\int_{\gamma}h\big(\lambda(Q))W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q),

(5.7)

where the contour
\gamma
and the function
h
are as in (
3.1
). Then the following formula

\eta\big(\omega_{\mathbf{q}}\big):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(\omega_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{j})\big)^{2}(d\lambda_{j})^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\bigg(\underset{P_{j}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\big(\omega_{\mathbf{q}}(P)\big)^{2}}{d\lambda(P)}\bigg)(d\lambda_{j})^{2}.

(5.8)

defines a Darboux-Egoroff metric on the open subset
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{\mathbf{q}}}
of the Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by the conditions

det(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B})\neq 0\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\omega_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{j})\neq 0,\quad\quad j=1,\dots,N.

(5.9)

The rotation coefficients of the metric
\eta\big(\omega_{\mathbf{q}}\big)
are given by
\kappa_{ij}(\mathbf{q}):=W_{\mathbf{q}}(P_{i},P_{j})/2
.

\bullet

Normalized holomorphic
\mathbf{q}
-differentials

For
k=1,\dots,g
, let us denote by
\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}
the holomorphic
\mathbf{q}
-differentials defined by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}(P)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\oint_{b_{k}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)=\omega_{k}(P)-\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\mathbb{B}_{k\beta}\omega_{\alpha}(P).

Therefore

\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}(P)=\big(I+\mathbb{B}\mathbf{q}\big)^{k\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}(P).

(5.10)

The holomorphic
\mathbf{q}
-differentials
\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}
enjoy the following normalization property:

\oint_{a_{j}}\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}+\mathbf{q}_{j\alpha}\oint_{b_{\alpha}}\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}=\delta_{jk},\quad\quad j=1,\dots,g.

(5.11)

Let us now introduce the
\mathbf{q}
-Riemann matrix of
b
-periods of normalized holomorphic
\mathbf{q}
-differentials (
5.10
) by

(\mathbb{B}_{\mathbf{q}})_{kl}:=\oint_{b_{l}}\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}=\big(I+\mathbb{B}\mathbf{q}\big)^{k\alpha}\mathbb{B}_{\alpha{l}}.

(5.12)

Note that the normalization property (
5.11
) specifies the holomorphic
\mathbf{q}
-differentials
\omega_{\mathbf{q},k}
uniquely. This follows from the fact that a holomorphic differential
\phi
on
C_{g}
satisfying

\oint_{a_{j}}\phi+\mathbf{q}_{j\alpha}\oint_{b_{\alpha}}\phi=0,\quad\quad\forall\ j=1,\dots,g,

(5.13)

must vanish identically. Indeed, if we write
\phi=c_{1}\omega_{1}+\dots+c_{g}\omega_{g}
, then (
5.13
) implies that

\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)(c_{1},\dots,c_{g})^{T}=0

and thus we get
c_{1}=\dots=c_{g}=0
.

\bullet

Quasi-homogeneous
\mathbf{q}
-differentials and primary
\mathbf{q}
-differentials

We start with the following definition of quasi-homogeneous differentials with respect to the
\mathbf{q}
-bidifferential (
5.3
). It is similar to Definition
4.2
.

Definition 5.1

Let
d_{0}
be a nonnegative real number. A
\mathbf{q}
-differential
\omega_{\mathbf{q}}
is called quasi-homogeneous of degree
d_{0}
if
\omega_{\mathbf{q}}
can be written as a linear combination of
\mathbf{q}
-differentials of the following form:

\int_{\ell_{0}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{d_{0}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)

with
\ell_{0}
being a contour satisfying condition
C1
) in (
3.1
).

We are going to introduce the following family of
N
typical examples of quasi-homogeneous
\mathbf{q}
-differentials.
They can be regarded as the
\mathbf{q}
-analogue of the Dubrovin primary differentials (
3.23
). These
\mathbf{q}
-differentials and the corresponding Darboux-Egoroff metrics (
5.8
) turn out to the so-called
\mathbf{q}
-deformed semi-simple Frobenius manifold structures, studied in
[
42
]
.

i)
For
t^{A}\in\big\{t^{i,\epsilon},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k}\big\}
, with
t^{i,\epsilon}
,
v^{i}
,
s^{i}
,
\rho^{k}
being the operations defined by (
3.21
), consider the
\mathbf{q}
-differential

\begin{split}\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}(P)&:=c_{A}\int_{\ell_{A}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{d_{A}}W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)=\phi_{t^{A}}(P)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}t^{A}(\omega_{\beta})\omega_{\alpha}(P),\end{split}

(5.14)

where the triplet
(d_{A},c_{A},\ell_{A})
is determined by integral representation formulas (
3.23
) and (
3.24
) for the Abelian differential
\phi_{t^{A}}
.

In particular, the
\mathbf{q}
-differential
\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}
is a quasi-homogeneous
\mathbf{q}
-differential of degree
d_{A}
and has the same singularity structure as the primary differential
\phi_{t^{A}}
(
3.23
) described in Proposition
3.4
. Furthermore, because of the symmetry property
\oint_{b_{r}}\phi_{t^{A}}={2{\rm i}\pi}\rho^{r}(\phi_{t^{A}})=t^{A}(\omega_{r})
(
3.36
), differential
\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}
has the following property:

\oint_{a_{j}}\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}+\mathbf{q}_{j\alpha}\oint_{b_{\alpha}}\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}=\delta_{t^{A},\rho^{j}},\quad\quad j=1,\dots,g.

(5.15)

On the other hand, (
5.14
) implies that
\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}
coincides with the differential
\phi_{t^{A}}
provided that the matrix
\mathbf{q}
is zero.

ii)
For
k=1,\dots,g
, let
\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}
be the multivalued
\mathbf{q}
-differential defined by

\begin{split}\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}(P)&:=\oint_{a_{k}}\lambda(Q)W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)+\mathbf{q}_{k\sigma}\oint_{Q\in b_{\sigma}}\lambda(Q)W_{\mathbf{q}}(P,Q)\\
&=\phi_{u^{k}}(P)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}u^{k}(\omega_{\beta})\omega_{\alpha}(P)\\
&\quad+\mathbf{q}_{k\sigma}\Big(\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{\sigma}}(P)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}{\widetilde{u}}^{\sigma}(\omega_{\beta})\omega_{\alpha}(P)\Big),\end{split}

(5.16)

with
\phi_{u^{k}}
being the primary differential in (
3.23
),
u^{k}(\omega)
the function
u^{k}(\omega)=\oint_{a_{k}}\lambda(P)\omega(P)
and
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{r}}
and
\widetilde{u}^{r}
being respectively the differential and the corresponding operation defined by

\begin{split}&\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{r}}(P):=\oint_{b_{r}}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q);\\
&{\widetilde{u}}^{r}(\omega):=\oint_{b_{r}}\lambda(P)\omega(P),\end{split}\quad\quad r=1,\dots,g.

(5.17)

In expression (
5.16
), we have assumed the sum over the repeated indices
\alpha,\beta,\sigma
.

The multivalued differential
\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{r}}
is the particular case
\nu=1
of the differentials
\big\{\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{k,\nu}}\big\}_{k,\nu}
studied in Appendix 2.

According to the described jumps (
3.29
) and (
8.8
) of the differentials
\phi_{u^{k}}
and
\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{r}}
, we get

\begin{split}&\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}(P+a_{j})-\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}(P)={2{\rm i}\pi}\mathbf{q}_{jk}d\lambda(P);\\
&\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}(P+b_{j})-\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}(P)=-{2{\rm i}\pi}\delta_{jk}d\lambda(P).\end{split}

(5.18)

Moreover, using (
8.9
) and taking into account the fact that the cycles
a_{k},b_{k}
start at a marked point
P_{0}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=0
, we deduce that the multivalued differential
\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}
(
5.16
) satisfies the normalization property (
5.15
). Note that, the first equality in (
5.16
) and Definition
5.1
tell us that
\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}
is a quasi-homogeneous
\mathbf{q}
-differential of degree 1. In addition,
\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}
turns into the multivalued differential
\phi_{u^{k}}
defined in (
3.23
) when
\mathbf{q}=0
.

Proposition 5.2

Assume that
\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}
is among the list of
\mathbf{q}
-differentials

\Big\{\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{i,\epsilon}},\phi_{\mathbf{q},v^{i}},\phi_{\mathbf{q},s^{i}},\phi_{\mathbf{q},\rho^{k}},\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}\Big\}

(5.19)

defined by (
5.14
) and (
5.16
).

Then the
\mathbf{q}
-differential
\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}
is primary with respect to vector field
e=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
, where by this we mean that
e
is covariantly constant with respect to the Levi-Civita connection of the metric
\eta\big(\phi_{{\mathbf{q}},t^{A}}\big)
defined by (
5.8
).

Proof:
Let
\nabla
be the Levi-Civita connection of the metric
\eta\big(\phi_{{\mathbf{q}},t^{A}}\big)
. Analogously to (
4.7
), we can see that the flatness property
\nabla{e}=0
of the vector field
e
can be characterized by the N conditions

e.\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}(P_{j})=0,\quad\quad\forall\ j=1,\dots,N.

Suppose first that
t^{A}\in\big\{t^{i,\epsilon},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k}\big\}
. We have

\displaystyle e.\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}(P_{j})

\displaystyle=e.\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(e.\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\Big)t^{A}(\omega_{\beta})\omega_{\alpha}(P_{j})

\displaystyle\quad-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\Big(e.\big[t^{A}(\omega_{\beta})\omega_{\alpha}(P_{j})\big]\Big)

\displaystyle=0,

where we used expression (
5.14
) for
\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}
in the first equality and in the second equality, we used the following arguments:

1) relations
e.\mathbb{B}=0=e.t^{A}(\omega_{k})
: the first one was observed in (
5.1
) and the second follows from (
3.37
) and (
3.38
);

2) the differentials
\phi_{t^{i,\epsilon}}
,
\phi_{v^{i}}
,
\phi_{s^{i}}
and
\phi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k}
are
e
-primary (by Corollary
4.1
) and thus
e.\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})=0
.

Let us now consider the case
t^{A}=\xi^{k}
. We first observe that formula (
3.8
) applied to the differential
\omega=\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{r}}
given by (
5.17
)
and relation (
8.10
) imply respectively the following:

e.\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{\sigma}}(P_{j})={2{\rm i}\pi}\omega_{\sigma}(P_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad e.{\widetilde{u}}^{\sigma}(\omega_{\beta})=\mathbb{B}_{\beta\sigma}.

These relations, formula (
5.16
) and the arguments used in the previous case enable us to arrive at the following:

\displaystyle e.\phi_{\mathbf{q},\xi^{k}}(P_{j})

\displaystyle=e.\phi_{u^{k}}(P_{j})-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\big(e.u^{k}(\omega_{\beta})\big)\omega_{\alpha}(P_{j})

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{q}_{k\sigma}\bigg(e.\phi_{{\widetilde{u}}^{\sigma}}(P_{j})-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\big(e.{\widetilde{u}}^{\sigma}(\omega_{\beta})\big)\omega_{\alpha}(P_{j})\bigg)

\displaystyle=-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{k\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}(P_{j})+{2{\rm i}\pi}\mathbf{q}_{k\sigma}\bigg(\omega_{\sigma}(P_{j})-\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}\mathbb{B}_{\beta\sigma}\omega_{\alpha}(P_{j})\bigg)

\displaystyle=0.

\square

\bullet

Flat coordinates of the flat metric
\eta\big(\omega_{\mathbf{q}}\big)

Similarly to the link between (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), we consider the list of
N
operations

\big\{t^{i,\epsilon},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k},\xi^{k}\big\}
corresponding to the primary
\mathbf{q}
-differentials (
5.14
) and (
5.16
) where
t^{i,\epsilon},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k}
are the same as in (
3.21
) and
\xi^{k}
is the following operation:

\xi^{k}(\omega):=\oint_{a_{k}}\lambda(P)\omega(P)+\mathbf{q}_{k\alpha}\oint_{b_{\alpha}}\lambda(P)\omega(P)=u^{k}(\omega)+\mathbf{q}_{k\alpha}\widetilde{u}^{\alpha}(\omega).

(5.20)

Here
\omega
is a differential as in (
3.1
) or a
\mathbf{q}
-differential as in (
5.7
) and
u^{k}
and
\widetilde{u}^{\alpha}
are respectively the operations defined in (
3.22
) and (
5.17
).

i)
Assume that
t^{A},t^{B}\in\big\{t^{i,\epsilon},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k}\big\}
. Then, due to (
5.14
) and the symmetry property (
3.36
), we have

\begin{split}t^{A}\big(\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{B}}\big)&=t^{A}\big(\phi_{t^{B}}\big)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\Big(\big(I+\mathbf{q}\mathbb{B}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{q}\Big)_{\alpha\beta}t^{A}(\omega_{\alpha})t^{B}(\omega_{\beta})\\
&=t^{B}\big(\phi_{\mathbf{q},t^{A}}\big)+\log(-1)\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(1-\delta_{ij})\delta_{t^{A},s^{i}}\delta_{t^{B},s^{j}}.\end{split}

(5.21)

ii)
When
t^{A}\in\big\{t^{i,\epsilon},v^{i},s^{i},\rho^{k}\big\}
and
t^{B}=\xi^{k}
, then we have
