) and the quasi-periodicity (
7.8
) of the function zeta, we see that its
a
-period is zero.

\square

It is more convenient to work with the following explicit formulas for the four differentials
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{4}
. The results are obtained with the help of (
7.13
).

Proposition 7.1

1)

Normalized holomorphic differential:

\phi_{1}(P)=\frac{dz_{P}}{2\omega_{1}}.

(7.14)

2)

Abelian differential of the second kind:

\phi_{2}(P)=\sqrt{2}\Big(\wp(z_{P})+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)dz_{P}.

(7.15)

3)

Multivalued differential with jumps along the cycle
b
:

\phi_{3}(P)=\bigg(4\omega_{1}\wp^{2}(z_{P})-4\zeta(\omega_{1})\wp(z_{P})+2\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\big(\omega_{1}\zeta(z_{P})-\zeta(\omega_{1})z_{P}\big)-\frac{g_{2}}{2}\omega_{1}-2\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\bigg)dz_{P}.

(7.16)

Remark 7.1

In view of (
7.16
) and the Legendre identity (
7.9
), we can observe that the jumps of the differential
\phi_{3}
are such that:

\phi_{3}(P+a)-\phi_{3}(P)=0,\quad\quad\phi_{3}(P+b)-\phi_{3}(P)=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})dz_{P}.

On the other hand, introducing the (holomorphic) function

\mathcal{V}(u):=\omega_{1}\wp^{\prime}(u)+2\zeta(\omega_{1})\Big(\zeta(u)-\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}u\Big)+2\wp(u)\Big(\omega_{1}\zeta(u)-\zeta(\omega_{1})u\Big),\quad u\in\mathbb{C},

(7.17)

and employing the differential equation
2\wp^{\prime\prime}=12\wp^{2}-g_{2}
and expression (
7.16
) for the differential
\phi_{3}
we can write

\phi_{3}(P)=d\mathcal{V}(z_{P})=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(z_{P})dz_{P}.

(7.18)

Proof:

1)
Formula (
7.14
) can be easily checked using (
7.13
), (
7.5
), the quasi-periodicity property (
7.8
) of the
\zeta
-function and Legendre’s relation (
7.9
).

2)
In order to show (
7.15
), let us first claim that near the point
\infty^{0}
, the holomorphic differential
dz_{P}=2\omega_{1}\phi_{1}(P)
behaves as follows

dz_{P}\underset{P\sim\infty^{0}}{=}\Big(1+\frac{3c}{2}z_{\infty}(P)^{2}+\big(\frac{g_{2}}{8}+\frac{15}{8}c^{2}\big)z_{\infty}(P)^{4}+\dots\Big)dz_{\infty}(P),

(7.19)

where
z_{\infty}(P)
is the standard local parameter near
\infty^{0}
defined by
\lambda(P)=z_{\infty}(P)^{-2}
.

Indeed, since the principal part near the origin of
\wp^{\prime}(z)
is
-2z^{-3}
and
d\lambda(P)=\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})dz_{P}
, the holomorphic differential
dz_{P}
can be rewritten as

dz_{P}=\frac{d\lambda(P)}{\wp^{\prime}(z_{p})}=-\frac{d\lambda(P)}{2\sqrt{(\lambda(P)-\lambda_{1})(\lambda(P)-\lambda_{2})(\lambda(P)-\lambda_{3})}}.

(7.20)

In particular

dz_{P}\underset{P\sim\infty^{0}}{=}\frac{dz_{\infty}(P)}{f\big(z_{\infty}(P)\big)},

where
f
is the function

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\Big(1-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3})x^{2}+(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{3}\lambda_{1})x^{4}-(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3})x^{6}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\Big(1-3cx^{2}+\big(3c^{2}-\frac{g_{2}}{4}\big)x^{4}+\big(\frac{g_{3}}{4}-c\frac{g_{2}}{4}+c^{3}\big)x^{6}\Big)^{1/2}.

Hence the behavior of
f
near zero gives (
7.19
).

Now (
7.19
) and (
7.13
) yield that

W(P,Q)\underset{Q\sim\infty^{0}}{=}\Big(\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q})+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{3c}{2}z_{\infty}(Q)^{2}+\dots\Big)dz_{\infty}(Q)dz_{P}.

Accordingly, we obtain

\phi_{2}(P)=\sqrt{2}W(P,\infty^{0})=\sqrt{2}\frac{W(P,Q)}{dz_{\infty}(Q)}\Big|_{Q=\infty^{0}}=\sqrt{2}\Big(\wp(z_{P})+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)dz_{P}.

3)
Let us look at (
7.16
). We have

\phi_{3}(P)=\oint_{a}(\lambda(Q)-c)W(P,Q)=\left(\oint_{a}\wp(z_{Q})\wp(z_{Q}-z_{P})dz_{Q}\right)dz_{P}-2\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}dz_{P}.

Thus it suffices to establish that

\oint_{a}\wp(z_{Q})\wp(z_{Q}-z_{P})dz_{Q}=4\omega_{1}\wp^{2}(z_{P})-4\zeta(\omega_{1})\wp(z_{P})+2\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\big(\omega_{1}\zeta(z_{P})-\zeta(\omega_{1})z_{P}\big)-\frac{g_{2}}{2}\omega_{1}.

(7.21)

In order to show (
7.21
), let us claim that:

\Big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\Big)\wp(z_{Q}-z_{P})=2\wp^{2}(z_{P})+\wp(z_{P})\wp(z_{Q})+\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\Big(\zeta(z_{Q}-z_{P})-\zeta(z_{Q})+\zeta(z_{P})\Big)-\frac{g_{2}}{4}.

Indeed, we can write

\displaystyle\Big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\Big)\wp(z_{Q}-z_{P})

\displaystyle=\wp^{2}(z_{P})-\wp^{2}(z_{Q})+\frac{\wp^{\prime 2}(z_{P})+\wp^{\prime 2}(z_{Q})+2\wp^{\prime}(z_{Q})\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})}{4\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}

\displaystyle=\wp^{2}(z_{P})-\wp^{2}(z_{Q})+\frac{\wp^{\prime 2}(z_{P})}{4\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}+\frac{\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\wp^{\prime}(z_{Q})}{2\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}+\frac{4\wp^{3}(z_{Q})-g_{2}\wp(z_{Q})-g_{3}}{4\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}

\displaystyle=\wp^{2}(z_{P})-\wp^{2}(z_{Q})+\frac{\wp^{\prime 2}(z_{P})}{4\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}+\frac{\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\wp^{\prime}(z_{Q})}{2\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4\big(\wp^{3}(z_{Q})-\wp^{3}(z_{P})\big)-g_{2}\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)+4\wp^{3}(z_{P})-g_{2}\wp(z_{P})-g_{3}}{4\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}

\displaystyle=2\wp^{2}(z_{P})+\wp(z_{P})\wp(z_{Q})+\frac{\wp^{\prime 2}(z_{P})}{2\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}+\frac{\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\wp^{\prime}(z_{Q})}{2\big(\wp(z_{Q})-\wp(z_{P})\big)}-\frac{g_{2}}{4}

\displaystyle=2\wp^{2}(z_{P})+\wp(z_{P})\wp(z_{Q})+\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\Big(\zeta(z_{Q}-z_{P})-\zeta(z_{Q})+\zeta(z_{P})\Big)-\frac{g_{2}}{4},

where we have used

- the addition theorem (
7.10
) for the
\wp
-function in the first equality;

- the differential equation (
7.2
) in the second and forth equalities;

- the addition theorem (
7.11
) for the
\zeta
-function in the last one.

Finally, the claimed relation, the
\sigma
-quasi-periodicity property
\sigma(u+2\omega_{1})=-\sigma(u)e^{2\zeta(\omega_{1})u+2\omega_{1}\zeta(\omega_{1})}
and a straightforward calculation based on (
7.5
) and (
7.8
) enable us to arrive at (
7.21
).

\square

Our next goal is to calculate the
\phi_{j}
-Darboux-Egoroff metrics:

\eta(\phi_{j})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{3}\big(\phi_{j}(P_{k})\big)^{2}(d\lambda_{k})^{2},\quad j=1,2,3,

and the corresponding flat coordinates
\mathbf{t}^{A}(\phi_{j})
. Recall that
\phi_{j}(P_{k})
denotes the evaluation of the differential
\phi_{j}
at the ramification point
P_{k}
with respect to the local parameter
x_{k}(P)=\sqrt{\lambda(P)-\lambda_{k}}
(see (
2.5
)).

From (
3.21
) we know that the three flat coordinates of the flat metric
\eta(\phi_{j})
are chosen as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{j}):=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}\phi_{j}(P);\quad\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{j}):=\sqrt{2}{\rm res}_{\infty^{0}}\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\phi_{j}(P);\quad\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{j}):=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{j}(P).

Proposition 7.2

1)
The flat metric induced by the normalized holomorphic differential
\phi_{1}
is given by

\eta(\phi_{1})=\frac{(d\lambda_{1})^{2}}{8\omega_{1}^{2}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})}+\frac{(d\lambda_{2})^{2}}{8\omega_{1}^{2}(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}+\frac{(d\lambda_{3})^{2}}{8\omega_{1}^{2}(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1})(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})}.

The flat coordinates of
\eta(\phi_{1})
are

\begin{split}&t_{1}:=\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{1})=\frac{\omega_{2}}{2{\rm{i}}\pi\omega_{1}}=\frac{\tau}{2{\rm{i}}\pi};\\
&t_{2}:=\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}};\\
&t_{3}:=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{1})=-\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}+c.\end{split}

(7.22)

2)
The flat metric
\eta(\phi_{2})
is given by

\eta(\phi_{2})=\frac{(e_{1}\omega_{1}+\zeta(\omega_{1}))^{2}}{\omega_{1}^{2}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})}(d\lambda_{1})^{2}+\frac{(e_{2}\omega_{1}+\zeta(\omega_{1}))^{2}}{\omega_{1}^{2}(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}(d\lambda_{2})^{2}+\frac{(e_{3}\omega_{1}+\zeta(\omega_{1}))^{2}}{\omega_{1}^{2}(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1})(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})}(d\lambda_{3})^{2}.

The flat coordinates of
\eta(\phi_{2})
are

\begin{split}&x_{1}:=\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{2})=t_{2};\\
&x_{2}:=\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{2})=2\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}+c;\\
&x_{3}:=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{2})=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{6}g_{2}\omega_{1}-2\sqrt{2}\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}.\end{split}

(7.23)

3)
The flat metric
\eta(\phi_{3})
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\eta(\phi_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\big(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(\omega_{1})\big)^{2}}{2(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})}(d\lambda_{1})^{2}+\frac{\big(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(\omega_{2})\big)^{2}}{2(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}(d\lambda_{2})^{2}+\frac{\big(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(\omega_{3})\big)^{2}}{2(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1})(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})}(d\lambda_{3})^{2},

with
\mathcal{V}
being the function (
7.17
).
The corresponding flat coordinates are

\begin{split}&y_{1}:=\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{3})=c-\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}-\lambda(P_{0});\\
&y_{2}:=\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{3})=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{6}g_{2}\omega_{1}-2\sqrt{2}\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}=x_{3};\\
&y_{3}:=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{3})=g_{3}\omega_{1}^{2}-g_{2}\omega_{1}\zeta(\omega_{1})+4\frac{\zeta^{3}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}},\end{split}

(7.24)

where
P_{0}
is an arbitrary starting point of the contour
b
.

Proof:

1)

Since the standard local parameter near the simple ramification point
P_{1}
is
x_{1}(P)=\sqrt{\lambda(P)-\lambda_{1}}
, it follows that
d\lambda(P)=2x_{1}(P)dx_{1}(P)
. This, (
7.14
) and (
7.20
) yield that

\phi_{1}(P)\underset{P\sim P_{1}}{=}-\frac{dx_{1}(P)}{2\omega_{1}\sqrt{(\lambda(P)-\lambda_{2})(\lambda(P)-\lambda_{3})}}.

Therefore

\phi_{1}(P_{1}):=\frac{\phi_{1}(P)}{dx_{1}(P)}\Big|_{P=P_{1}}=-\frac{1}{2\omega_{1}\sqrt{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})}}.

In the same way we get
\phi_{1}(P_{2})
and
\phi_{1}(P_{3})
.

Using (
7.14
), (
7.19
) and (
7.5
) we obtain

\displaystyle t_{1}=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{2}}\frac{dz_{P}}{2\omega_{1}}=\frac{\omega_{2}}{2{\rm{i}}\pi\omega_{1}};

\displaystyle t_{2}=\sqrt{2}{\rm res}_{0}\frac{1}{z_{\infty}(P)}\Big(1+\frac{3c}{2}z^{2}_{\infty}(P)+\dots\Big)\frac{dz_{\infty}(P)}{2\omega_{1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}};

\displaystyle t_{3}=\frac{1}{2\omega_{1}}\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{1}}\Big(\wp(z_{P})+c\Big)dz_{p}=-\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}+c.

2)
From (
7.14
) and (
7.15
), we observe that
\phi_{2}
can be expressed in terms of the normalized holomorphic differential
\phi_{1}
as follows

\phi_{2}(P)=2\sqrt{2}\big(\omega_{1}\wp(z_{P})+\zeta(\omega_{1})\big)\phi_{1}(P).

Accordingly for
k=1,2,3
we have

\phi_{2}(P_{k})=2\sqrt{2}\big(\omega_{1}\wp(\omega_{k})+\zeta(\omega_{1})\big)\phi_{1}(P_{k})=2\sqrt{2}\big(\omega_{1}e_{k}+\zeta(\omega_{1})\big)\phi_{1}(P_{k})

which gives the stated formula for the metric
\eta(\phi_{2})
.

Furthermore, by (
7.15
), (
7.5
), (
7.19
) and Legendre’s relation (
7.9
) we get

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{2}}\Big(\wp(z_{P})+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)dz_{P}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\Big(-2\zeta(\omega_{2})+2\frac{\omega_{2}}{\omega_{1}}\zeta(\omega_{1})\Big)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}};

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=2\underset{\infty^{0}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\Big(\wp(z_{P})+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)dz_{P}\bigg)=2\underset{\infty^{0}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\Big(\lambda(P)-c+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)dz_{P}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2\underset{0}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\frac{1}{z_{\infty}(P)}\Big(\frac{1}{z^{2}_{\infty}(P)}-c+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{3c}{2}z^{2}_{\infty}(P)+o\left(z^{2}_{\infty}(P)\right)\Big)dz_{\infty}(P)\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=c+2\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}};

\displaystyle x_{3}

\displaystyle:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{2}(P)=\oint_{a}\big(\lambda(P)-c\big)\phi_{2}(P)=\sqrt{2}\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{1}}\Big(\wp^{2}(z_{P})+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\wp(z_{P})\Big)dz_{p}

\displaystyle=-2\sqrt{2}\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}+\sqrt{2}\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{1}}\Big(\frac{1}{6}\wp^{\prime\prime}(z_{P})+\frac{g_{2}}{12}\Big)dz_{P}=-2\sqrt{2}\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{6}g_{2}\omega_{1}.

3)
Because of (
7.18
) and (
7.14
), we can write
\phi_{3}(P)=2\omega_{1}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(z_{P})\phi_{1}(P)
. This implies that
\phi_{3}(P_{j})=2\omega_{1}\mathcal{V}(\omega_{j})\phi(P_{j})
and then we arrive at the desired expression of the metric
\eta(\phi_{3})
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}\phi_{3}(P)=\frac{2}{{\rm{i}}\pi}\zeta(\omega_{1})\Big(\zeta(\omega_{2})-\frac{\omega_{2}}{\omega_{1}}\zeta(\omega_{1})\Big)-\wp(x)=-\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}-\wp(x);

\displaystyle y_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{2}{\rm res}_{\infty^{0}}\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\phi_{3}(P)=x_{3};

\displaystyle y_{3}

\displaystyle:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{3}(P)=\oint_{a}\big(\lambda(P)-c\big)\phi_{3}(P)

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{1}}\bigg(4\omega_{1}\wp^{3}(z_{P})-4\zeta(\omega_{1})\wp^{2}(z_{P})+2\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})\wp(z_{P})\big(\omega_{1}\zeta(z_{P})-\zeta(\omega_{1})z_{P}\big)\bigg)dz_{P}+g_{2}\omega_{1}\zeta(\omega_{1})+4\frac{\zeta^{3}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle=5\omega_{1}\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{1}}\wp^{3}(z_{P})dz_{P}-3\zeta(\omega_{1})\int_{x}^{x+2\omega_{1}}\wp^{2}(z_{P})dz_{P}+g_{2}\omega_{1}\zeta(\omega_{1})+4\frac{\zeta^{3}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle=g_{3}\omega_{1}^{2}-g_{2}\omega_{1}\zeta(\omega_{1})+4\frac{\zeta^{3}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}.

\square

Remark 7.2

Observe that if we assume that the contours
a
and
b
start at a point
P_{0}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=0
, then we have

y_{1}=\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{3})=c-\zeta(\omega_{1})/{\omega_{1}}=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{1})=t_{3}.

As a consequence, the following matrix is symmetric:

H:=(H_{AB})=\big(\mathbf{t}^{B}(\phi_{A})\big)=\begin{pmatrix}t_{1}&t_{2}&t_{3}\\
x_{1}&x_{2}&x_{3}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&y_{3}\end{pmatrix}

(7.25)

In the next results of this subsection, this additional assumption is considered to be fulfilled.

Note that, according to (
3.37
), the common constant matrix of the dual metrics
\eta^{*}(\phi_{j})
w.r.t. the flat coordinates

\big\{\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{j}),\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{j}),\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{j})\big\}
is anti-diagonal:

\big(\eta^{\alpha\beta}\big):=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\quad\eta^{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{*}(\phi_{j})\big(d\mathbf{t}^{\alpha}(\phi_{j}),d\mathbf{t}^{\beta}(\phi_{j})\big),\quad\alpha,\beta=1,2,3.

In particular, this implies the following duality relations between flat coordinates of the metric
\eta(\phi_{j})
:

\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{j})=\mathbf{t}^{4-A}(\phi_{j}),\quad A=1,2,3.

(7.26)

Now, we use these relations and apply formula (
4.31
) (which coincides with formulas (
4.19
) and (
4.27
) in this case), we deduce that the prepotential associated with the semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold induced by the differential
\phi_{j}
is given by:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{j}}&=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{j})}\sum_{A,B=1}^{3}\frac{\big(d_{j}+d_{A^{\prime}}\big)\big(d_{j}+d_{B^{\prime}}\big)}{1+d_{j}+d_{A}}\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{j})\mathbf{t}^{B^{\prime}}(\phi_{j})H_{AB}\\
&=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{j})}\sum_{A,B=1}^{3}\frac{\big(d_{j}+d_{4-A}\big)\big(d_{j}+d_{4-B}\big)}{1+d_{j}+d_{A}}\mathbf{t}^{4-A}(\phi_{j})\mathbf{t}^{4-B}(\phi_{j})H_{AB}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{j})^{2}H_{11}+\frac{(d_{j}+1/2)^{2}}{2(d_{j}+1)(d_{j}+3/2)}\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{j})^{2}H_{22}+\frac{d_{j}^{2}}{2(d_{j}+1)(d_{j}+2)}\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{j})^{2}H_{33}\\
&\quad+\frac{(d_{j}+1/2)(2d_{j}+5/2)}{2(d_{j}+1)(d_{j}+3/2)}\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{j})\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{j})H_{12}+\frac{d_{j}(2d_{j}+3)}{2(d_{j}+1)(d_{j}+2)}\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{j})\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{j})H_{13}\\
&\quad+\frac{d_{j}(d_{j}+1/2)(2d_{j}+7/2)}{2(d_{j}+1)(d_{j}+2)(d_{j}+3/2)}\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{j})\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{j})H_{23},\end{split}

(7.27)

where
d_{j}
is the quasi-homogeneous degree of the differential
\phi_{j}
and
(H_{AB})
is the symmetric matrix (
7.25
).
In Theorems
7.1
and
7.2
, we consider differentials
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{3}
one by one and rewrite (
7.27
) explicitly in each case.

7.2.1
Prepotential associated with the Chazy equation

Theorem 7.1

The semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold induced by the normalized holomorphic differential
\phi_{1}
is described by the Darboux-Egoroff metric
\eta(\phi_{1})
and its flat coordinates
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
(
7.22
), the unit vector field
e_{\phi_{1}}=\partial_{t_{3}}
, the Euler vector field
\mathcal{E}_{\phi_{1}}=\frac{t_{2}}{2}\partial_{t_{2}}+t_{3}\partial_{t_{3}}
and the prepotential

\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=\frac{t_{3}^{2}}{2}t_{1}+\frac{t_{3}}{2}t_{2}^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}t_{2}^{4}E_{2}(2{\rm{i}}\pi t_{1}),

(7.28)

where
E_{2}
is the Eisenstein series defined by

E_{2}(\tau)=P(q):=1-24\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{nq^{n}}{1-q^{n}},\quad\quad q=e^{2{\rm{i}}\pi\tau},\quad\quad\Im(\tau)>0.

(7.29)

Moreover, the function
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
is quasi-homogeneous of degree
2
:
\mathcal{E}_{\phi_{1}}.\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}=2\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
.

Remark 7.3

If we make the change of variables

(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\longleftrightarrow(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}):=\big(\frac{t_{3}}{2{\rm{i}}\pi},t_{2},2{\rm{i}}\pi{t_{1}}\big),

then

\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=2{\rm{i}}\pi\bigg(\frac{(u_{1})^{2}}{2}u_{3}+\frac{u_{1}}{2}(u_{2})^{2}-\frac{\rm{i}\pi}{48}(u_{2})^{4}E_{2}(u_{3})\bigg)=2{\rm{i}}\pi\mathrm{F}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}),

where
\mathrm{F}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
is the solution to the WDVV equations obtained by Dubrovin (see
[
14
]
, formula (C.87)).

Proof:
Applying (
4.9
) and taking into account duality relations (
7.26
), we see that the unit vector field
e=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
is represented by

e=\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{1^{\prime}}(\phi_{1})}=\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{1})}=\partial_{t_{3}}.

Moreover, the expression of the Euler vector field in flat coordinates of
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
follows directly from the relation

E.\mathbf{t}^{i}(\phi_{j})=(d_{i}+d_{j})\mathbf{t}^{i}(\phi_{j}),\quad d_{i}=deg(\phi_{j}).

Now, since the holomorphic differential
\phi_{1}
is of degree
d_{1}=0
, formula (
7.27
) implies that the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
reduces to

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{3}^{2}}{2}H_{11}+\frac{1}{12}t_{2}^{2}H_{22}+\frac{5}{12}t_{2}t_{3}H_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{3}^{2}}{2}t_{1}+\frac{1}{12}t_{2}^{2}x_{2}+\frac{5}{12}t_{2}^{2}t_{3}.

In addition, by (
7.22
), (
7.23
) and the homogeneity property of the
\zeta
-function we have

x_{2}=t_{3}+3\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1};2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})}{\omega_{1}}=t_{3}+3\frac{\zeta(1/2|\tau)}{2\omega_{1}^{2}}=t_{3}+3t_{2}^{2}\zeta(1/2|\tau).

On the other hand, it is known that the function
\zeta(1/2|\tau)
(sometimes called Weierstrass eta function) satisfies

2\zeta(1/2|\tau)=\zeta(z+1|\tau)-\zeta(z|\tau)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}E_{2}(\tau),

(7.30)

where
\zeta(z|\tau)
is the zeta function associated with the lattice
\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
(see for instance
[
2
]
-Section 20 and
[
14
]
).
Substituting this into the expression of the function
x_{2}
we arrive at

x_{2}=t_{3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}t_{2}^{2}E_{2}(\tau)=t_{3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}t_{2}^{2}E_{2}({2{\rm i}\pi}t_{1})

(7.31)

and this shows formula (
7.28
).

\square

The following result appeared in
[
1
]
and (
[
14
]
-Appendix C). It allows to view the 3-dimensional semi-simple Frobenius manifold induced by the holomorphic differential
\phi_{1}
as a geometric framework of the Chazy equation (
7.32
).

Corollary 7.1

The Eisenstein series
E_{2}
satisfies the Chazy equation:

E_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}-2{\rm i}\pi E_{2}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}+3{\rm i}\pi\big(E_{2}^{\prime}\big)^{2}=0.

(7.32)

Proof:
By the above theorem and Dubrovin’s theory of Frobenius manifolds, we know that the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
obeys the WDVV equations:

\displaystyle F_{1}F_{3}F_{2}=F_{2}F_{3}F_{1},

where
F_{j}
is the Hessian matrix of the
\partial_{t_{j}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
and hence
F_{3}=F_{3}^{-1}=(\eta^{\alpha\beta})
is the anti-diagonal constant matrix of the metric
\eta(\phi_{1})
.

By calculating the left and right hand sides of this equation, we see that the associativity condition is equivalent to:

\partial_{t_{1}}^{3}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}+\big(\partial_{t_{1}}^{2}\partial_{t_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{2}}^{3}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}\big)=\big(\partial_{t_{1}}\partial_{t_{2}}^{2}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}\big)^{2}

Substituting the function
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
(
7.28
) into this equation we conclude that
E_{2}
solves the Chazy equation.

\square

7.2.2
Ramanujan identities

In the next result, we show how Ramanujan’s identities
[
32
]
, involving the Eisenstein series
E_{2},E_{4}
and
E_{6}
, can be easily derived from the formalism we have developed for prepotentials of Hurwitz-Frobenius manifolds, namely from our formula (
4.19
) in Theorem
4.1
and the observation that all the Frobenius structures of the same family share the Hessian matrix (
4.20
).
Let
q=e^{2{\rm{i}}\pi\tau}
, with
\Im(\tau)>0
. The Eisenstein series
E_{2}
is given by (
7.29
) and the series
E_{4}
and
E_{6}
are respectively defined by

\begin{split}&E_{4}(\tau)=Q(q):=1+240\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{3}q^{n}}{1-q^{n}};\\
&E_{6}(\tau)=R(q):=1-504\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{5}q^{n}}{1-q^{n}}.\end{split}

(7.33)

Proposition 7.3

Let
x_{3}=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{2})
and
y_{3}=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{3})
be the functions in (
7.23
) and (
7.24
), respectively.
Then we have

x_{3}=y_{2}=\frac{\rm{i}\pi^{3}}{3}t_{2}^{3}E_{2}^{\prime}(\tau),\quad\quad\quad y_{3}=-\frac{\pi^{4}}{6}t_{2}^{4}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau),\quad\quad\text{with}\quad t_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}}.

(7.34)

In particular, the following Ramanujan identities hold:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}E_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{12}\big(E_{2}^{2}-E_{4}\big);

(7.35)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}E_{4}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{3}\big(E_{2}E_{4}-E_{6}\big);

(7.36)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}E_{6}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\big(E_{2}E_{6}-E_{4}^{2}\big).

(7.37)

Proof:
Using (
4.20
), we deduce that the Hessian matrix of the function
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
is connected to matrix
H

(
7.25
) by:

\partial_{t_{A}}\partial_{t_{B}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}=\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}\big(\phi_{B^{\prime}}\big)=H_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}},\quad\text{for all $A,B$}.

Note that this expression of the Hessian matrix follows from our formalism. On the other hand, we can derive it from expression (
7.28
) for prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}
by differentiation. Comparing these two ways produces the three Ramanujan identities. Namely, using the duality of flat coordinates from
(
7.26
), we obtain

x_{3}=H_{23}=\partial_{t_{1}}\partial_{t_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}=\frac{\rm{i}\pi^{3}}{3}t_{2}^{3}E_{2}^{\prime}(2{\rm{i}}\pi{t_{1}})=\frac{\rm{i}\pi^{3}}{3}t_{2}^{3}E_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)

and

y_{3}=H_{33}=\partial_{t_{1}}\partial_{t_{1}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{1}}=-\frac{\pi^{4}}{6}t_{2}^{4}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}(2{\rm{i}}\pi{t_{1}})=-\frac{\pi^{4}}{6}t_{2}^{4}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau).

On the other hand, in (
7.23
) and (
7.24
), we have already obtained an expression for the flat coordinates
x_{3}
and
y_{3}
by direct calculation in terms of the Weierstrass invariants
g_{2}
,
g_{3}
and the zeta-function. Let us now recall that
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
can also be expressed via the Eisenstein series
E_{4}
and
E_{6}
, respectively. Specifically, we have (see for instance
[
3
]
, Theorem 1.18)

\begin{split}&g_{2}(2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})=\frac{1}{16\omega_{1}^{4}}g_{2}(1,\tau)=\frac{\pi^{4}}{12\omega_{1}^{4}}E_{4}(\tau);\\
&g_{3}(2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})=\frac{1}{64\omega_{1}^{6}}g_{3}(1,\tau)=\frac{\pi^{6}}{216\omega_{1}^{6}}E_{6}(\tau).\end{split}

(7.38)

Now, equating the two expressions for
x_{3}
, in (
7.23
) and (
7.34
) and using
the first relation in (
7.38
) and formula (
7.30
), yields the first Ramanujan differential equation (
7.35
):

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{6\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}^{3}}{\rm{i}\pi^{3}}x_{3}=\frac{6\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}^{3}}{\rm{i}\pi^{3}}\Big(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{6}\omega_{1}g_{2}(2\omega_{1};2\omega_{2})-2\sqrt{2}\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1};2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})}{\omega_{1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{6\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}^{3}}{\rm{i}\pi^{3}}\Big(\frac{\pi^{4}\sqrt{2}}{72\omega_{1}^{3}}E_{4}(\tau)-\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{4\omega_{1}^{3}}\zeta^{2}(1/2|\tau)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{6\rm{i}}E_{4}(\tau)-\frac{\pi}{6\rm{i}}E_{2}^{2}(\tau).

The second Ramanujan identity (
7.36
) is obtained in the same way by comparing two expressions for
y_{3}
. By virtue of (
7.24
) and (
7.30
) and (
7.38
), we have

\displaystyle y_{3}

\displaystyle=\omega_{1}^{2}g_{3}(2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})-\omega_{1}g_{2}(2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})\zeta(\omega_{1};2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})+\frac{4}{\omega_{1}}\zeta^{3}(\omega_{1};2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{6}}{216\omega_{1}^{4}}E_{6}(\tau)-\frac{\pi^{4}}{24\omega_{1}^{4}}E_{4}(\tau)\zeta(1/2|\tau)+\frac{1}{2\omega_{1}^{4}}\zeta^{3}(1/2|\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{6}}{216\omega_{1}^{4}}E_{6}(\tau)-\frac{\pi^{6}}{144\omega_{1}^{4}}E_{2}(\tau)E_{4}(\tau)+\frac{\pi^{6}}{432\omega_{1}^{4}}E_{2}^{3}(\tau).

This and (
7.34
) yield that

E_{2}^{\prime\prime}=-\frac{24\omega_{1}^{4}}{\pi^{4}}y_{3}=-\frac{\pi^{2}}{9}E_{6}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}E_{2}E_{4}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{18}E_{2}^{3}.

(7.39)

On the other hand, if we use the first Ramanujan identity (
7.35
), we also get

E_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\pi}{6i}E_{4}^{\prime}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{18}E_{4}E_{2}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{18}E_{2}^{3}.

Thus, by comparing this with (
7.39
) we deduce that (
7.36
) holds true.

It remains to prove the third identity (
7.36
).
Making use of the Chazy equation (
7.32
), (
7.39
) as well as the first and second Ramanujan relations (
7.35
)-(
7.36
), we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=E_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}-2{\rm i}\pi E_{2}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}+3{\rm i}\pi\big(E_{2}^{\prime}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}E_{6}^{\prime}+E_{2}^{\prime}E_{4}+E_{2}E_{4}^{\prime}-E_{2}^{\prime}E_{2}^{2}-2{\rm i}\pi E_{2}\Big(-\frac{2}{3}E_{6}+E_{2}E_{4}-\frac{1}{3}E_{2}^{3}\Big)-\frac{2{\rm i}\pi}{4}\big(E_{2}^{4}-2E_{2}^{2}E_{4}+E_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}E_{6}^{\prime}+\frac{2{\rm i}\pi}{3}E_{2}E_{6}-\frac{2{\rm i}\pi}{3}E_{4}^{2}.

From this we arrive at (
7.37
).

\square

Remark 7.4

Assume that
2\omega_{1}=1
and
2\omega_{2}=\tau
and consider the Weierstrass functions
\wp=\wp(\cdot|\tau)
and
\zeta=\zeta(\cdot|\tau)
associated with the lattice
\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
. Then from explicit formulas (
7.15
) and (
7.16
) for the differentials
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{3}
, the two relations in (
7.34
) (where we take
\omega_{1}=1/2
) and the fact that
x_{3}=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{2})
and
y_{3}=\mathbf{t^{3}}(\phi_{3})
, we obtain that the Eisenstein series
E_{2}
and its first and second derivatives can be viewed as the
a
-periods of some specific differentials as follows:

\displaystyle E_{2}=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\zeta(1/2|\tau)=-\frac{3}{\pi^{2}}\oint_{a}\wp(u|\tau)du;

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{3}{2\rm{i}\pi^{3}}\oint_{a}\bigg(\wp^{2}(u)+2\zeta(1/2)\wp(u)\bigg)du;

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime\prime}=-\frac{3}{2\pi^{4}}\oint_{a}\bigg(2\wp^{2}(u)-4\zeta(1/2)\wp(u)+\wp^{\prime}(u)\big(\zeta(u)-2\zeta(1/2)u\big)-\frac{g_{2}}{4}-4\zeta^{2}(1/2)\bigg)\wp(u)du.

As an application, we shall employ the first Ramanujan differential equation (
7.35
) and a special case of relation (
3.42
)
to derive a nonlinear differential equation satisfied by the three functions

e_{1}(\tau):=\wp(1/2|\tau),\quad e_{2}(\tau):=\wp(\tau/2|\tau),\quad e_{3}(\tau):=\wp((1+\tau)/2|\tau)

with
\wp(u|\tau):=\wp(u;1,\tau)
. This differential equation is a particular case of the rule for the derivative of the
\wp
-function with respect to the modulus
\tau
, recently proved in
[
5
]
(Section 10).

Recall that (
3.42
) gives a way to calculate derivatives of the critical values of the Weierstrass covering (
7.12
):

\lambda_{j}=\wp(\omega_{j};2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})+c=e_{j}(2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})+c,\quad\quad j=1,2,3,

with respect to flat coordinates
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
(
7.22
) of the metric
\eta(\phi_{1})
.

Corollary 7.2

For
j=1,2,3
, the function
e_{j}(\tau)
satisfies the equation:

\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}e_{j}^{\prime}(\tau)=-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}e_{j}^{2}(\tau)+\frac{1}{6}E_{2}e_{j}(\tau)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{9}E_{4}.

Proof:
Using (
3.42
) and relation
\tau=2\rm{i}\pi\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{1})=2\rm{i}\pi t_{1}
from (
7.22
), we have

\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\partial_{\tau}\lambda_{j}=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{\partial\lambda_{j}}{\partial\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{1})}=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{\phi_{3}(P_{j})}{\phi_{1}(P_{j})}.

(7.40)

Due to relations (
7.22
), (
7.30
),
e_{j}(\tau)=(2\omega_{1})^{2}e_{j}
and the first Ramanujan identity (
7.35
), the left hand side of (
7.40
) can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\partial_{\tau}\lambda_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\partial_{\tau}\Big(\frac{1}{4\omega_{1}^{2}}e_{j}(\tau)+c\Big)=\frac{1}{4\omega_{1}^{2}}\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(\tau)}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}+\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\partial_{\tau}\Big(t_{3}+\frac{1}{2\omega_{1}^{2}}\zeta(1/2|\tau)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\omega_{1}^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}e_{j}^{\prime}(\tau)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}E^{\prime}_{2}(\tau)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\omega_{1}^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}e_{j}^{\prime}(\tau)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{36}E_{2}^{2}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{36}E_{4}\Big).

Now, using explicit form (
7.16
) of the differential
\phi_{3}
and expression (
7.38
) for
g_{2}
in terms of
E_{4}
, we deduce that the right
hand side of (
7.40
) is

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{\phi_{3}(P_{j})}{\phi_{1}(P_{j})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\Big(8\omega_{1}^{2}e_{j}^{2}(2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})-8\omega_{1}\zeta(\omega_{1})e_{j}(2\omega_{1},2\omega_{2})-g_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}-4\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})\Big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\omega_{1}^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}e_{j}^{2}-\frac{1}{6}E_{2}e_{j}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}E_{4}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{36}E_{2}^{2}\Big).

This shows the desired differential equations.

\square

Remark 7.5

A link between the Ramanujan identities and the Gromov-Witten correlation functions was recently studied in
[
36
]
.

7.2.3
Further examples in dimension 3

In the next theorem we deal with prepotentials associated the differentials
\phi_{2}
(
7.15
) and
\phi_{3}
(
7.16
).

Theorem 7.2

The prepotentials corresponding to the semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold induced by the differential
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{3}
are respectively given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{2}^{3}}{6}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+\frac{x_{3}^{2}}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}(E_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}x_{1}^{3}}\Big)-\frac{2}{15}x_{1}^{3}x_{3}E_{2}\Big((E_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}x_{1}^{3}}\Big)\Big)-\frac{\pi^{4}}{180}x_{1}^{6}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big((E_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}x_{1}^{3}}\Big)\Big);

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{1}}{2}y_{2}^{2}+\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{2}y_{3}+\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)+\frac{27}{40\pi}y_{2}y_{3}\big(-6y_{3}\big)^{1/4}\Big(E_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big[\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{-1/4}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{9}{80\pi^{2}}y_{2}^{2}\big(-6y_{3}\big)^{1/2}\Big(E_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big[\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{-1/2}E_{2}\Big(\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)\Big),

where
(E_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}
denotes the inverse function of the Eisenstein series (
7.29
) and
\chi^{-1}
is the inverse function of

\chi(\tau):=\frac{\big(E_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)\big)^{3}}{\big(E_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)\big)^{4}}.

The functions
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{2}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{3}}
are respectively quasi-homogeneous with respect to the Euler vector fields:

\displaystyle E_{\phi_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{1}}{2}\partial_{x_{1}}+x_{2}\partial_{x_{2}}+\frac{3}{2}x_{3}\partial_{x_{3}};

\displaystyle E_{\phi_{3}}

\displaystyle=y_{1}\partial_{y_{1}}+\frac{3}{2}y_{2}\partial_{y_{2}}+2y_{3}\partial_{y_{3}},

with
E_{\phi_{2}}.\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{2}}=3\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{2}}
and
E_{\phi_{3}}.\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{3}}=4\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{3}}
.

Proof
: In the two cases, using (
3.56
) we obtain the precise action of the Euler vector field.
Let us now prove the stated formulas for prepotentials
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{2}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{3}}
.

1)
When
\phi_{j}=\phi_{2}
we have
d_{2}=1/2
and thus by (
7.27
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{3}^{2}}{2}H_{11}+\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{6}H_{22}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{30}H_{33}+\frac{7}{12}x_{2}x_{3}H_{12}+\frac{4}{15}x_{1}x_{3}H_{13}+\frac{3}{20}x_{1}x_{2}H_{23}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{3}^{2}}{2}t_{1}+\frac{x_{2}^{3}}{6}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{30}y_{3}+\frac{7}{12}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+\frac{4}{15}x_{1}x_{3}t_{3}+\frac{3}{20}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{3}^{2}}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\tau+\frac{x_{2}^{3}}{6}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{30}y_{3}+\frac{11}{15}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+\frac{4}{15}x_{1}x_{3}t_{3}.

We need to express the coordinates
\tau
,
t_{3}
and
y_{3}
in terms of
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
. Recall from (
7.23
) that
x_{1}=1/{\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}}
and then relation (
7.34
) implies that

x_{3}=\frac{\rm{i}\pi^{3}}{6\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}^{3}}E_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)=\frac{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}}{3}x_{1}^{3}E_{2}^{\prime}(\tau).

The function
E_{2}^{\prime\prime}
cannot vanish identically and then there is an open subset of
U\subset\mathbb{H}
(where
\mathbb{H}
is the upper half plane) such that
E^{\prime}_{2}
is a biholomorphic function on
U
. This implies that

\displaystyle\tau=(E_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}x_{1}^{3}}\Big);

\displaystyle y_{3}=-\frac{\pi^{4}}{6}x_{1}^{4}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big((E_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}x_{1}^{3}}\Big)\Big);

\displaystyle t_{3}=x_{2}-\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}E_{2}(\tau)=x_{2}-\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}E_{2}\Big((E_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}x_{1}^{3}}\Big)\Big),

which gives us the stated formula for
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{2}}
.

2)
Here we treat the case where
\phi_{j}=\phi_{3}
. Since
d_{3}=1
and

x_{2}=y_{1}+3\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}=y_{1}+\frac{t_{2}^{2}}{2}E_{2}(\tau),

it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}H_{11}+\frac{9}{40}y_{2}^{2}H_{22}+\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{12}H_{33}+\frac{27}{40}y_{2}y_{3}H_{12}+\frac{5}{12}y_{1}y_{3}H_{13}+\frac{11}{40}y_{1}y_{2}H_{23}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}t_{1}+\frac{9}{40}y_{2}^{2}x_{2}+\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{2}y_{3}+\frac{27}{40}y_{2}y_{3}t_{2}+\frac{11}{40}y_{1}y_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{1}}{2}y_{2}^{2}+\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{2}y_{3}+\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{27}{40}y_{2}y_{3}t_{2}+\frac{9}{80}y_{2}^{2}t_{2}^{2}E_{2}(\tau).

By (
7.34
) we can write

\chi(\tau):=\frac{\big(E_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)\big)^{3}}{\big(E_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)\big)^{4}}=-\frac{8y_{3}^{3}}{3y_{2}^{4}}

and in particular we have

\chi^{\prime}(\tau)\partial_{y_{3}}\tau=-8\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{y_{2}^{4}}.

Hence
\chi
is biholomorphic on some open subset of the upper half plane and then we can write

\tau=\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big).

Moreover, given that
y_{3}=-\frac{\pi^{4}}{6}t_{2}^{4}E_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)
, we get

t_{2}=\frac{\big(-6y_{3}\big)^{1/4}}{\pi}\Big(E_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big[\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{-1/4}.

Accordingly we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{1}}{2}y_{2}^{2}+\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{2}y_{3}+\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{27}{40}y_{2}y_{3}t_{2}+\frac{9}{80}y_{2}^{2}t_{2}^{2}E_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{1}}{2}y_{2}^{2}+\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{2}y_{3}+\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)+\frac{27}{40\pi}y_{2}y_{3}\big(-6y_{3}\big)^{1/4}\Big(E_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big[\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{-1/4}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{9}{80\pi^{2}}y_{2}^{2}\big(-6y_{3}\big)^{1/2}\Big(E_{2}^{\prime\prime}\Big[\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{-1/2}E_{2}\Big(\chi^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}y_{3}^{3}y_{2}^{-4}\Big)\Big)

which is the stated formula.

7.3
Examples of 3-dimensional deformed Hurwitz-Frobenius manifolds

Let
q
be a complex number such that
1+q\tau\neq 0
, with
\tau=\frac{\omega_{2}}{\omega_{1}}
. In this subsection we briefly describe the ingredients of the deformed Frobenius manifold structures on the three dimensional Hurwitz space of Weierstrass elliptic curves (
7.12
).

\bullet

Genus one
q
-bidifferential.

From (
5.3
) and (
7.13
) we deduce that the genus one
q
-bidifferential is given by

W_{q}(P,Q)=\Big(\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q})+\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{2\omega_{1}}-\frac{2{\rm i}\pi{q}}{4\omega_{1}^{2}(1+q\tau)}\Big)dz_{P}dz_{Q}.

(7.41)

\bullet

Primary
q
-bidifferentials and flat coordinates of the corresponding flat metrics.

Using the intimate connection between the Dubrovin primary differentials and their
\mathbf{q}
-deformations (
5.14
) and (
5.16
), we see that the
q
-analogue of the primary differentials (
7.14
), (
7.15
) and (
7.16
) are explicitly determined by

\begin{split}\phi_{q,1}(P)&:=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}W_{q}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{1+q\tau}\phi_{1}(P);\\
\phi_{q,2}(P)&:=\sqrt{2}{\rm res}_{\infty^{0}}\sqrt{\lambda(Q)}W_{q}(P,Q)\\
&=\phi_{2}(P)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{qt_{2}}{1+q\tau}\phi_{1}(P);\\
\phi_{q,3}(P)&:=\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)W_{q}(P,Q)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(Q)W_{q}(P,Q)\\
&=\phi_{3}(P)+q\phi_{4}(P)-{2{\rm i}\pi}q\frac{t_{3}+qs_{1}}{1+q\tau}\phi_{1}(P).\end{split}

(7.42)

Here
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
are the functions (
7.22
),
s_{1}:=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}\phi_{4}
and
\phi_{4}
denotes the following multivalued differential

\begin{split}&\phi_{4}(P)=\oint_{b}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(z_{P})dz_{P}=2\omega_{1}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(z_{P})\phi_{1}(P),\quad\text{with}\\
&\mathcal{U}(u):=\omega_{2}\wp^{\prime}(u)+2\zeta(\omega_{2})\Big(\zeta(u)-\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}u\Big)+2\wp(u)\Big(\omega_{2}\zeta(u)-\zeta(\omega_{2})u\Big)+{\rm{i}}\pi\frac{c}{\omega_{1}}u.\end{split}

(7.43)

We obtain (
7.43
) by arguing as in (
7.16
) and (
7.18
).

The three relations in (
7.42
) and the precise values of the evaluations
\phi_{k}(P_{j})
that were computed in Proposition
7.2
allow us to determine explicit formulas for the Darboux-Egoroff metrics

\eta(\phi_{q,k})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\big(\phi_{q,k}(P_{j})\big)^{2}(d\lambda_{j})^{2},\quad\quad k=1,2,3

induced by the
q
-differentials
\phi_{q,1}
,
\phi_{q,2}
and
\phi_{q,3}
. Let us denote by
\mathbf{t}^{j}(\phi_{q,k})
the flat coordinates (
5.25
) of the metric

\eta(\phi_{q,k})
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{q,k}):=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}\phi_{q,k};

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{q,k}):=\sqrt{2}{\rm res}_{\infty^{0}}\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\phi_{q,k}(P);

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{q,k}):=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{q,k}(P)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(P)\phi_{q,k}(P).

In order to determine explicit formulas for the functions
\big\{\mathbf{t}^{k}(\phi_{q,s}):\ k,s=1,2,3\big\}
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.2

Let
\phi_{4}
be the multivalued differential (
7.43
) and
\tau={2{\rm{i}}\pi}t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
be the coordinates (
7.22
). We have

\begin{split}s_{1}&:=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}\phi_{4}=t_{3}\tau+{\rm{i}}\pi t_{2}^{2};\\
s_{2}&:=\sqrt{2}\underset{\infty^{0}}{\rm res}\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\phi_{4}(P)=\frac{\pi^{4}}{18}t_{2}^{3}\bigg({\tau}E_{4}(\tau)-{\tau}E_{2}^{2}(\tau)+\frac{12{\rm{i}}}{\pi}E_{2}(\tau)\bigg)+{2{\rm{i}}\pi}t_{2}t_{3};\\
s_{3}&:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{4}(P)=\frac{\pi^{6}}{108}{\tau}t_{2}^{4}\Big(E_{2}^{3}(\tau)-3E_{2}(\tau)E_{4}(\tau)+2E_{6}(\tau)\Big)-\frac{{\rm i}\pi^{5}}{12}t_{2}^{4}\Big(E_{2}^{2}(\tau)-E_{4}(\tau)\Big);\\
s_{4}&:=\oint_{b}\lambda(P)\phi_{4}(P)=2{{\rm{i}}\pi}t_{3}^{2}\tau-4\pi^{2}t_{3}t_{2}^{2}-\pi^{4}t_{2}^{4}E_{2}+\frac{\pi^{6}}{54}t_{2}^{4}\tau^{2}\big(E_{6}-E_{2}E_{4})\\
&\quad\hskip 65.44142pt+\frac{\pi^{6}}{108}t_{2}^{4}\tau^{2}E_{2}\big(E_{2}^{2}-E_{4}\big)-\frac{{\rm{i}}\pi^{5}}{6}t_{2}^{4}\tau(E_{2}^{2}-E_{4}).\end{split}

(7.44)

Here
E_{2},E_{4},E_{6}
denote the Eisenstein series defined in (
7.29
) and (
7.33
).

Proof:
We obtain
s_{1}
using (
7.43
), the Legendre identity (
7.9
), (
7.22
). By a simple calculation based on (
7.43
) and (
7.12
) and properties of Weierstrass’s functions, we arrive at

\displaystyle s_{2}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{6}g_{2}\tau\omega_{1}-2\sqrt{2}\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\zeta(\omega_{2})+\frac{2{\rm{i}}\pi}{\sqrt{2}\omega_{1}}c;

\displaystyle s_{3}=g_{3}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}-g_{2}\omega_{2}\zeta(\omega_{1})+\frac{{\rm{i}}\pi}{4}g_{2}+{{\rm{i}}\pi}c^{2}-2{\rm{i}}\pi c\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}+4\frac{\zeta^{2}(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\zeta(\omega_{2});

\displaystyle s_{4}=2{{\rm{i}}\pi}cs_{1}+g_{3}\omega_{2}^{2}-g_{2}\omega_{2}\zeta(\omega_{2})-2{\rm{i}}\pi\frac{c}{\omega_{1}}\zeta(\omega_{2})+4\frac{\zeta(\omega_{1})}{\omega_{1}}\zeta^{2}(\omega_{2}).

Thus, the desired expressions for
s_{2}
,
s_{3}
and
s_{4}
in terms of
\tau,t_{2},t_{3}
follow from the Legendre identity (
7.9
), (
7.38
), (
7.30
) and the following fact that

c=t_{3}+\zeta(\omega_{1})/\omega_{1}=t_{3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}t_{2}^{2}E_{2}(\tau)

which is an immediate consequence of (
7.22
).

\square

In the next proposition, we express flat coordinates of the metrics
\eta(\phi_{q,k})
in terms of the functions
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}
from (
7.44
) as well as the systems of flat coordinates (
7.22
)-(
7.24
).

Proposition 7.4

i)

Flat coordinates of
\eta(\phi_{q,1})
:

\begin{split}&t_{q,1}:=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}\phi_{q,1}(P)=\frac{\tau_{q}}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}=\frac{t_{1}}{1+q\tau};\\
&t_{q,2}:=\sqrt{2}{\rm res}_{\infty^{0}}\sqrt{\lambda(P)}\phi_{q,1}(P)=\frac{t_{2}}{1+q\tau};\\
&t_{q,3}:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{q,1}(P)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(P)\phi_{q,1}(P)=\frac{t_{3}+qs_{1}}{1+q\tau}.\end{split}

(7.45)

ii)

Flat coordinates of
\eta(\phi_{q,2})
:

\begin{split}&x_{q,1}:=\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{q,2})=t_{q,2};\\
&x_{q,2}:=\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{q,2})=x_{2}-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q}{1+q\tau}(t_{2})^{2};\\
&x_{q,3}:=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{q,2})=x_{3}-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q}{1+q\tau}t_{2}t_{3}+qs_{2}-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q^{2}}{1+q\tau}t_{2}s_{1}.\end{split}

(7.46)

iii)

Flat coordinates of
\eta(\phi_{q,3})
:

\begin{split}y_{q,1}&:=\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{q,3})=t_{q,3};\\
y_{q,2}&:=\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{q,3})=x_{q,3};\\
y_{q,3}&:=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{q,3})=y_{3}+qs_{3}-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q(t_{3}+s_{1})}{1+q\tau}t_{3}+qs_{3}+qs_{4}-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q^{2}(t_{3}+s_{1})}{1+q\tau}s_{1}.\end{split}

(7.47)

Proof:
In view of (
7.42
), we obtain (
7.45
)-(
7.47
).

\square

\bullet
Prepotential associated with the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q,1}
.

Applying (
5.34
) for a prepotential of the deformed Frobenius manifold, we deduce that the prepotential corresponding to the deformed
\phi_{q,j}
-Frobenius manifold structure is given by

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,j}}&=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{j})}\sum_{k,s=1}^{3}\frac{\big(d_{j}+d_{4-k}\big)\big(d_{j}+d_{4-s}\big)}{1+d_{j}+d_{k}}\mathbf{t}^{4-k}(\phi_{q,j})\mathbf{t}^{4-s}(\phi_{q,j})\mathbf{t}^{k}(\phi_{q,s}).\end{split}

(7.48)

This formula is the
q
-analogue of (
7.27
).

We are going to apply formula (
7.48
) to calculate the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,1}}
corresponding to the deformed Frobenius manifold structure induced by the
q
-differential
\phi_{q,1}
. Let us mention that this example appeared in
[
42
]
, where a generalized Dubrovin bilinear pairing was employed.

Let
E_{2}
be the Eisenstein series (
7.29
) and
E_{q,2}
be the
q
-Eisenstein series defined by

E_{q,2}(\tau_{q}):=\frac{1}{(1-q\tau_{q})^{2}}E_{2}\Big(\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big)-\frac{6{\rm i}q}{\pi(1-q\tau_{q})},\quad\quad\tau_{q}:=\frac{\tau}{1+q\tau}.

(7.49)

Remark 7.6

Note that when
q
is an integer, we have
\Im(\tau_{q})>0
and thus, due to the following quasi-modular property of
E_{2}

[
3
,
46
]
:

E_{2}\Big(\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\Big)=(cz+d)^{2}E_{2}(z)-\frac{6{\rm i}}{\pi}c(cz+d),\quad\quad\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z}),

we see that (
7.49
) becomes as:

E_{q,2}(\tau_{q})=E_{2}(\tau_{q}).

Let
t_{q,1}
,
t_{q,2}
,
t_{q,3}
be the flat coordinates (
7.45
) of the flat metric
\eta(\phi_{q,1})
.
Then the WDVV-quasi-homogeneous solution
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,1}}
induced by the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q,1}
is of the form:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,1}}&=\frac{(t_{q,3})^{2}}{2}t_{q,1}+\frac{(t_{q,2})^{2}}{2}t_{q,3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}(t_{q,2})^{4}E_{q,2}(\tau_{q}).\end{split}

(7.50)

The prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,1}}
is quasi-homogeneous of degree 2 with respect to the Euler vector field
\frac{t_{q,2}}{2}\partial_{t_{q,2}}+t_{q,3}\partial_{t_{q,3}}
.
Indeed, using (
7.48
) and bearing in mind the fact that
\phi_{q,1}
is a quasi-homogeneous
q
-differential of degree 0 (see Section 5.1), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(t_{q,3})^{2}}{2}\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{q,1})+\frac{1}{12}(t_{q,2})^{2}\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{q,2})+\frac{5}{12}t_{q,2}t_{q,3}\mathbf{t}^{1}(\phi_{q,2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(t_{q,3})^{2}}{2}t_{q,1}+\frac{1}{12}(t_{q,2})^{2}\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{q,2})+\frac{5}{12}(t_{q,2})^{2}t_{q,3}.

Therefore, we only need to prove that

x_{q,2}:=\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{q,2})=t_{q,3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}(t_{q,2})^{2}E_{q,2}(\tau_{q}),

(7.51)

in order to obtain the prepotential as a function of the variables
t_{q,1},t_{q,2}
and
t_{q,3}
.

Let us first observe that (
7.42
) and (
7.31
) imply that

x_{q,2}=x_{2}-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q}{1+q\tau}(t_{2})^{2}=t_{3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}t_{2}^{2}E_{2}(\tau)-{2{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q}{1+q\tau}(t_{2})^{2}.

On the other hand, (
7.45
) implies that the inverse of the map
(\tau={2{\rm{i}}\pi}t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\longmapsto(\tau_{q}={2{\rm{i}}\pi}t_{q,1},t_{q,2},t_{q,3})
is determined by

\begin{split}&\tau=\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}};\\
&t_{2}=\frac{t_{q,2}}{1-q\tau_{q}};\\
&t_{3}=t_{q,3}-\frac{i\pi{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}(t_{q,2})^{2}.\end{split}

(7.52)

Plugging this into the above expression for
x_{q,2}=x_{q,2}(\tau,t_{2},t_{3})
and using (
7.49
) we obtain (
7.51
).

\bullet

Chazy equation.

Similarly to (
7.32
), the WDVV equations for the prepotential (
7.50
) is equivalent to the Chazy equation for the function
E_{q,2}
defined by (
7.49
):

E_{q,2}^{\prime\prime\prime}-2{\rm i}\pi E_{q,2}E_{q,2}^{\prime\prime}+3{\rm i}\pi\big(E_{q,2}^{\prime}\big)^{2}=0.

\bullet

Generalized Ramanujan identities.

Let
E_{q,2}
be given by (
7.49
) and
E_{q,4}
and
E_{q,6}
be the following
q
-Eisenstein series:

\begin{split}&E_{q,4}(\tau_{q}):=\frac{1}{(1-q\tau_{q})^{4}}E_{4}\Big(\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big);\\
&E_{q,6}(\tau_{q}):=\frac{1}{(1-q\tau_{q})^{6}}E_{6}\Big(\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big),\end{split}\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\tau_{q}:=\frac{\tau}{1+q\tau},

(7.53)

with
E_{4}
and
E_{6}
being the Eisenstein series (
7.33
).

Then the following
q
-Ramanujan identities are satisfied:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}E_{q,2}^{\prime}(\tau_{q})=\frac{1}{12}\big(E_{q,2}^{2}(\tau_{q})-E_{q,4}(\tau_{q})\big);\\
&\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}E_{q,4}^{\prime}(\tau_{q})=\frac{1}{3}\big(E_{q,2}(\tau_{q})E_{q,4}(\tau_{q})-E_{q,6}(\tau_{q})\big);\\
&\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}E_{q,6}^{\prime}(\tau_{q})=\frac{1}{2}\big(E_{q,2}(\tau_{q})E_{q,6}(\tau_{q})-E_{q,4}^{2}(\tau_{q})\big).\end{split}

(7.54)

The differential equations (
7.54
) can be proved using the usual Ramanujan identities (
7.35
)-(
7.37
) and expressions (
7.49
) and (
7.53
) for the functions
E_{q,2}
,
E_{q,4}
and
E_{q,6}
.

Alternatively, they can be obtained by adapting the scheme of the proof of (
7.35
)-(
7.35
) where the main idea relies on the following result:

\displaystyle x_{3}=\mathbf{t}^{2}(\phi_{q,3})=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{q,2})=\partial_{t_{q,1}}\partial_{t_{q,2}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,1}}=\frac{{\rm i}\pi^{3}}{3}(t_{q,2})^{3}E^{\prime}_{q,2}(\tau_{q});

\displaystyle y_{3}=\mathbf{t}^{3}(\phi_{q,3})=\partial_{t_{q,1}}^{2}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,1}}=-\frac{\pi^{4}}{6}(t_{q,2})^{4}E^{\prime\prime}_{q,2}(\tau_{q}).

These equalities are direct consequence of the fact that the three prepotentials
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,j}}
in (
7.48
) have the same Hessian matrix:

\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{k}(\phi_{q,j})}\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{s}(\phi_{q,j})}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,j}}=\mathbf{t}^{4-k}(\phi_{q,4-s}),\quad\quad\forall\ j,k,s=1,2,3.

\bullet

Prepotentials associated with the
q
-differentials
\phi_{q,2}
and
\phi_{q,3}
.

Let
\phi_{q,2}
and
\phi_{q,3}
be differential given by
(
7.42
) and
x_{q,k}:=\mathbf{t}^{k}(\phi_{q,2})
(
7.46
) and
y_{q,k}:=\mathbf{t}^{k}(\phi_{q,3})
(
7.47
) be the flat coordinates of the flat metrics

\eta(\phi_{q,2})
and
\eta(\phi_{q,3})
, respectively.
Let us denote by
(E_{q,2}^{\prime})^{-1}
the inverse function of the
q
-Eisenstein series (
7.49
) and
\chi_{q}^{-1}
that of

\chi_{q}(\tau_{q}):=\frac{\big(E_{q,2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{q})\big)^{3}}{\big(E_{q,2}^{\prime}(\tau_{q})\big)^{4}}.

Then using arguments similar to those in the proof of Theorem
7.2
, we conclude that the prepotentials
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,2}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,3}}
take the following forms:

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(x_{q,2})^{3}}{6}+x_{q,1}x_{q,2}x_{q,3}+\frac{(x_{q,3})^{2}}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\big(E_{q,2}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{q,3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}(x_{q,1})^{3}}\Big)-\frac{2}{15}(x_{q,1})^{3}x_{q,3}E_{q,2}\Big(\big(E_{q,2}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{q,3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}(x_{q,1})^{3}}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\pi^{4}}{180}(x_{q,1})^{6}E_{q,2}^{\prime\prime}\Big(\big(E_{q,2}^{\prime}\big)^{-1}\Big(\frac{3x_{q,3}}{{\rm{i}}\pi^{3}(x_{q,1})^{3}}\Big)\Big)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{q,1}}{2}(y_{q,2})^{2}+\frac{(y_{q,1})^{2}}{2}y_{q,3}+\frac{(y_{q,3})^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\chi_{q}^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}(y_{q,3})^{3}(y_{q,2})^{-4}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{27}{40\pi}y_{q,2}y_{q,3}\big(-6y_{q,3}\big)^{1/4}\Big(E_{q,2}^{\prime\prime}\Big[\chi_{q}^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}(y_{q,3})^{3}(y_{q,2})^{-4}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{-1/4}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{9}{80\pi^{2}}(y_{q,2})^{2}\big(-6y_{q,3}\big)^{1/2}\Big(E_{q,2}^{\prime\prime}\Big[\chi_{q}^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}(y_{q,3})^{3}(y_{q,2})^{-4}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{-1/2}E_{q,2}\Big(\chi_{q}^{-1}\Big(-\frac{8}{3}(y_{q,3})^{3}(y_{q,2})^{-4}\Big)\Big).

The functions
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,2}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,3}}
satisfy the following quasi-homogeneity property:

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{x_{q,1}}{2}\partial_{x_{q,1}}+x_{q,2}\partial_{x_{q,2}}+\frac{3}{2}x_{q,3}\partial_{x_{q,3}}\Big).\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,2}}=3\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,2}};

\displaystyle\Big(y_{q,1}\partial_{y_{q,1}}+\frac{3}{2}y_{q,2}\partial_{y_{q,2}}+2y_{q,3}\partial_{y_{q,3}}\Big).\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,3}}=4\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q,3}}.

8
Appendix

8.1
Appendix 1: Diagonal flat metrics

The goal of this Appendix is to prove the flatness property of the metric
\mathbf{ds^{2}_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}(\omega)
given by (
3.4
).

Theorem 8.1

Let
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\neq(0,0)
and
\omega
be a differential satisfying (
3.1
) and (
3.3
). Then the diagonal metric
\mathbf{ds^{2}_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}(\omega)
defined in (
3.4
) is flat.

We need the following lemma which investigates some useful relations involving the Christoffel symbols (
3.6
) of the diagonal metric (
3.4
).

Lemma 8.1

Let
i,j,s\in\big\{1,\dots,N\big\}
be distinct. Then the Christoffel symbols of the metric
\mathbf{ds^{2}_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}(\omega)
satisfy the following relations:

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{s}}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}=\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{js}^{j}-\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{is}^{i}+\Gamma_{is}^{i}\Gamma_{sj}^{s},

(8.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{s}}\Gamma_{ii}^{j}=\Gamma_{ii}^{j}\Gamma_{is}^{i}-\Gamma_{ii}^{j}\Gamma_{js}^{j}-\Gamma_{ii}^{s}\Gamma_{ss}^{j},

(8.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}=\frac{\omega(P_{j})}{2\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}W(P_{i},P_{j})+\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{ij}^{j}-\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}\Gamma_{ij}^{i},

(8.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}=\frac{\omega(P_{j})}{2\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}W(P_{i},P_{j})-(\Gamma_{ij}^{i})^{2}+\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\omega(P_{j})}{\omega(P_{j})}\Gamma_{ij}^{i},

(8.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Gamma_{ii}^{i}=\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\Gamma_{ij}^{i},

(8.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{\underset{s\neq i,j}{s=1,}}^{N}\Gamma_{jj}^{s}\Gamma_{is}^{i}&=\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{j}+\beta+2}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}+\frac{\omega(P_{j})}{2\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}W(P_{i},P_{j})\\
&\quad\displaystyle+\frac{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{j}+\beta_{2}}\times\frac{\omega(P_{j})}{2\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}W(P_{i},P_{j}).\end{array}\end{split}

(8.6)

Proof:

Taking the partial derivative with respect to a branch point
\lambda_{s}
,
s\neq i,j
, in the first relation in (
3.6
) and using Rauch formulas
(
2.15
) and (
3.2
), we obtain (
8.1
). Relation (
8.2
) follows immediately from the second equality in (
3.6
) and (
8.1
). We get the two equalities (
8.3
) and (
8.4
) using (
3.6
) and (
3.2
).

By virtue of Rauch formulas (
3.2
), expressions (
3.6
) for the Christoffel symbols and (
8.4
) we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\Gamma_{ii}^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}-\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\omega(P_{i})}{(\omega(P_{i}))^{2}}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\omega(P_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(P_{j})}{2\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}W(P_{i},P_{j})+\frac{1}{4}W(P_{i},P_{j})^{2}-\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\Gamma_{ij}^{i},

which establishes (
8.5
).

Finally, note that from (
3.6
) and the Rauch formulas, we can write

\forall\ s\neq i,j,\quad\Gamma_{jj}^{s}\Gamma_{is}^{i}=-\frac{\beta_{1}\lambda_{s}+\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{j}+\beta_{2}}\frac{\omega(P_{j})}{2\omega(P_{i})}\partial_{\lambda_{s}}W(P_{i},P_{j}).

We obtain (
8.6
) using this relation together with the action (
3.7
) of the vector field
\sum_{k}(\beta_{1}\lambda_{k}+\beta_{2})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}
on
W(P_{i},P_{j})
.

\square

We now move on to prove Theorem
8.1
.

Proof of Theorem
8.1
:
We are going to show that the Riemann curvature tensor of the metric
\mathbf{ds^{2}_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}(\omega)
vanishes identically.
Recall that the Riemann curvature tensor is defined by

R_{ijk}^{t}:=\partial_{j}\Gamma_{ik}^{t}-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{t}+\textstyle\sum_{s}\big(\Gamma_{ik}^{s}\Gamma_{sj}^{t}-\Gamma_{ij}^{s}\Gamma_{sk}^{t}\big).

We distinguish four cases.

First case:

i,j,k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
are distinct. We have

\displaystyle R_{ijk}^{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}\Gamma_{ik}^{t}-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{t}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ij}^{t}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{kj}^{t}-\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{t}-\Gamma_{ij}^{j}\Gamma_{jk}^{t}.

If
t\notin\{i,j,k\}
, clearly we have
R_{ijk}^{t}=0
. By (
8.1
) we deduce that if
t=i
,

\displaystyle R_{ijk}^{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}=\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{kj}^{k}-\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}+\Gamma_{jk}^{j}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}-\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{jk}^{j}+\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}-\Gamma_{kj}^{k}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}=0

and if
t=j
,

\displaystyle R_{ijk}^{j}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{j}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ij}^{j}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{kj}^{j}-\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{j}-\Gamma_{ij}^{j}\Gamma_{jk}^{j}

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{ji}^{j}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}+\Gamma_{ji}^{j}\Gamma_{jk}^{j}-\Gamma_{jk}^{j}\Gamma_{ki}^{j}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ij}^{j}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{kj}^{j}-\Gamma_{ij}^{j}\Gamma_{jk}^{j}=0.

Again (
8.1
) implies the vanishing of
R_{ijk}^{k}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle R_{ijk}^{k}

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{kj}^{k}-\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}-\Gamma_{ij}^{j}\Gamma_{jk}^{k}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ij}^{i}-\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{kj}^{k}+\Gamma_{kj}^{k}\Gamma_{ji}^{i}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{kj}^{k}-\Gamma_{ij}^{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}-\Gamma_{ij}^{j}\Gamma_{jk}^{k}=0.

Second case:

i=j\neq k
. In this case, the Riemann curvature tensor takes the following form

\displaystyle R_{iik}^{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{t}-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ii}^{t}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{t}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ki}^{t}-\sum_{s}\Gamma_{ii}^{s}\Gamma_{sk}^{t}.

If
t\notin\{i,k\}
then by (
8.2
), we get

\displaystyle R_{iik}^{t}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ii}^{t}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{t}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ki}^{t}-\Gamma_{ii}^{k}\Gamma_{kk}^{t}-\Gamma_{ii}^{t}\Gamma_{tk}^{t}

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{ii}^{t}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}+\Gamma_{ii}^{t}\Gamma_{kt}^{t}+\Gamma_{ii}^{k}\Gamma_{kk}^{t}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{t}-\Gamma_{ii}^{k}\Gamma_{kk}^{t}-\Gamma_{ii}^{t}\Gamma_{tk}^{t}=0.

If
t=i
, then (
8.5
) implies that

\displaystyle R_{iik}^{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ii}^{i}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{i}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ki}^{i}-\sum_{s}\Gamma_{ii}^{s}\Gamma_{sk}^{i}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{i}+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ki}^{i}-\Gamma_{ii}^{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}-\Gamma_{ii}^{k}\Gamma_{kk}^{i}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ki}^{i}-\Gamma_{ii}^{k}\Gamma_{kk}^{i}=0.

If
t=k
, according to (
3.6
), (
8.3
), (
8.4
) and (
8.6
) we can write

\displaystyle R_{iik}^{k}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}-\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ii}^{k}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{k}+(\Gamma_{ik}^{k})^{2}-\sum_{s}\Gamma_{ii}^{s}\Gamma_{sk}^{k}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}+\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}+\frac{\beta_{1}\lambda_{k}+\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\partial_{k}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}+\Gamma_{ik}^{i}\Gamma_{ii}^{k}+(\Gamma_{ik}^{k})^{2}-\Gamma_{ii}^{i}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}-\Gamma_{ii}^{k}\Gamma_{kk}^{k}-\sum_{s\neq i,k}\Gamma_{ii}^{s}\Gamma_{sk}^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(P_{i})}{2\omega(P_{k})}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}W(P_{k},P_{i})-(\Gamma_{ik}^{k})^{2}+\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}+\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta_{1}\lambda_{k}+\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\bigg(\frac{\omega(P_{i})}{2\omega(P_{k})}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}W(P_{k},P_{i})+\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}-\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{k}}\omega(P_{k})}{\omega(P_{k})}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\beta_{1}\lambda_{k}+\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}\Gamma_{ik}^{i}+(\Gamma_{ik}^{k})^{2}-\bigg(-\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+2\beta_{2}}+\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}\omega(P_{i})}{\omega(P_{i})}\bigg)\Gamma_{ik}^{k}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\beta_{1}\lambda_{k}+\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\bigg(-\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{1}\lambda_{k}+2\beta_{2}}+\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{k}}\omega(P_{k})}{\omega(P_{k})}\bigg)\Gamma_{ik}^{k}-\sum_{s\neq i,k}\Gamma_{ii}^{s}\Gamma_{sk}^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega(P_{i})}{2\omega(P_{k})}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}W(P_{k},P_{i})+\frac{\beta_{1}\lambda_{k}+\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\frac{\omega(P_{i})}{2\omega(P_{k})}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}W(P_{k},P_{i})+\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{1}\lambda_{i}+\beta_{2}}\Gamma_{ik}^{k}-\sum_{s\neq i,k}\Gamma_{ii}^{s}\Gamma_{sk}^{k}

\displaystyle=0.

Third case:

i=k\neq j
. The vanishing of
R_{iji}^{t}
follows from the second case and the properties of the Riemann tensor:

\displaystyle\forall\ t,\quad R_{iji}^{t}=-R_{iij}^{t}=0.

Fourth case:
If
j=k
, then we always have
R_{ijj}^{t}=R_{iii}^{t}=0
.

\square

8.2
Appendix 2: Multivalued differentials and quasi-homogeneity properties

In this appendix we explore some results related to multivalued differentials defined on surface
C_{g}
of genus
g\geq 1
, where
\big\{(C_{g},\lambda),\{a_{k},b_{k}\}_{k}\big\}
is a fixed point in the simple Hurwitz space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
.

Let
\sigma
and
\nu
be two positive integers. For
k=1,\dots,g
, consider the multivalued differentials

\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P):=\oint_{a_{k}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\sigma}W(P,Q),\quad\quad\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P):=\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}W(P,Q).

(8.7)

Proposition 8.1

Let
j=1,\dots,g
. The differentials
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
and
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}
(
8.7
) have the following jumps:

\begin{split}&\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P+b_{j})-\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)=-2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jk}d\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma};\\
&\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P+a_{j})-\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)=0;\\
&\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P+a_{j})-\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)=2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jk}d\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\nu};\\
&\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P+b_{j})-\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)=0.\end{split}

(8.8)

Moreover, the periods of the differentials
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
and
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}
are as follows:

\begin{split}&\oint_{a_{j}}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}=0;\\
&\oint_{b_{j}}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}=-2{\rm{i}}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\sigma}\delta_{jk}+2{\rm{i}}\pi\oint_{a_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\omega_{j}(P);\\
&\oint_{a_{j}}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}=2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jk}\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu};\\
&\oint_{b_{j}}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}=2{\rm i}\pi\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\nu}\omega_{j}(P).\end{split}

(8.9)

Here
P_{0}
denotes an arbitrary starting point of the cycles
a_{k},b_{k}
.

Proof:
We obtain the jumps and the periods of the multivalued differential
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
by a direct adaptation of the proofs of (
3.29
) and (
3.30
).

Let us deal with the differential
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}W(P,Q)=\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}d_{P}d_{Q}\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=d_{Q}\Big[\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}d_{P}\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)\Big)\Big]-\Big[d_{P}\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)\Big)\Big]d\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}.

Integrating both sides with respect to
Q
along the contour
b_{k}
while choosing a starting point
P_{0}
of the contour
b_{k}
and keeping in
mind the quasi-periodicity properties (
2.9
) of the Riemann Theta function, we see that the differential
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}
can
be represented by

\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}\omega_{k}(P)-\oint_{b_{k}}d_{P}\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)\Big)d\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}.

Let us define

F_{k,\nu}(P):=\int^{P}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}\int^{P}\omega_{k}-\oint_{b_{k}}\log\Big(\Theta_{\Delta}\big(\mathcal{A}(P)-\mathcal{A}(Q)\big)\Big)d\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}.

Then, due to the quasi-periodicity property (
2.9
) of the Riemann Theta function, we see that
F_{k,\nu}
is a multivalued function with

\displaystyle F_{k,\nu}(P+a_{j})-F_{k,\nu}(P)

\displaystyle=0=\oint_{a_{j}}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}},\quad\forall\ j\neq k;

\displaystyle F_{k,\nu}(P+a_{k})-F_{k,\nu}(P)

\displaystyle=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}=\oint_{a_{k}}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}};

\displaystyle F_{k,\nu}(P+b_{j})-F_{k,\nu}(P)

\displaystyle=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}\mathbb{B}_{jk}-\oint_{b_{k}}\Big(-{\rm i}\pi\mathbb{B}_{jj}-2{\rm{i}}\pi\mathop{\langle\beta,e_{j}\rangle}-2{\rm i}\pi\int_{Q}^{P}\omega_{j}\Big)d\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}

\displaystyle=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}\mathbb{B}_{jk}+2{\rm i}\pi\oint_{b_{k}}\Big(\int_{Q}^{P}\omega_{j}\Big)d\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}

\displaystyle=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}\mathbb{B}_{jk}+2{\rm i}\pi\Big(\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}\int_{Q}^{P}\omega_{j}\Big)\bigg|_{Q=P_{0}}^{Q=P_{0}+b_{k}}+2{\rm i}\pi\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}\omega_{j}(Q)

\displaystyle=2{\rm i}\pi\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{\nu}\omega_{j}(Q).

This, given that
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)=dF_{k,\nu}(P)
, yields

\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P+b_{j})-\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)=0=\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P+a_{l})-\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)

for all
j,l=1,\dots,g
and
l\neq k
.

On the other hand, the expression
F_{k,\nu}(P+a_{k})-F_{k,\nu}(P)
assumes that the point
P
is considered as the starting point of the
contour
a_{k}
. Therefore, since the point
P_{0}
can be chosen as the intersection point of the contours
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
, i.e.
P_{0}=P
, we get

\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P+a_{k})-\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)=dF_{k,\nu}(P+a_{k})-dF_{k,\nu}(P)=2\rm{i}\pi d\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\nu}.

\square

Proposition 8.2

Let
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
and
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}
be the multivalued differentials defined by (
8.7
) and
t^{A}
be an operation from the set
\big\{t^{i,\alpha},\ v^{i},\ s^{i},\ \rho^{k},\ u^{k}\Big\}
described in (
3.21
)-(
3.22
).

Then the action of the vector field
e=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
on the functions
t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)
and
t^{A}\big(\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big)
is given by

\begin{split}&e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)=\sigma\oint_{a_{k}}\lambda(P)^{\sigma-1}\phi_{t^{A}}(P);\\
&e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big)=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}+\nu\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\nu-1}\phi_{t^{A}}(P).\end{split}

(8.10)

Proof:
Let us denote by
F_{g}
the fundamental polygon associated with the compact surface
C_{g}
. The differential

\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P):=\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)

(8.11)

is multivalued and, by (
8.8
), its jumps are such that
\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P+a_{j})-\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)=0
and

\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P+b_{j})-\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)=-2{\rm i}\pi\Big(\delta_{jk}\sigma\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma-1}\phi_{t^{A}}(P)+\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)-2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jk}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}d\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\Big).

Therefore, using Rauch formulas (
3.15
) and the residue theorem we conclude that

\displaystyle e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P_{j})=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\underset{P_{j}}{{\rm res}}\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\oint_{F_{g}}\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{a_{j}}\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)-\oint_{a_{j}}\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P+b_{j})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sigma\oint_{a_{k}}\lambda(P)^{\sigma-1}\phi_{t^{A}}(P).

Now, due to (
3.29
) and (
8.8
), the jumps of the multivalued differential

\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P):=\frac{\phi_{t^{A}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)

are as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P+a_{j})-\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P)=2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jk}\nu\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\nu-1}\phi_{t^{A}}(P);

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P+b_{j})-\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P)=-2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P).

Using this and proceeding as in the previous case, we deduce that

\displaystyle e.t^{A}\big(\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\underset{P_{j}}{{\rm res}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P)=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\oint_{F_{g}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{a_{j}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P)-\oint_{a_{j}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P+b_{j})\bigg)+\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\oint_{b_{j}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P)-\oint_{b_{j}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A,k,\nu}(P+a_{j}^{-1})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{g}\bigg(\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\oint_{a_{j}}\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}(P)\bigg)+\nu\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\nu-1}\phi_{t^{A}}(P)

\displaystyle=2{\rm i}\pi\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\nu}+\nu\oint_{b_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\nu-1}\phi_{t^{A}}(P).

\square

Proposition 8.3

Consider the multivalued differentials
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
and
\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}
defined by (
8.7
) and assume that all the cycles
\{a_{k},b_{k}\}
start at a marked point
P_{0}
whose projection by
\lambda
is zero. Then, for any operation
t^{A}
among those defining flat coordinates in (
3.21
)-(
3.22
), the functions
t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)
and
t^{A}\big(\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big)
are eigenfunctions of the vector field
E=\sum_{s=1}^{N}\lambda_{s}\partial_{\lambda_{s}}
with

\displaystyle E.t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)=(d_{A}+\sigma)t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big);

(8.12)

\displaystyle E.t^{A}\big(\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big)=(d_{A}+\nu)t^{A}\big(\phi_{\widetilde{u}^{k,\nu}}\big).

(8.13)

Proof:
Let
(C_{g},\lambda)
be a generic branched covering representing an equivalence class in the simple Hurwitz space

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{g,L}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
. Denote by
F_{g}
the fundamental polygon associated with the compact surface
C_{g}
.

Using the properties of the multivalued differential
\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}
defined by (
8.11
), we deduce that the differential
\lambda(P)\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)

has no jumps along the cycles
a_{j}
and its jumps along the cycle
b_{j}
are as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda(P)\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P+b_{j})-\lambda(P)\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)

\displaystyle=-2{\rm i}\pi\Big(\delta_{jk}\sigma\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\phi_{t^{A}}(P)+\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\lambda(P)\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)-2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jk}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\sigma\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}d\lambda(P)\Big).

Moreover, the restriction of the differential
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
to the fundamental polygon
F_{g}
is holomorphic inside
F_{g}
. This enables us
to apply identity (
3.58
) with
\phi_{0}=\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
. Therefore, using Rauch formulas (
3.15
) and formula (
3.58
) while
bearing in mind the jumps of
\lambda(P)\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}
, we get

\displaystyle E.t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\phi_{t^{A}}(P_{j})\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P_{j})=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\underset{P_{j}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi_{t^{A}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{\partial{F_{g}}}\lambda(P)\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)-\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi_{t^{A}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{v^{i}}}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda(P)\phi_{t^{A}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{g}\oint_{a_{j}}\bigg(\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P)-\Phi_{A,k,\sigma}(P+b_{j})\bigg)+\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{i}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{g}\oint_{a_{j}}\bigg(\delta_{jk}\sigma\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\phi_{t^{A}}(P)+\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\lambda(P)\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)-2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jk}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\sigma\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}d\lambda(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{i}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma\oint_{a_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\phi_{t^{A}}(P)+\sum_{j=1}^{g}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}\oint_{a_{j}}\lambda(P)\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)+\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{i}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma t^{A}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)+\sum_{j=1}^{g}\delta_{\phi_{t^{A}},\phi_{u^{j}}}u^{j}\big(\phi_{\sigma,k}\big)+\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{t^{i,\alpha}}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}t^{i,\alpha}\big(\phi_{\sigma,k}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\phi,\phi_{v^{i}}}v^{i}\big(\phi_{\sigma,k}\big).

In the last equality we have interchanged the order of integration over the cycles
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
when
\phi_{t^{A}}=\phi_{\rho^{k}}=\omega_{k}
. More precisely, we have used the following relation:

\oint_{a_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\omega_{k}(P)=\big(\lambda(P_{0})\big)^{\sigma}+\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b_{j}}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b_{j}}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}},

where the first equality follows from (
8.9
) and the second is an immediate consequence of the fact that the cycles
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
start at a point
P_{0}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=0
.

Formula (
8.13
) can be obtained in a similar way.

\square

The next proposition addresses the quasi-homogeneity property of the functions

q^{j}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big):=\oint_{a_{j}}\log\big(\lambda(P)\big)\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P),\quad j=1,\dots,g,

(8.14)

with
\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}
being the differential defined by (
8.7
). Note that these functions
are among the family of flat coordinates (
3.47
) of the intersection form
\mathbf{g}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)
(
3.46
).

Proposition 8.4

For each
j=1,\dots,g
, the function
q^{j}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)
(
8.14
)
is an eigenfunction of the vector field
E=\sum_{s=1}^{N}\lambda_{s}\partial_{\lambda_{s}}
with

E.q^{j}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)=\sigma q^{j}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big).

(8.15)

Proof:
Consider the multivalued differential

\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P):=\frac{\lambda(P)\psi_{q^{j}}(P)}{d\lambda(P)}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P),\quad\text{with}\quad\psi_{q^{k}}(P):=\oint_{a_{k}}\log(\lambda(Q))W(P,Q).

Due to (
3.51
) and (
8.8
), the jumps of
\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P)
are as follows:

\displaystyle\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P+a_{r})-\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P)=0;

\displaystyle\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P+b_{r})-\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P)

\displaystyle=-2{\rm i}\pi\delta_{jr}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)-2{\rm i}\pi\sigma\delta_{kr}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\psi_{q^{j}}(P)+(2{\rm i}\pi)^{2}\sigma\delta_{jr}\delta_{kr}d\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}.

Thus, observing that the ramification points
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}
are the only simple poles of the meromorphic differential
\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}
within a fixed fundamental polygon
F_{g}
and using its jumps, we get

\displaystyle E.q^{j}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big)=\sum_{s=1}^{N}\underset{P_{s}}{{\rm res}}\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P)=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{\partial{F_{g}}}\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\sum_{r=1}^{g}\oint_{a_{r}}\bigg(\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P)-\Psi_{u^{k,\sigma},q^{j}}(P+b_{r})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\oint_{a_{j}}\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}(P)+\sigma\oint_{a_{k}}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}\psi_{q^{j}}(P)+2{\rm i}\pi\sigma\sum_{r=1}^{g}\delta_{jr}\delta_{kr}\oint_{a_{r}}d\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sigma q^{j}\big(\phi_{u^{k,\sigma}}\big).

\square

8.3
Appendix 3: Dubrovin’s bilinear pairing and prepotentials

Let
\omega^{(1)}
and
\omega^{(2)}
be two differentials on the surface
C_{g}
holomorphic outside the points
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}
with the following properties:

\displaystyle\omega^{(\alpha)}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\sum_{n=-n^{(\alpha)}}^{\infty}c_{n,i}^{(\alpha)}z_{i}(P)^{n}dz_{i}(P)+\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}d\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}r_{n,i}^{(\alpha)}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{n}\log\big(\lambda(P)\big)\right);

\displaystyle\oint_{a_{k}}\omega^{(\alpha)}=A_{k}^{(\alpha)};

\displaystyle\omega^{(\alpha)}(P+a_{k})-\omega^{(\alpha)}(P)=dp_{k}^{(\alpha)}\big(\lambda(P)\big),\quad\quad p_{k}^{(\alpha)}=\sum_{j>0}p_{kj}^{(\alpha)}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{j};

\displaystyle\omega^{(\alpha)}(P+b_{k})-\omega^{(\alpha)}(P)=dq_{k}^{(\alpha)}\big(\lambda(P)\big),\quad\quad q_{k}^{(\alpha)}=\sum_{j>0}q_{kj}^{(\alpha)}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{j},

where
z_{i}(P)
is the local parameter near the pole
\infty^{i}
defined by
\lambda(P)=z_{i}(P)^{-n_{i}-1}
,
n^{(\alpha)}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
c_{n,i}^{(\alpha)}
,
r_{n,i}^{(\alpha)}
,

p_{kj}^{(\alpha)}
and
q_{kj}^{(\alpha)}
are some coefficients.

The Dubrovin bilinear pairing of
\omega^{(1)}
and
\omega^{(2)}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}[\omega^{(1)},\omega^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{-n-2,i}^{(1)}}{n+1}c_{n,i}^{(2)}+c_{-1,i}^{(1)}p.v.\int_{P_{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\omega^{(2)}-p.v.\int_{P_{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}r_{n,i}^{(1)}\big(\lambda(P)\big)^{n}\omega^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{g}\left(-\oint_{a_{k}}q_{k}^{(1)}(\lambda)\omega^{(2)}+\oint_{b_{k}}p_{k}^{(1)}(\lambda)\omega^{(2)}+A_{k}^{(1)}\oint_{b_{k}}\omega^{(2)}\right),

where
P_{0}
is a marked point on
C_{g}
such that
\lambda(P_{0})=0
.

Theorem 8.2

Let
\phi
be one of the primary differential (
3.23
) and
\Psi_{\phi}
be the following multivalued differential

\Psi_{\phi}(P):=\left(p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{P}\phi\right)d\lambda(P).

Then the Dubrovin prepotential associated with the Frobenius manifold structure determined by
\phi
is given by means of the bilinear pairing
\mathfrak{F}[\cdot,\cdot]
as follows:

F_{\phi}=\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{F}[\Psi_{\phi},\Psi_{\phi}].

(8.16)
